\displaystyle+\left[b_{21}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{3}{2}b_{30}-2b_{01}b_{20}-2b_{10}b_{11}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}

(267)

\displaystyle+\left[b_{40}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-2b_{10}b_{30}-b_{20}^{2}\right]z^{4}+\cdots

(268)

Because the leading quantity
b_{00}\left(1-b_{00}\right)
will show up a lot, we give it a special name for convenience:

B_{00}\equiv b_{00}\left(1-b_{00}\right).

(269)

Now we need use the following Taylor series:

\sqrt{1+\epsilon}=1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{8}+\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{16}-\frac{5}{128}\epsilon^{4}+O\left(\epsilon^{5}\right)

(270)

When we use the above Taylor series to take the square root of
\cos^{2}\theta_{-}\sin^{2}\theta_{+}
, we obtain (take a deep breath now…)

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{-}\sin\theta_{+}=\,\,

\displaystyle\sqrt{B_{00}}\Bigg\{1+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]z+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{01}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{b_{10}}{2}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}}

(271)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{20}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-b_{10}^{2}\right]z^{2}+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{11}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+b_{20}-2b_{10}b_{01}\right]\frac{z}{N_{C}}

(272)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{30}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-2b_{10}b_{20}\right]z^{3}

(273)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{02}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{b_{11}}{2}+\frac{b_{20}}{4}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-b_{01}^{2}+\frac{b_{10}^{2}}{4}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(274)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{21}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{3}{2}b_{30}-2b_{01}b_{20}-2b_{10}b_{11}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}

(275)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2B_{00}}\left[b_{40}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-2b_{10}b_{30}-b_{20}^{2}\right]z^{4}-\frac{1}{8B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]^{2}z^{2}

(276)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]\left[b_{01}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{b_{10}}{2}\right]\frac{z}{N_{C}}

(277)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]\left[b_{20}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-b_{10}^{2}\right]z^{3}

(278)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]\left[b_{11}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+b_{20}-2b_{10}b_{01}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}

(279)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]\left[b_{30}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-2b_{10}b_{20}\right]z^{4}

(280)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{01}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{b_{10}}{2}\right]^{2}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(281)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{01}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{b_{10}}{2}\right]\left[b_{20}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-b_{10}^{2}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}

(282)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8B_{00}^{2}}\left[b_{20}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-b_{10}^{2}\right]^{2}z^{4}+\frac{1}{16B_{00}^{3}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]^{3}z^{3}

(283)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{16B_{00}^{3}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]^{2}\left[b_{01}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)+\frac{b_{10}}{2}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}

(284)

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{16B_{00}^{3}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]^{2}\left[b_{20}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)-b_{10}^{2}\right]z^{4}

(285)

\displaystyle-\frac{5}{128B_{00}^{4}}\left[b_{10}\left(1-2b_{00}\right)\right]^{4}z^{4}\Bigg\}+\cdots

(286)

For the sake of readability, we have organized the terms in the above expression in the following way. Within the curly braces, we start with the term
1
in the Taylor series. Next, we put all the terms coming from the
+\epsilon/2
term in the Taylor series. After that, we put all the terms coming from the
-\epsilon^{2}/8
term in the Taylor series. Then, we put all the terms coming from the
+\epsilon^{3}/16
term in the Taylor series. Finally, we put all the terms coming from the
-5\epsilon^{4}/128
term in the Taylor series.

It is now time to plug in the values for
b_{00}
and
b_{10}
, which we derived in Appendix
F
from
0^{\text{th}}
-order optimality and
1^{\text{st}}
-order optimality, respectively:

\boxed{b_{00}=\frac{1-C_{\text{out}}}{2}}\quad\boxed{b_{10}=\lambda\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}}.

(287)

Now we recall the fidelity formula that we derived in Appendix
D
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\left(\frac{1+C_{\text{out}}}{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{w}+\frac{1-C_{\text{out}}}{2}\sin^{2}\theta_{w}\right)P_{w,w}+\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w}P_{w-1,w}

(288)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+C_{\text{out}}}{2}\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}P_{w,w}-C_{\text{out}}\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\sin^{2}\theta_{w}P_{w,w}+S_{\text{out}}\sum_{w\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2},\,\,0\leq w\leq N_{C}}\cos\theta_{-}\sin\theta_{+}P_{-,+}.

(289)

We now plug everything we have derived so far (the asymptotic expansion, the values of
b_{00}
and
b_{10}
, and the
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(\alpha)}
values) into the fidelity formula. After a lot of tedious algebra, we find that the
\Theta(N_{C}^{-2})
contribution to the fidelity is a quadratic polynomial in the variables
b_{01}
and
b_{20}
. (This is the point at which the terms that depend on
b_{11}
,
b_{30}
,
b_{02}
,
b_{21}
,
b_{40}
at
\Theta(N_{C}^{-2})
precision “magically” cancel out.) In particular, we can write

\left[\Theta\left(N_{C}^{-2}\right)\text{ fidelity term}\right]=Ab_{01}^{2}+2Bb_{01}b_{20}+Cb_{20}^{2}+Db_{01}+Eb_{20}+F

(290)

A=-\frac{1}{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}

(291)

B=-\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}

(292)

C=-\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{4}}{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}

(293)

D=-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2\lambda}\frac{C_{\text{out}}}{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}

(294)

E=\frac{-1+5\lambda^{2}}{8\lambda^{2}}C_{\text{out}}-\frac{3-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda}C_{\text{in}}

(295)

\displaystyle F=\,\,

\displaystyle\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{16S_{\text{in}}^{4}\lambda^{2}}\Bigg\{-(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)-8\lambda^{4}C_{\text{out}}^{2}

(296)

\displaystyle+4\lambda\left(-1-3\lambda+\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{3}\right)C_{\text{in}}^{2}+4\lambda\left(1+4\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{4}\right)C_{\text{out}}C_{\text{in}}\Bigg\}.

(297)

One good sanity check is to look at the equatorial special case. In that case, the coefficients of the quadratic polynomial simplify dramatically:

\displaystyle\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}=\frac{\pi}{2}\quad\left(C_{\text{in}}=C_{\text{out}}=0\right)

(298)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle A=-1,\quad B=-\frac{1}{4\lambda},\quad C=-\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}},\quad D=0,\quad E=0,\quad F=-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)}{16\lambda^{2}}.

(299)

Fortunately, a quadratic polynomial is extremely easy to optimize. If a quadratic polynomial has the form

\displaystyle f(x,y)

\displaystyle=Ax^{2}+2Bxy+Cy^{2}+Dx+Ey+F

(300)

with
AC\neq B^{2}
, then it has a unique critical point
(x_{0},y_{0})
that satisfies the following:

\displaystyle(x_{0},y_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{BE-CD}{2(AC-B^{2})},\frac{BD-AE}{2(AC-B^{2})}\right)

(301)

\displaystyle f(x_{0},y_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{2BDE-CD^{2}-AE^{2}}{4(AC-B^{2})}+F=\frac{D}{2}x_{0}+\frac{E}{2}y_{0}+F.

(302)

In particular, if
A,C<0
and
AC-B^{2}>0
, then the Hessian is negative definite, so the critical point is a local maximum, and thus also the global maximum. In this case, it is easy to verify that
A,B,C
indeed satisfy these conditions. As a result, by using the formulas for
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
above, we can find the optimum values for the parameters
b_{01}
and
b_{20}
:

\boxed{b_{01}=\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}\left[\lambda(3-\lambda^{2})C_{\text{in}}-(1+\lambda^{2})C_{\text{out}}\right]}

(303)

\boxed{b_{20}=\lambda\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{4}}{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}\left[2\lambda C_{\text{out}}-(3-\lambda^{2})C_{\text{in}}\right]}.

(304)

Once again, a good sanity check is to examine the equatorial special case:

\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}=\frac{\pi}{2}\quad\left(C_{\text{in}}=C_{\text{out}}=0\right)\implies b_{01}=b_{20}=0.

(305)

As we mentioned in Appendix
E
, bit-flip symmetry implies that
b_{pq}=0
for all even
p
(except
p=q=0
, in which case
b_{00}=\frac{1}{2}
). So the fact that we get
b_{01}=b_{20}=0
in this case is reassuring.

We now compute the
2^{\text{nd}}
-order contribution to the fidelity, which equals
\frac{D}{2}b_{01}+\frac{E}{2}b_{20}+F
. We first compute the following two auxiliary quantities:

\frac{D}{2}b_{01}=-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{C_{\text{out}}S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{4}}\left[\lambda(3-\lambda^{2})C_{\text{in}}-(1+\lambda^{2})C_{\text{out}}\right]

(306)

\frac{E}{2}b_{20}=\frac{1}{16\lambda}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{4}}\left[(-1+5\lambda^{2})C_{\text{out}}-2\lambda(3-\lambda^{2})C_{\text{in}}\right]\left[2\lambda C_{\text{out}}-(3-\lambda^{2})C_{\text{in}}\right]

(307)

Adding the above two quantities and
F
yields

\displaystyle\left[\Theta\left(N_{C}^{-2}\right)\text{ fidelity term}\right]=\frac{1-\lambda}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{4}}\Big\{

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}^{2}\left(1+\lambda-\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}+8\lambda^{4}\right)

(308)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{in}}^{2}\left[2\lambda^{2}\left(7+\lambda-3\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}\right)\right]

(309)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}C_{\text{in}}\left[2\lambda^{2}\left(7+\lambda-3\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}\right)\right]

(310)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle\left[-(1-\lambda)(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)\right]\Big\}.

(311)

Finally, for the sake of completeness, we re-introduce the
0^{\text{th}}
-order and
1^{\text{st}}
-order contributions to write out the full fidelity to
\Theta(N_{C}^{-2})
precision:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\frac{1-\lambda}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{4}}\Big\{

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}^{2}\left(1+\lambda-\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}+8\lambda^{4}\right)

(312)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{in}}^{2}\left[2\lambda^{2}\left(7+\lambda-3\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}\right)\right]

(313)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}C_{\text{in}}\left[2\lambda^{2}\left(7+\lambda-3\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}\right)\right]

(314)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle\left[-(1-\lambda)(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)\right]\Big\}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right).

(315)

Our last task is to convert this power series in
N_{C}^{-1}
to a power series in
N^{-1}
. To do this, we need to compute the expectation values of both
N_{C}^{-1}
and
N_{C}^{-2}
to
\Theta(N^{-2})
precision. We explain how to do this in Appendix
J
. The relevant expectation values are as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)

(316)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right).

(317)

When we plug in these formulas, we can obtain the
2^{\text{nd}}
-order contribution to the fidelity:

\displaystyle\left[\Theta\left(N^{-2}\right)\text{ fidelity term}\right]=\frac{1-\lambda}{16\lambda^{4}}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{4}}\Big\{

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}^{2}\left(1+\lambda-\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}+8\lambda^{4}\right)

(318)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{in}}^{2}\left[2\lambda\left(2+7\lambda-\lambda^{2}-3\lambda^{3}-\lambda^{4}\right)\right]

(319)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}C_{\text{in}}\left[4\lambda^{2}\left(1-5\lambda-\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{3}\right)\right]

(320)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle\left[-3(1-\lambda)(1+\lambda)^{2}\right]\Big\}.

(321)

Finally, for the sake of completeness, we re-introduce the
0^{\text{th}}
-order and
1^{\text{st}}
-order contributions to write out the full fidelity to
\Theta(N^{-2})
precision:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1-\lambda}{16\lambda^{4}}\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{S_{\text{in}}^{4}}\Big\{

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}^{2}\left(1+\lambda-\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{3}+8\lambda^{4}\right)

(322)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{in}}^{2}\left[2\lambda\left(2+7\lambda-\lambda^{2}-3\lambda^{3}-\lambda^{4}\right)\right]

(323)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle C_{\text{out}}C_{\text{in}}\left[4\lambda^{2}\left(1-5\lambda-\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{3}\right)\right]

(324)

\displaystyle+\,\,

\displaystyle\left[-3(1-\lambda)(1+\lambda)^{2}\right]\Big\}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right).

(325)

This completes the
2^{\text{nd}}
-order analysis of single-shot qubit coherence distillation. It is unclear whether the above fidelity expression has any deeper meaning. Regardless, we find that the fact that this problem
can
be solved to higher orders at all interesting in its own right.

G.3
Equatorial Special Case

One special case we always like to check is the equatorial case. We already incorporated some of this discussion into the general derivation in Appendix
G
G.2
, but let us restate the key results. First, the protocol is actually extremely simple to describe, as both of the variables we solved for collapse to zero:

\boxed{b_{01}=b_{20}=0}.

(326)

This can be understood as a direct consequence of bit-flip symmetry, which actually enforces
b_{pq}=0
for all even
p
(except
p=q=0
, in which case
b_{00}=\frac{1}{2}
). One interesting way to state this is as follows:
for the equatorial special case, a
1^{\text{st}}
-order optimal protocol that satisfies bit-flip symmetry will automatically be
2^{\text{nd}}
-order optimal as well.

Another interesting thing to check is whether the lower bound on infidelity imposed by purity of coherence is saturated. In particular, as shown in Supplementary Note 10 of
Marvian 2020
, one can exploit bit-flip symmetry to lower bound the
2^{\text{nd}}
-order infidelity as well. In particular,

\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E}_{N})\geq\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\left(1+\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}\right)^{-1/2}\right]=\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{3\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{2}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{5\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{3}}{32\lambda^{6}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(327)

So is this bound saturated? To find out, we take the optimal fidelity, both as a power series in
N_{C}^{-1}
and as a power series in
N^{-1}
, and we simply plug in
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
:

\displaystyle\Theta_{\text{in}}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{\text{out}}=\frac{\pi}{2}\quad\left(C_{\text{in}}=C_{\text{out}}=0\right)

(328)

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)

(329)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{3(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)

(330)

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E}_{N})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{3(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right).

(331)

As we can see, the bound on the
N^{-2}
term coming from purity of coherence is NOT saturated. (The two terms look the same, but one has a minus sign, while the other has a plus sign!) In fact, in Appendix
L
, we show that this
2^{\text{nd}}
-order optimal protocol indeed wastes some purity of coherence, not at the
\Theta(N)
order (which is the leading order), but at the
\Theta(1)
order. The fact that the bound coming from purity of coherence monotonicity is not saturated at the
2^{\text{nd}}
order is directly tied to this wastage.

Perhaps this is unsurprising; after all, even in classical parameter estimation, the Cramér-Rao bound (which is by far the most famous application of Fisher information) can only be saturated at the leading order in general. (However, it can be saturated at higher orders, or even exactly, in some special cases.) It remains an open question to study whether there is some other natural information-geometric consideration that motivates the
\Theta\left(N^{-2}\right)
term in the minimum infidelity. If there were such a consideration, it would be a distillation analogue of a result in classical parameter estimation which computes the
\Theta\left(N^{-2}\right)
term in the variance of a bias-corrected, first-order efficient estimator and thus derives the condition for second-order efficiency
Amari 1985
.

G.4
Special Case for Pure Input States

In addition to the equatorial special case, there is one other especially interesting case to consider more closely. This is the
\lambda=1
case, meaning that the input qubits are already pure. (Of course, the term “distillation” may no longer be appropriate in this case, since the input qubits are already pure.)

The first thing we can do is to plug
\lambda=1
into the formulas for
b_{01}
and
b_{20}
:

\boxed{b_{01}=\frac{S_{\text{out}}^{2}}{2S_{\text{in}}^{2}}(C_{\text{in}}-C_{\text{out}})}

(332)

\boxed{b_{20}=\frac{2S_{\text{out}}^{4}}{2S_{\text{in}}^{2}}(C_{\text{out}}-C_{\text{in}})}.

(333)

In general, these are probably not that illuminating. However, in the even more restricted case
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}
, these values both collapse to zero! This actually makes perfect sense, because the discarding protocol
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=\frac{w}{N_{C}}
achieves perfect fidelity in this case. Therefore, in such a setting, plugging
z=0
into the asymptotic expansion for the optimal protocol should produce

z=0\implies\sin^{2}\theta=\mu=\frac{1-C_{\text{in}}}{2}+\frac{C_{\text{in}}(1-\lambda)}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\text{e.s.e.}=\frac{1-C_{\text{in}}}{2}+\text{e.s.e.}

(334)

However, plugging
z=0
into the general asymptotic expansion yields

z=0\implies\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=b_{00}+\frac{b_{01}}{N_{C}}+\frac{b_{02}}{N_{C}^{2}}+\cdots

(335)

Furthermore, the protocol should always have slope exactly
1
. Therefore, we should always have
b_{00}=\frac{1-C_{\text{in}}}{2}=\frac{1-C_{\text{out}}}{2}
,
b_{10}=1
, and all other values equal to
0
, and sure enough, the values we have derived so far match that perfectly.

The second thing we can do is to compute the fidelity. When we plug
\lambda=1
into the fidelity formulas derived in Appendix
G
G.2
, then regardless of
\Theta_{\text{in}}
and
\Theta_{\text{out}}
, both the
N_{C}^{-1}
and
N_{C}^{-2}
terms vanish (equivalently, both the
N^{-1}
and
N^{-2}
terms vanish), leaving us with

\lambda=1\implies\mathcal{F}=1+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)=1+O\left(N^{-3}\right).

(336)

Given that we already showed the connection between the
1^{\text{st}}
-order infidelity and purity of coherence, the fact that the
1^{\text{st}}
-order infidelity vanishes is perhaps unsurprising. However, here we see that even the
2^{\text{nd}}
-order infidelity vanishes! If
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}
, then this is obvious, because then the target state is the same as one input copy, but as long as
\Theta_{\text{in}}\neq\Theta_{\text{out}}
, this is not so obvious. In fact, if
\lambda=1
, then both the input and target states have infinite purity of coherence, so the purity of coherence cannot say anything about this case. However, this fact does have a satisfying intuitive explanation: in the
\lambda=1
case, there exist a protocol that achieves zero
p^{\text{th}}
-order infidelity for
every

p\in\mathbb{N}
, since the infidelity will actually be exponentially small. We present this protocol in Appendix
H
.

Appendix H
Perfect Conversion with Post-Selection Between Pure Coherent States at Different Latitudes

One interesting observation that comes out of the second-order analysis in Appendix
G
is that, when the input qubit state is pure (
\lambda=1
), the second-order infidelity is also zero, regardless of
\Theta_{\text{in}}
and
\Theta_{\text{out}}
. This fact is obvious for
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}
, since the discarding protocol achieves perfect fidelity, but it is, in fact, non-obvious for
\Theta_{\text{in}}\neq\Theta_{\text{out}}
. For instance, Appendix
D
D.2
argues that, for
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}
, the discarding protocol is the unique protocol on the symmetric subspace that achieves perfect fidelity, but this argument falls apart for
\Theta_{\text{in}}\neq\Theta_{\text{out}}
. In particular, one can show that it is no longer possible to saturate all of the inequalities that upper bound the fidelity at one, meaning that one cannot achieve perfect conversion from
N
pure coherent qubit states to even
1
pure coherent qubit state at a different latitude.

Furthermore, considerations involving purity of coherence no longer assist us here, since both the input and target pure coherent states have infinite purity of coherence. In fact, to draw an analogy to classical statistics, there are classical parameter estimation problems where the family of probability distributions has infinite Fisher information, yet the minimum-variance unbiased estimator (MVUE) still has
\Theta(N^{-2})
variance. (A particularly simple one is a continuum analogue of the German tank problem, where the underlying distribution is the uniform distribution on
[0,\theta]
for an unknown
\theta>0
.) As a result, one might be led to believe that, even with infinite RLD Fisher information, one might be forced to have
\Theta(N^{-2})
infidelity of distillation.

As a result, it is natural to ask: is there a simple and satisfying reason for why the second-order infidelity should vanish when the input states are pure? In fact, the answer is yes! In this appendix, we will demonstrate a procedure that, with positive probability under post-selection, achieves perfect one-to-one conversion between different latitudes of pure qubit states. This can be understood as providing a positive linear rate of perfect conversion, or alternatively an exponentially small chance of failure in the case of single-shot conversion.

For convenience, we will first assume that
\Theta_{\text{out}}\leq\Theta_{\text{in}}
, i.e., the output coherent states are northward of the input coherent states. (As we will describe later, an analogous strategy will work in the opposite case, where the output coherent states are southward of the input coherent states.) In this case, the conversion strategy looks as follows:

•

Begin with the input state
\alpha\ket{0}+\beta\ket{1}
, where
\alpha=\cos\frac{\Theta_{\text{in}}}{2}
and
\beta=e^{i\phi}\sin\frac{\Theta_{\text{in}}}{2}
, where
\phi\in[0,2\pi)
is arbitrary and unknown to us.

•

Introduce an ancilla in the
\ket{0}
state to obtain the joint state
\alpha\ket{00}+\beta\ket{10}
.

•

Apply the energy-conserving unitary
U(\eta)=\exp\left[-i\eta(\ket{01}\bra{10}-\ket{10}\bra{01})\right]
, which is a rotation on the
2
-dimensional subspace with total Hamming weight
1
. (We will compute the value of
\eta
later.) The resulting state is
\alpha\ket{00}+\beta c\ket{10}+\beta s\ket{01}
, where
c\equiv\cos\eta
and
s\equiv\sin\eta
for convenience.

•

Measure the ancilla qubit in the computational basis.

–

If the measurement yields
\ket{0}
, then the conversion succeeds. The post-measurement state of the original qubit is
\frac{\alpha\ket{0}+\beta c\ket{1}}{\lvert\alpha\rvert^{2}+\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}c^{2}}
. This occurs with probability
\mathbb{P}=\lvert\alpha\rvert^{2}+\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}c^{2}=1-\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}s^{2}
.

–

If the measurement yields
\ket{1}
, then the conversion fails. The post-measurement state of the original qubit is
\ket{0}
. This occurs with probability
1-\mathbb{P}=\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}s^{2}
.

We now compute the value of the angle
\eta
needed to carry out the conversion. Rather than computing
\eta
directly, we compute
c^{2}
and
s^{2}
, with the understanding that
0\leq\eta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
:

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\beta}{\alpha}=e^{i\phi}\tan\frac{\Theta_{\text{in}}}{2},\quad\frac{\beta c}{\alpha}=e^{i\phi}\tan\frac{\Theta_{\text{out}}}{2}

(337)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\quad c^{2}=\frac{\tan^{2}\frac{\Theta_{\text{out}}}{2}}{\tan^{2}\frac{\Theta_{\text{in}}}{2}}=\frac{\left(1-C_{\text{out}}\right)\left(1+C_{\text{in}}\right)}{\left(1+C_{\text{out}}\right)\left(1-C_{\text{in}}\right)}

(338)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\quad s^{2}=\frac{2\left(C_{\text{out}}-C_{\text{in}}\right)}{\left(1+C_{\text{out}}\right)\left(1-C_{\text{in}}\right)}.

(339)

It is a good sanity check to confirm that
\Theta_{\text{out}}\leq\Theta_{\text{in}}
implies that
c^{2}
and
s^{2}
are both nonnegative, and to see that
c^{2}=1
and
s^{2}=0
precisely when
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}
, which renders the ancilla useless, since in that case we can just return the input qubit with no alteration.

We can then also compute the probability of success, which turns out to have a very nice form:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle=1-\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}s^{2}

(340)

\displaystyle=1-\sin^{2}\frac{\Theta_{\text{in}}}{2}\frac{2\left(C_{\text{out}}-C_{\text{in}}\right)}{\left(1+C_{\text{out}}\right)\left(1-C_{\text{in}}\right)}

(341)

\displaystyle=\frac{1+C_{\text{in}}}{1+C_{\text{out}}}.

(342)

Notice that this formula makes sense in the case we are considering, because
\Theta_{\text{out}}\leq\Theta_{\text{in}}
implies that
C_{\text{out}}\geq C_{\text{in}}
, so the success probability
\mathbb{P}
is always in the interval
[0,1]
. It is interesting to observe that, if we measure from the south pole,
\mathbb{P}
is simply the ratio of the input state elevation to the output state elevation. In other words, if we define the Hamiltonian of each qubit to be
H=\ket{0}\bra{0}=\frac{I+Z}{2}
,
\mathbb{P}
is the ratio of the input state energy to the output state energy.

A simple modification of the above strategy works in the opposite case, namely
\Theta_{\text{out}}\geq\Theta_{\text{in}}
. First, we initialize the ancilla qubit to
\ket{1}
instead of
\ket{0}
. Next, the unitary will now be a rotation in the opposite direction, i.e.,
U(\eta)=\exp\left[+i\eta(\ket{01}\bra{10}-\ket{10}\bra{01})\right]
. Finally, the successful measurement outcome on the ancilla is
\ket{1}
instead of
\ket{0}
(in both cases, the successful measurement outcome matches the initial state of the ancilla). Then the trigonometric functions of the rotation angle
\eta
and the success probability assume the new formulas

\displaystyle c^{2}=\frac{\tan^{2}\frac{\Theta_{\text{in}}}{2}}{\tan^{2}\frac{\Theta_{\text{out}}}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(1+C_{\text{out}}\right)\left(1-C_{\text{in}}\right)}{\left(1-C_{\text{out}}\right)\left(1+C_{\text{in}}\right)}

(343)

\displaystyle s^{2}=1-c^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\left(C_{\text{in}}-C_{\text{out}}\right)}{\left(1-C_{\text{out}}\right)\left(1+C_{\text{in}}\right)}

(344)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-C_{\text{in}}}{1-C_{\text{out}}}.

(345)

Once again, we can confirm that these values are all sensible in the setting where
\Theta_{\text{out}}\geq\Theta_{\text{in}}
, including in the special case where
\Theta_{\text{out}}=\Theta_{\text{in}}
and the ancilla becomes useless.

So what does this all mean? Notice that each execution of this conversion strategy consumes only one input coherent state and outputs the desired pure coherent state with a positive constant probability. We have
N
attempts at this strategy, and we only need to succeed once, which occurs with probability
1-(1-\mathbb{P})^{N}
. The average fidelity is at least this amount, since if we succeed, we achieve perfect fidelity, and if we fail, we achieve some incoherent state with nonnegative fidelity that we do not really care about. Therefore, the infidelity of this protocol is
\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E})\leq(1-\mathbb{P})^{N}
, which is exponentially vanishing! Therefore, even though we cannot saturate all the inequalities in Appendix
D
D.2
to achieve perfect fidelity, we can achieve something exponentially close to perfect fidelity. Furthermore, we have found a natural explanation for why the second-order infidelity vanishes for
\lambda=1
, regardless of the values of
\Theta_{\text{in}}
and
\Theta_{\text{out}}
, as we discovered in Appendix
G
. In fact, the exponentially vanishing infidelity of this protocol tells us that we would continue to find zero
p^{\text{th}}
-order infidelity at
\lambda=1
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
and all possible values of
\Theta_{\text{in}}
and
\Theta_{\text{out}}
.

This conversion strategy also has implications for another popular problem in the resource theory of asymmetry, namely that of linear conversion rate, where we try to produce
\approx rN
copies of the desired state for the largest possible value of
r
, which is then called the linear conversion rate. For linear-rate conversion between families of pure states with
asymptotically vanishing error
, the maximum linear conversion rate was recently found by Yamaguchi et al. to be closely related to the quantum geometric tensor, which has interesting connections to both SLD and RLD Fisher information
Yamaguchi et al. 2024
. However, what we have achieved here is actually
“perfect” linear-rate conversion
between families of pure states, or in other words, linear-rate conversion between families of pure states with
zero error
. Optimizing the linear rate of perfect conversion between families of pure states in the resource theory of asymmetry may very well be an interesting question in its own right. For example, is there some asymmetry resource that is monotone for pure state conversions but not for general CPTP maps? In fact, it is not even immediately apparent whether the rate we achieved above is the best possible for converting between pure coherent states at different latitudes.

Appendix I
Third-Order Optimality for Equatorial Distillation

To conclude our asymptotic analysis, we extend the equatorial special case to
3^{\text{rd}}
-order optimality. Solving for
2^{\text{nd}}
-order optimality in the general case, as we did in Appendix
G
, is already quite painful. However, in Appendix
G
G.3
, we observe that bit-flip symmetry actually makes
2^{\text{nd}}
-order optimality somewhat trivial. As a result, we charge forward to
3^{\text{rd}}
-order optimality in the equatorial case.

To derive the conditions for
3^{\text{rd}}
-order optimality, we need to compute the relevant “moments” of the
P_{w-1,w}
values to
\Theta\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)
precision. Refer to Lemma
22
in Appendix
K
K.4
for a derivation of these formulas:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=1+\left(-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{-3+4\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-9+12\lambda-10\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(346)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{-3+2\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-3+4\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(347)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-21+12\lambda+2\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(348)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{15}{64\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(349)

As we show in Appendix
D
D.1
, we do not need to care about the
P_{w,w}
values anymore. Furthermore, the odd centered moments are automatically zero. Hence the above values are truly all we need.

We begin by writing out our protocol, which we characterize using
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
. As we explain in Appendix
E
, bit-flip symmetry allows us to forcibly set
b_{00}=\frac{1}{2}
and
b_{pq}=0
for all
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
with
p
even. Therefore, our protocol looks as follows, where we only show terms that can contribute at the
\Theta(N_{C}^{-3})
order:

\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=\frac{1}{2}+\left(\lambda+\frac{b_{11}}{N_{C}}+\frac{b_{12}}{N_{C}^{2}}\right)z+\left(b_{30}+\frac{b_{31}}{N_{C}}\right)z^{3}+b_{50}z^{5}+O\left(N_{C}^{-7/2}\right).

(350)

Just as before, we use for convenience the notation

\theta_{\pm}\coloneqq\theta_{N_{C}x\pm\frac{1}{2}}.

(351)

We need to compute
\cos\theta_{-}\sin\theta_{+}
, since this is the quantity that affects the fidelity of the protocol. We begin by computing
\sin^{2}\theta_{+}
:

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\theta_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\left(\lambda+\frac{b_{11}}{N_{C}}+\frac{b_{12}}{N_{C}^{2}}\right)\left(z+\frac{1}{2N_{C}}\right)

(352)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(b_{30}+\frac{b_{31}}{N_{C}}\right)\left(z+\frac{1}{2N_{C}}\right)^{3}+b_{50}\left(z+\frac{1}{2N_{C}}\right)^{5}+O\left(N_{C}^{-7/2}\right)

(353)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\lambda z+\frac{\lambda}{2}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+b_{11}\frac{z}{N_{C}}+b_{30}z^{3}+\frac{b_{11}}{2}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{3}{2}b_{30}\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}

(354)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[b_{12}+\frac{3}{4}b_{30}\right]\frac{z}{N_{C}^{2}}+b_{31}\frac{z^{3}}{N_{C}}+b_{50}z^{5}+\left[\frac{b_{12}}{2}+\frac{b_{30}}{8}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}

(355)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{2}b_{31}\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{5}{2}b_{50}\frac{z^{4}}{N_{C}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-7/2}\right).

(356)

We now compute
\cos^{2}\theta_{-}
:

\displaystyle\cos^{2}\theta_{-}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\left(\lambda+\frac{b_{11}}{N_{C}}+\frac{b_{12}}{N_{C}^{2}}\right)\left(z-\frac{1}{2N_{C}}\right)

(357)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(b_{30}+\frac{b_{31}}{N_{C}}\right)\left(z-\frac{1}{2N_{C}}\right)^{3}-b_{50}\left(z-\frac{1}{2N_{C}}\right)^{5}+O\left(N_{C}^{-7/2}\right)

(358)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}-\lambda z+\frac{\lambda}{2}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-b_{11}\frac{z}{N_{C}}-b_{30}z^{3}+\frac{b_{11}}{2}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{3}{2}b_{30}\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}

(359)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[-b_{12}-\frac{3}{4}b_{30}\right]\frac{z}{N_{C}^{2}}-b_{31}\frac{z^{3}}{N_{C}}-b_{50}z^{5}+\left[\frac{b_{12}}{2}+\frac{b_{30}}{8}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}

(360)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3}{2}b_{31}\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{5}{2}b_{50}\frac{z^{4}}{N_{C}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-7/2}\right).

(361)

We now multiply the two previous quantities together:

\displaystyle\cos^{2}\theta_{-}\sin^{2}\theta_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\lambda^{2}z^{2}+\left[\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}+\frac{b_{11}}{2}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(362)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[-2\lambda b_{11}+\frac{3}{2}b_{30}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}-2\lambda b_{30}z^{4}

(363)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\frac{\lambda b_{11}}{2}+\frac{b_{12}}{2}+\frac{b_{30}}{8}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}

(364)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[-b_{11}^{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}+\frac{3}{2}\lambda b_{30}}-2\lambda b_{12}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}-\frac{3}{2}\lambda b_{30}}+\frac{3}{2}b_{31}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}^{2}}

(365)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\frac{5}{2}b_{50}-2\lambda b_{31}-2b_{11}b_{30}\right]\frac{z^{4}}{N_{C}}

(366)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[-b_{30}^{2}-2\lambda b_{50}\right]z^{6}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(367)

For readability, we highlight two of the terms in
red
to show that they cancel out. (It is unclear whether there is any “meaningful” reason for this cancellation.) The more interesting observation at this point is that the terms with an odd power of
z
all cancel out. This is a unique feature of the equatorial special case, and it is a direct consequence of the condition
\theta_{w}+\theta_{N_{C}-w}=\frac{\pi}{2}
.

We now wish to take the square root of the above quantity. To do so, we use the Taylor series

\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon^{3}+O(\varepsilon^{4}).

(368)

When we use this Taylor series to compute
\cos\theta_{-}\sin\theta_{+}
, we obtain (time for another deep breath…)

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{-}\sin\theta_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\Bigg\{\frac{\lambda}{2}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\lambda^{2}z^{2}+\left[{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}}+\frac{b_{11}}{2}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(369)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[-2\lambda b_{11}+\frac{3}{2}b_{30}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}-2\lambda b_{30}z^{4}

(370)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\frac{\lambda b_{11}}{2}}+\frac{b_{12}}{2}+\frac{b_{30}}{8}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+\left[-b_{11}^{2}-2\lambda b_{12}+\frac{3}{2}b_{31}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}^{2}}

(371)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[\frac{5}{2}b_{50}-2\lambda b_{31}-2b_{11}b_{30}\right]\frac{z^{4}}{N_{C}}+\left[-b_{30}^{2}-2\lambda b_{50}\right]z^{6}\Bigg\}

(372)

\displaystyle\quad-\Bigg\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}}-\lambda^{3}\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}}+\lambda^{4}z^{4}+\left[{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\frac{\lambda^{3}}{4}}{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}+\frac{\lambda b_{11}}{2}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}

(373)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}-\frac{\lambda^{4}}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-\lambda^{2}b_{11}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}-2\lambda^{2}b_{11}}+\frac{3}{2}\lambda b_{30}\right]\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}^{2}}

(374)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}-2\lambda^{2}b_{30}}+4\lambda^{3}b_{11}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}-3\lambda^{2}b_{30}}\right]\frac{z^{4}}{N_{C}}+4\lambda^{3}b_{30}z^{6}\Bigg\}

(375)

\displaystyle\quad+2\Bigg\{{\color[rgb]{0,1,0}\frac{\lambda^{3}}{8}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}-\frac{3}{4}\lambda^{4}\frac{z^{2}}{N_{C}^{2}}}+\frac{3}{2}\lambda^{5}\frac{z^{4}}{N_{C}}-\lambda^{6}z^{6}\Bigg\}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(376)

Once again for readability, we highlight some of the terms in
red
,
orange
, and
green
to show that they cancel out. We also highlight some of the terms in
teal
,
blue
, and
violet
to show that they are like terms and hence can be combined (but do not cancel out).

We are now ready to evaluate, and then maximize, the fidelity. When working in the equatorial special case, because the output state is also equatorial, it is common to work with the output purity parameter
\tilde{\lambda}
, rather than the output fidelity
\mathcal{F}
. Of course, these two quantities are related in a very straightforward way:

\mathcal{F}=\frac{1+\tilde{\lambda}}{2}.

(377)

As we showed in Appendix
D
, the formula for the output fidelity simplifies to no longer depend on the
P_{w,w}
values in the equatorial case. In particular, the output purity parameter
\tilde{\lambda}
can be written as follows:

\tilde{\lambda}=2\mathcal{F}-1=2\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}P_{w-1,w}\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w}.

(378)

The above expression for
\cos\theta_{-}\sin\theta_{+}
tells us that
\tilde{\lambda}
will take the form

\tilde{\lambda}=(\cdots)\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}+(\cdots)\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}+(\cdots)\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(1)}+(\cdots)\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right),

(379)

where the coefficients of
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}
for
p=0,2,4,6
are as follows:

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}\text{ coeff}\right]

\displaystyle=1+\lambda\frac{1}{N_{C}}+b_{11}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left[b_{12}+\frac{b_{30}}{4}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}

(380)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}\text{ coeff}\right]

\displaystyle=-2\lambda^{2}+\left[-4\lambda b_{11}+3b_{30}+2\lambda^{3}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}}

(381)

\displaystyle\quad+\left[-2b_{11}-4\lambda b_{12}+3b_{31}-2\lambda^{4}+6\lambda^{2}b_{11}-3\lambda b_{30}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(382)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(1)}\text{ coeff}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[-4\lambda b_{30}-2\lambda^{4}\right]+\Big[5b_{50}-4\lambda b_{31}

(383)

\displaystyle\quad-4b_{11}b_{30}+10\lambda^{2}b_{30}-8\lambda^{3}b_{11}+6\lambda^{5}\Big]\frac{1}{N_{C}}

(384)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}\text{ coeff}\right]

\displaystyle=-2b_{30}^{2}-4\lambda b_{50}-8\lambda^{3}b_{30}-4\lambda^{6}.

(385)

Now comes the part where we plug in the formulas for
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}
, which we stated at the start of this appendix, and which we show how to derive in Appendix
K
K.4
. This is the part where the effect of quadratic variation in the vicinity of a local optimum will kick in, and the terms in
\tilde{\lambda}
that depend on
b_{12}
,
b_{31}
, and
b_{50}
will “magically” cancel out, leaving us with
b_{11}
and
b_{30}
as the variables we need to optimize. When we put everything together, we obtain

\tilde{\lambda}=1+\left(-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left[-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)}{8\lambda^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+(\cdots)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right),

(386)

where we write the coefficient of
N_{C}^{-3}
separately for readability:

\displaystyle\left[N_{C}^{-3}\text{ coeff}\right]

\displaystyle=Ab_{11}^{2}+2Bb_{11}b_{30}+Cb_{30}^{2}+Db_{11}+Eb_{30}+F

(387)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\lambda}

(388)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}

(389)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=-\frac{15}{32\lambda^{3}}

(390)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}

(391)

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\frac{3(1-\lambda)(2+\lambda)}{4\lambda^{2}}

(392)

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(-9-6\lambda-16\lambda^{2}-18\lambda^{3}-7\lambda^{4})}{16\lambda^{3}}.

(393)

In general, a two-variable quadratic polynomial of the form

f(x,y)=Ax^{2}+2Bxy+Cy^{2}+Dx+Ey+F

(394)

with
A^{2}-BC\neq 0
has the unique critical point
(x_{0},y_{0})
and evaluation at that critical point as follows:

\displaystyle\left(x_{0},y_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{BE-CD}{2(AC-B^{2})},\frac{BD-AE}{2(AC-B^{2})}\right)

(395)

\displaystyle f\left(x_{0},y_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2BDE-CD^{2}-AE^{2}}{4(AC-B^{2})}+F.

(396)

Furthermore, since
A,C<0
and
AC-B^{2}>0
, the Hessian is negative definite, so this critical point is indeed a local maximum (and thus also the global maximum). Plugging in the values shown above yields the following solution for our protocol:

\displaystyle b_{11}=\frac{BE-CD}{2(AC-B^{2})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+3\lambda)}{2}

(397)

\displaystyle b_{30}=\frac{BD-AE}{2(AC-B^{2})}

\displaystyle=2\lambda(1-\lambda^{2}).

(398)

The resulting coefficient of
N_{C}^{-3}
in
\tilde{\lambda}
is

\left[N_{C}^{-3}\text{ coeff}\right]=-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1+\lambda)(9+6\lambda+5\lambda^{2})}{16\lambda^{3}}.

(399)

Putting this all together, we obtain
\tilde{\lambda}
and the output infidelity for a
3^{\text{rd}}
-order equatorial optimal protocol:

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}=

\displaystyle\,\,1+\left(-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left[-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)}{8\lambda^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(400)

\displaystyle\,\,+\left[-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1+\lambda)(9+6\lambda+5\lambda^{2})}{16\lambda^{3}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(401)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E})=

\displaystyle\,\,\frac{1-\tilde{\lambda}}{2}

(402)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\,\left(\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)}{16\lambda^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(403)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1+\lambda)(9+6\lambda+5\lambda^{2})}{32\lambda^{3}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(404)

Finally, as always, we use the negative moments of the angular momentum outcome distribution to convert the power series in
N_{C}^{-1}
to a power series in
N^{-1}
. We now need to expand the expectation values of
N_{C}^{-p}
for
p=1,2,3
to the
\Theta\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)
order. As we show in Lemma
17
in Appendix
J
, the relevant moments look as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N}+\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+2\lambda-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(405)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+3\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(406)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(407)

Plugging these values into the infidelity as a power series in
N_{C}^{-3}
finally gives us the infidelity series of a
3^{\text{rd}}
-order optimal protocol (of course, only the
N^{-3}
term is new):

\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E})=\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{3(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}}{16\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}(15-7\lambda^{2})}{32\lambda^{6}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(408)

Just as for
2^{\text{nd}}
-order optimality, it is unclear whether there is any deeper meaning to this value. One observation is that, while the
N^{-2}
term has a coefficient that is just a constant times a power of the purity of coherence
P_{H}(\rho)=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{1-\lambda^{2}}
, the
N^{-3}
term has a coefficient that does not satisfy this.

Appendix J
Distribution of Angular Momentum Measurement Outcomes in Schur Sampling

Throughout this paper, we have made frequent use of the following all-important quantity:

N_{C}\equiv\text{number of qubits remaining after Schur sampling.}

(409)

This quantity is important to define because, as we discuss in Appendix
B
, it always makes sense to start with Schur sampling
Cirac et al. 1999
, and then follow it up with another procedure that is defined by the Kraus operators on the
N_{C}
-qubit symmetric subspace (since the Schur-sampled state
\rho_{j}
with
j=N_{C}/2
has support only on the
N_{C}
-qubit symmetric subspace).

When studied in a “post-Schur-sampling” sense, a distillation protocol will produce contributions to the infidelity in terms of
N_{C}
. (In particular, for the asymptotic protocols we study, the infidelity will be a power series in
N_{C}^{-1}
.) However, we would ideally like to write our infidelity in terms of the original number of qubits
N
. As a result, we need to compute expectations of the form
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-p}\right]
for positive integers
p
. To optimize the leading-order infidelity and demonstrate the operational meaning of purity of coherence, we only need
p=1
, but to find more fine-grained contributions to the infidelity, we need higher values of
p
as well.

Cirac et al. state the mean of
N_{C}
with one extra correction term
Cirac et al. 1999
, although they do not prove it:

\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}\right]=N\left[\lambda+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N}+O\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)\right].

(410)

As a brief note, the “yield” is generally defined as the
fraction
of the original number of qubits that are present in the final state, so
N_{C}
is precisely
N
times the yield. In particular, the expected yield is the quantity in brackets above.

In this appendix, we will prove this formula and many others using a variety of methods. In the past, it has already been shown that, for large
N
, the angular momentum distribution outcome is approximately a Gaussian with mean
\sim\lambda N
and variance
\sim(1-\lambda^{2})N

Keyl and Werner 2001
. In fact, this is already good enough for our first-order results in Appendix
F
. However, we will dive much deeper than that. As just one example, we will prove the above formula for
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
including the
\Theta(N^{-1})
correction term, but we will also show that the correction afterward is much smaller than
O(N^{-2})
.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the most detailed study into the moments of this distribution to date. As result, we believe that this appendix may be of independent interest to people who use Schur sampling as a primitive for various problems.

This appendix is broken down as follows:

•

In Appendix
J
J.1
, we show how to compute the positive integer moments of the total angular momentum operator
J^{2}
, which has eigenvalues
j(j+1)=\frac{N_{C}(N_{C}+2)}{4}
.

•

In Appendix
J
J.2
, we show how to use the positive moments of
N_{C}(N_{C}+2)
to compute the positive moments of
N_{C}
. We suspect that this method of computing the moments of
N_{C}
(by going through the moments of
J^{2}
) will be most pleasing to those well-versed in the representation theory of
SU(2)
. This method also has the advantage of allowing one to compute the moments of
N_{C}
systematically without guessing the answer in advance.

•

In Appendix
J
J.3
, we show how to use the positive moments of
N_{C}
to compute the negative moments of
N_{C}
, i.e., expressions of the form
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{-p}]
for
p\in\mathbb{N}
. These negative moments are the ones used throughout this paper to convert power series in
N_{C}^{-1}
to power series in
N^{-1}
.

•

In Appendix
J
J.4
, we show an original method to compute the positive moments of
N_{C}
up to exponentially small error, which we present as Lemma
18
. The fact that we can achieve exponentially small error in these approximations is not apparent from the method we use in Appendix
J
J.2
, and as far as we know, it is not mentioned in any prior literature that invokes the Schur transform. The only disadvantage of this method is that we had to guess the answers based on numerics before we could prove them.

•

In Appendix
J
J.5
, we combine the improved results for the positive moments of
N_{C}
from Appendix
J
J.4
with the method shown in Appendix
J
J.3
to finally prove the negative moments of
N_{C}
, which we present as Lemma
17
.

J.1
Computing Positive Integer Moments of
J^{2}
Total Angular Momentum Operator

When computing the moments of
N_{C}
, a useful trick is to take advantage of the total angular momentum operator

J^{2}=J_{x}^{2}+J_{y}^{2}+J_{z}^{2},

(411)

where the term
J_{x}^{2}
can be expanded as

J_{x}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N}X_{i}\implies 4J_{x}^{2}=N\mathbb{I}+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}X_{i}X_{j},

(412)

and similarly for
J_{y}^{2}
and
J_{z}^{2}
. The
J^{2}
operator is known to have eigenvalues
j(j+1)
, and
N_{C}=2j
, so

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}(N_{C}+2)\right)^{p}\right]=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J^{2}\right)^{p}\right].

(413)

Therefore, we can more easily compute the expected value of specific polynomials of
N_{C}
, in particular those that can be written as polynomials of
N_{C}(N_{C}+2)
. The first
p
equations tell us about moments of
N_{C}
up to
2p
, but we would need
2p
equations to solve for all of them, so these equations only give us “half” of what we want. For example, we wish to have
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}\right]
and
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}\right]
separately, but the above equation with
p=1
only gives us a specific combination of these two quantities. However, it is certainly a step in the right direction, and if we ever compute one of these expressions using some other method, we will get the other for free. Similarly, when we move up to
p=2
, we obtain an expression for
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{4}+4N_{C}^{3}+4N_{C}^{2}\right]
. Therefore, if we compute any two of the three quantities
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}\right]
,
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{3}\right]
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{4}\right]
, we can quickly obtain the third.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\rho
has its Bloch vector pointing in the positive
x
-direction, which implies that
\rho^{\otimes N}
takes the following form as a linear combination of Pauli strings:

\rho=\frac{\mathbb{I}+\lambda X}{2}\implies\rho^{\otimes N}=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\sum_{S\subseteq[N]}\lambda^{\lvert S\rvert}X^{\otimes S}.

(414)

From here, we can easily compute
\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J_{x}^{2}\right)\right]
,
\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J_{y}^{2}\right)\right]
, and
\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J_{z}^{2}\right)\right]
. Recall that the trace of an
N
-qubit Pauli string is zero unless it is the identity, in which case it is
2^{N}
. Therefore, to compute
\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J_{x}^{2}\right)\right]
, for example, we simply find all instances where a Pauli string in
\rho^{\otimes N}
matches a Pauli string in
4J_{x}^{2}
, multiply their coefficients, add all these numbers, and multiply by
2^{N}
. When we do this for
4J_{x}^{2}
,
4J_{y}^{2}
, and
4J_{z}^{2}
, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J_{x}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\cdot\left[N+2\binom{N}{2}\lambda^{2}\right]\cdot 2^{N}=N+N(N-1)\lambda^{2}

(415)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J_{y}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\cdot N\cdot 2^{N}=N

(416)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J_{z}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N}}\cdot N\cdot 2^{N}=N.

(417)

We now add these three expressions to obtain

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J^{2}\right)\right]=N(N-1)\lambda^{2}+3N

(418)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}+2N_{C}\right]=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+\left(3-\lambda^{2}\right)N},

(419)

where in the second line, we have rewritten the expression in standard polynomial form with respect to
N
(instead of with respect to
\lambda
). We have thus obtained
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}(N_{C}+2)\right]
, as desired.

We now proceed to compute
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}+2)^{2}\right]
. We begin by expanding
\left(4J^{2}\right)^{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle\left(4J^{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=16\sum_{\text{sym}}J_{x}^{4}+16\sum_{\text{sym}}J_{x}^{2}J_{y}^{2}

(420)

\displaystyle=16\left(J_{x}^{4}+J_{y}^{4}+J_{z}^{4}+J_{x}^{2}J_{y}^{2}+J_{y}^{2}J_{x}^{2}+J_{x}^{2}J_{z}^{2}+J_{z}^{2}J_{x}^{2}+J_{y}^{2}J_{z}^{2}+J_{z}^{2}J_{y}^{2}\right).

(421)

We now expand each of these quantities in turn. For example,

\displaystyle 16J_{x}^{4}

\displaystyle=\left[N+\binom{4}{2}\binom{N}{2}\right]\mathbb{I}+24\sum_{i<j<k<l}X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}X_{l}+\left[(N-2)\binom{4}{2}\cdot 2+2\cdot 4\right]\sum_{i<j}X_{i}X_{j}

(422)

\displaystyle=(3N^{2}-2N)\mathbb{I}+24\sum_{i<j<k<l}X_{i}X_{j}X_{k}X_{l}+(12N-16)\sum_{i<j}X_{i}X_{j},

(423)

and similarly for
16J_{y}^{4}
and
16J_{z}^{4}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle 16J_{x}^{2}J_{y}^{2}=N^{2}\mathbb{I}+2N\sum_{i<j}X_{i}X_{j}+2N\sum_{i<j}Y_{i}Y_{j}-4\sum_{i<j}Z_{i}Z_{j}+4i\sum_{i,j,k\text{ distinct}}X_{i}Y_{j}Z_{k},

(424)

and similarly for the other five products of two distinct operators chosen from
4J_{x}^{2}
,
4J_{y}^{2}
, and
4J_{z}^{2}
. As a result, we can derive the following expectation values:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{x}^{4}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=(3N^{2}-2N)1+24\binom{N}{4}\lambda^{4}+(12N-16)\binom{N}{2}\lambda^{2}

(425)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{4}N^{4}+6\lambda^{2}\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)N^{3}+\left(3-11\lambda^{2}\right)\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)N^{2}+(-2)\left(1-3\lambda^{2}\right)\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)N

(426)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{x}^{2}J_{y}^{2}\right)\right]=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{x}^{2}J_{z}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=N^{2}\cdot 1+2N\binom{N}{2}\lambda^{2}=\lambda^{2}N^{3}+\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)N^{2}

(427)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{y}^{2}J_{x}^{2}\right)\right]=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{z}^{2}J_{x}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=N^{2}\cdot 1+2N\binom{N}{2}\lambda^{2}=\lambda^{2}N^{3}+\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)N^{2}

(428)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{y}^{4}\right)\right]=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{z}^{4}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=3N^{2}-2N

(429)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{y}^{2}J_{z}^{2}\right)\right]=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(16J_{z}^{2}J_{y}^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=(1-2\lambda^{2})N^{2}+2\lambda^{2}N.

(430)

Adding these all together yields

\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J^{2}\right)^{2}\right]=\lambda^{4}N^{4}+2\lambda^{2}\left(5-3\lambda^{2}\right)N^{3}+\left(15-22\lambda^{2}+11\lambda^{4}\right)N^{2}-6(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)^{2}N,

(431)

from which we conclude that

\boxed{\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{4}+4N_{C}^{3}+4N_{C}^{2}\right]=\lambda^{4}N^{4}+2\lambda^{2}\left(5-3\lambda^{2}\right)N^{3}+\left(15-18\lambda^{2}+11\lambda^{4}\right)N^{2}+2\left(-3+4\lambda^{2}-3\lambda^{4}\right)N}.

(432)

We do not need any higher moments of
N_{C}(N_{C}+2)
for the computations we do in this paper, so we stop here.

J.2
Going from Moments of
J^{2}
to Moments of
N_{C}

So far, we have only shown how to compute the moments of
N_{C}(N_{C}+2)
. But how do we compute the moments of
N_{C}
itself? As we observed in the previous subsection, considering the first
p
moments of
N_{C}(N_{C}+2)
yields
p
equations for the first
2p
moments of
N_{C}
, but we would need
2p
equations to tease apart all these moments.

Here is an intriguing method that skirts the need for more equations. Since
N_{C}
is a function of
N_{C}(N_{C}+2)
via the identity

N_{C}=\sqrt{N_{C}(N_{C}+2)+1}-1,

(433)

we can consider the following question: given the moments of a random variable
X
, how do we compute the moments of
\sqrt{X}
? It turns out that there is a simple trick we can use. First, we use the following Taylor series expansion:

\displaystyle\sqrt{X}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu}\sqrt{1+\frac{X-\mu}{\mu}}

(434)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu}\left[1+\frac{1}{2}\frac{X-\mu}{\mu}+\left(-\frac{1}{8}\right)\frac{(X-\mu)^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{1}{16}\frac{(X-\mu)^{3}}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right]

(435)

\displaystyle\therefore\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu}\left[1+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathbb{E}[X-\mu]}{\mu}+\left(-\frac{1}{8}\right)\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{2}\right]}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{1}{16}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{3}\right]}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right]

(436)

In the above formula,
\mu
can in fact be anything. But a particularly natural choice is

\mu\coloneq\mathbb{E}[X]\implies\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{X}\right]=\sqrt{\mu}\left[1+\left(-\frac{1}{8}\right)\frac{\text{Var}(X)}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{1}{16}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{3}\right]}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right].

(437)

In this way, we have successfully written a moment of
\sqrt{X}
in terms of the mean of
X
and the positive centered moments of
X
. Of course, this series may converge very slowly or not at all. However, if you have a distribution that is fairly concentrated around a positive value far from zero and which is also bounded away from zero, you can have a reasonable convergence rate on this series. In particular, for large
N
, the distribution of
N_{C}
converges to a Gaussian with mean and variance both
\Theta(N)
, and such a distribution will broadly exhibit reasonable convergence.

We begin by defining
X
and
\mu
as follows:

\displaystyle X\coloneq\left(N_{C}+1\right)^{2}=N_{C}\left(N_{C}+2\right)+1

(438)

\displaystyle\mu\coloneq\mathbb{E}[X]=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J^{2}\right)\right]+1.

(439)

We now compute the mean and variance of
X
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J^{2}\right)\right]+1

(440)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+\left(3-\lambda^{2}\right)N+1

(441)

\displaystyle\text{Var}\left[X\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Var}\left[N_{C}\left(N_{C}+2\right)\right]

(442)

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J^{2}\right)^{2}\right]-\text{Tr}\left[\rho^{\otimes N}\left(4J^{2}\right)\right]^{2}

(443)

\displaystyle=\left[\lambda^{4}N^{4}+2\lambda^{2}\left(5-3\lambda^{2}\right)N^{3}+\left(15-18\lambda^{2}+11\lambda^{4}\right)N^{2}+2\left(-3+4\lambda^{2}-3\lambda^{4}\right)N\right]

(444)

\displaystyle\quad-\left[\lambda^{2}N^{2}+\left(3-\lambda^{2}\right)N\right]^{2}

(445)

\displaystyle=4\lambda^{2}\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)N^{3}+2\left(3-6\lambda^{2}+5\lambda^{4}\right)N^{2}+2\left(-3+4\lambda^{2}-3\lambda^{4}\right)N.

(446)

We can then compute the quantity
\frac{\text{Var}(X)}{\mu^{2}}
that appears in the second term in the infinite series:

\frac{\text{Var}[X]}{\mu^{2}}=\frac{4\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)}{\lambda^{2}}N^{-1}+O\left(N^{-2}\right).

(447)

We also compute
\sqrt{\mu}
using the approximation
\sqrt{1+\epsilon}=1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}+O\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)
:

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+\left(3-\lambda^{2}\right)N+1

(448)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}N^{2}\left[1+\frac{3-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right]

(449)

\displaystyle\implies\sqrt{\mu}

\displaystyle=\lambda N\left[1+\frac{3-\lambda^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}}N^{-1}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right].

(450)

We can now put everything together as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu}\left[1-\frac{\text{Var}[X]}{8\mu^{2}}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]

(451)

\displaystyle=\lambda N\left[1+\frac{3-\lambda^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}}N^{-1}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]\left[1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2\lambda^{2}}N^{-1}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]

(452)

\displaystyle=\lambda N\left[1+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}N^{-1}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]

(453)

\displaystyle=\lambda N+\frac{1}{\lambda}+O\left(N^{-1}\right).

(454)

Finally, since
\sqrt{X}=N_{C}-1
, all that remains is to subtract
1
from
\mathbb{E}[X]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}+1\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda N+\frac{1}{\lambda}+O\left(N^{-1}\right)

(455)

\displaystyle\implies\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+O\left(N^{-1}\right).

(456)

We have thus derived the
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
term to
\Theta(1)
precision and demonstrated the yield formula that is shown without proof in
Cirac et al. 1999
. As a reminder, they define the yield as the
fraction
of qubits remaining after the purification procedure, so they show the above expression divided by
N
.

This method has two key advantages. The first is that it computes the moments of
N_{C}
using the moments of
N_{C}(N_{C}+2)
, without the need for the “extra equations” that we previously thought we might need. Another advantage is that it shows how one can systematically compute the moments of
N_{C}
without guessing the answers in advance (via numerics or some other method). We also find this method to be pleasing for two other reasons. First, this method invokes the
J^{2}
operator, which is a familiar object of interest to those well-versed in representation theory and quantum information theory. Second, this method bounds the error terms using the asymptotic normality of the distribution of
N_{C}
, which is another broadly important concept. In fact, the asymptotic normality of
N_{C}
is a special case of a more general asymptotic normality result for the distribution of invariant subspaces of
\rho^{\otimes N}
for a qudit state
\rho
, which are indexed by Young diagrams with at most
d
rows
Keyl and Werner 2001
.

Unfortunately, this method also has two disadvantages. First, computing the moments of
J^{2}
beyond the first two that we showed above becomes increasingly tedious. Second, it leaves open the possibility for inverse polynomial error terms in terms of
N
. However, it turns out that each positive integer moment of
N_{C}
equals a polynomial in
N
plus an
exponentially small error
. We would never be able to see that using the above method. In fact, even revealing that the
\Theta\left(N^{-1}\right)
term in
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
is zero would require us to compute some non-obvious information about the third moment of
J^{2}
. To demonstrate that the positive integer moments of
N_{C}
exhibit exponentially small errors beyond polynomials in
N
, we must use a different technique, which we show later in Appendix
J
J.4
.

J.3
Going from Positive Moments of
N_{C}
to Negative Moments of
N_{C}

So far, we have only shown positive moments of
N_{C}
. But how do we compute the negative moments of
N_{C}
? It turns out that we can recycle the infinite series trick we used in Appendix
J
J.2
. First, we use the following geometric series expansion:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{X}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu}\frac{1}{1+\frac{X-\mu}{\mu}}

(457)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu}\left[1-\frac{X-\mu}{\mu}+\frac{(X-\mu)^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{(X-\mu)^{3}}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right]

(458)

\displaystyle\therefore\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{X}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mu}\left[1-\frac{\mathbb{E}[X-\mu]}{\mu}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{2}\right]}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{3}\right]}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right]

(459)

Once again, in the equation above,
\mu
can be anything. But we again make a natural choice as follows:

\mu\coloneq\mathbb{E}[X]\implies\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{X}\right]=\frac{1}{\mu}\left[1+\frac{\text{Var}(X)}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{3}\right]}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right].

(460)

In this way, we have successfully written the mean of
X^{-1}
in terms of the mean of
X
and the positive centered moments of
X
.

To compute higher negative moments of
X
, we use the fact that, for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
,

(1-x)^{-p}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\binom{n+p-1}{p-1}x^{n},

(461)

which can be obtained by differentiating the geometric series
(p-1)
times (among many other ways). Therefore, using the same sequence of manipulations as we did to compute
\mathbb{E}[X^{-1}]
, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{X^{p}}\right]=\frac{1}{\mu^{p}}\left[1-p\frac{\mathbb{E}[X-\mu]}{\mu}+\frac{p(p+1)}{2}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{2}\right]}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{p(p+1)(p+2)}{6}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{3}\right]}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right],

(462)

which can again be simplified using a smart choice of
\mu
:

\mu\coloneq\mathbb{E}[X]\implies\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{X^{p}}\right]=\frac{1}{\mu^{p}}\left[1+\frac{p(p+1)}{2}\frac{\text{Var}(X)}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{p(p+1)(p+2)}{6}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu)^{3}\right]}{\mu^{3}}+\cdots\right].

(463)

Similarly to the infinite series we showed in Subsection
J
J.2
, these series may converge very slowly or not at all for a general distribution. However, the asymptotic normality of
N_{C}
with mean and variance both
\Theta(N)
will come to the rescue again and provide the same type of term-by-term decay that we saw previously. In particular, the
p^{\text{th}}
centered moment of
N_{C}
is
\Theta\left(N_{C}^{\lfloor p/2\rfloor}\right)
. As a result, in the above infinite series, the second term in the square brackets will be
O(1/N)
, the third and fourth terms will be
O(1/N^{2})
, the fifth and sixth terms will be
O(1/N^{3})
, and so on.

Using this method, we can finally compute the formulas for
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{-p}]
that we invoke in many other parts of this paper. In particular, using Appendices
J
J.1
and
J
J.2
, we can systematically compute the positive moments of
N_{C}
, and thus also the positive centered moments of
N_{C}
. Once we have all these moments, we can plug them into the formulas above to compute
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-p}\right]
to whatever precision we like. Up to
3^{\text{rd}}
-order precision, these negative moments look as follows:

Lemma 17

(Negative moments of
N_{C}
up to
\Theta(N^{-3})
precision)
.

The first three negative moments of the distribution of
N_{C}
look as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N}+\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+2\lambda-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(464)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+3\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(465)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(466)

However, we will hold off on proving Lemma
17
for now, because although we
can
prove it using only the information we have provided so far, it would be extremely tedious to do so. For example, computing
\mu^{-1}
to three terms (to obtain
\Theta(N^{-3})
precision) requires computing
\mu
to
3
terms as well (which corresponds to
\Theta(N^{-1})
precision). The more precision we want on the negative moments, the more precision we must demand on the positive moments that get used in the above infinite series, but at the moment, those positive moments are themselves infinite series based on the method shown in Appendix
J
J.2
.

To prove Lemma
17
more efficiently, we ideally want an improved way to compute the positive moments of
N_{C}
, and this is precisely what we will show in Appendix
J
J.4
. Subsequently, in Appendix
J
J.5
, we will plug these improved results (shown in Lemma
18
) into the above infinite series to finally prove Lemma
17
.

J.4
Computing Positive Moments of
N_{C}
with Exponentially Small Error

The method we used in Appendix
J
J.2
to compute
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{p}]
may lead you to believe that
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{p}]
has an infinite power series expansion, in the sense that it has
N_{C}^{q}
terms for all
-\infty<q\leq p
. However, for positive integers
p
, this is actually not the case! It turns out that, for positive integers
p
,
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{p}]
is a degree-
p
polynomial
P^{(p)}_{\lambda}(N)
in
N
, up to an
exponentially small error
(which we will commonly abbreviate to “e.s.e.” for convenience). In particular, we prove the following result for the first few values of
p
:

Lemma 18

(Positive moments of
N_{C}
with exponentially small error)
.

The first four positive moments of the distribution of
N_{C}
can each be written as a polynomial in
N
plus an exponentially small error. In particular:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+O\left(\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\right)

(467)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}+O\left(\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\right)

(468)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{3}\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{3}N^{3}+3\lambda(1-\lambda)(2+\lambda)N^{2}+\frac{(1-\lambda)\left(3-6\lambda-5\lambda^{2}-2\lambda^{3}\right)}{\lambda}N

(469)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{4(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}+O\left(\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\right)

(470)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{4}N^{4}+2\lambda^{2}(1-\lambda)(5+3\lambda)N^{3}+(1-\lambda)\left(15-9\lambda-23\lambda^{2}-11\lambda^{3}\right)N^{2}

(471)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2(1-\lambda)(2+\lambda)(-3+3\lambda+\lambda^{2}+3\lambda^{3})}{\lambda}N-\frac{8(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}+O\left(\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\right).

(472)

Before we prove these formulas, we will first observe some general patterns they exhibit, which in fact apply to
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]
for all nonnegative integers
p
. We will additionally point out when these patterns extend even to negative integers:

•

\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]
is a degree-
p
polynomial in
N_{C}
plus
O\left(\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\right)
. In fact, as we will see later, the error magnitude can be bounded above by
2^{p-1}\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}\right)\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}
, and the error term is positive for even values of
p
and negative for odd values of
p
.
(nonnegative
p
only)

•

The leading term in
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]
is
\lambda^{p}N^{p}
. This can be understood as a consequence of the fact that the typical values of
N_{C}
are
\sim\lambda N
.
(negative
p
also)

•

All non-leading terms have a
(1-\lambda)
factor. This is because, when
\lambda=1
,
N_{C}=N
with probability
1
, so
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]
collapses to
N^{p}
.
(negative
p
also)

•

For all
q\geq\frac{p}{2}
, the coefficient of
N^{q}
in
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]
has
\lambda^{p-2q}
in the denominator. This can be understood as a consequence of the fact that the quantity
\lambda^{2}N
serves as a good measure of whether one is in the asymptotic regime for Schur sampling.
(nonnegative
p
only)

•

For all
q<\frac{p}{2}
, the coefficient of
N^{q}
in
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]
has
\lambda
in the denominator. This can be at least partially explained by the fact that, when
\lambda
is
exactly
zero,
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]=\Theta\left(N^{p/2}\right)
, regardless of whether
p
is odd or even. So there is no harm in having these terms diverge as
\lambda\rightarrow 0
. In fact, when
p
is odd, the
N^{(p-1)/2}
term becomes leading as
\lambda\rightarrow 0
, since by the previous item, all the higher powers of
N
have coefficients going to
0
. Hence, the coefficient of
N^{(p-1)/2}
must diverge, or else
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{p}\right]
would be
O\left(N^{(p-1)/2}\right)
, contradicting the above fact.
(nonnegative
p
only)

The central idea is to write everything in terms of degree-
N
homogeneous “polynomials” in
c_{0}=\frac{1-\lambda}{2}
and
c_{1}=\frac{1+\lambda}{2}
. We put “polynomials” in quotation marks because we will also need to permit negative powers of
c_{1}
. In other words, we will write everything in terms of quantities of the form
c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}=c_{1}^{N}(c_{0}/c_{1})^{k}
for nonnegative integers
k
. Since
c_{0}+c_{1}=1
, if there is ever an expression of degree less than
N
, we will multiply it by the appropriate power of
(c_{0}+c_{1})
to make it have degree
N
. For the sake of brevity, we will only present the details for
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
and
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]
, but the same general method works for
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{3}]
and
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{4}]
as well.

For convenience, we will use the following two notations:

\displaystyle(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{N})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}a_{k}c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(473)

\displaystyle(a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},\cdots,a_{N}\,|\,a_{N+1},a_{N+2},\cdots)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}.

(474)

The first notation does not permit negative powers of
c_{1}
, meaning that is a proper degree-
N
homogeneous polynomial of
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
. In contrast, the second notation does permit negative powers of
c_{1}
, meaning that it is an infinite power series. For convenience, we will often call these “
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomials” and “
(c_{1},c_{0})
extended polynomials”, respectively.

This notation is motivated by the fact that the probabilities of different
N_{C}
values are given by such polynomials. In particular, the probability of getting
N_{C}=2j
qubits when performing the Schur transform on
N=2J
identical uncorrelated qubits with purity parameter
\lambda
is as follows
Cirac et al. 1999
:

\displaystyle p(N,N_{C},\lambda)

\displaystyle=d_{j}(c_{1}c_{0})^{J-j}\frac{c_{1}^{2j+1}-c_{0}^{2j+1}}{c_{1}-c_{0}}=d_{j}\sum_{k=0}^{2j}c_{1}^{J+j-k}c_{0}^{J-j+k}

(475)

\displaystyle d_{j}

\displaystyle=\binom{2J}{J-j}-\binom{2J}{J-j-1}.

(476)

These values form a very nice pattern for computing moments of
N_{C}
. For example, consider
N=5
. The possible values of
N_{C}
are
N_{C}=1,3,5
, and they have the following probabilities:

\displaystyle p(5,1,\lambda)=5(c_{1}c_{0})^{2}(c_{1}+c_{0})

\displaystyle=(0,0,5,5,0,0)

(477)

\displaystyle p(5,3,\lambda)=4(c_{1}c_{0})^{1}(c_{1}^{3}+c_{1}^{2}c_{0}+c_{1}c_{0}^{2}+c_{0}^{3})

\displaystyle=(0,4,4,4,4,0)

(478)

\displaystyle p(5,5,\lambda)=1(c_{1}c_{0})^{0}(c_{1}^{5}+c_{1}^{4}c_{0}+c_{1}^{3}c_{0}^{2}+c_{1}^{2}c_{0}^{3}+c_{1}c_{0}^{4}+c_{0}^{5})

\displaystyle=(1,1,1,1,1,1).

(479)

We can now compute
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
as a
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial by multiplying each
p(N,N_{C},\lambda)
by
N_{C}
and adding them up:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]

\displaystyle=1\cdot p(5,1,\lambda)+3\cdot p(5,3,\lambda)+5\cdot p(5,5,\lambda)

(480)

\displaystyle=1\cdot(0,0,5,5,0,0)+3\cdot(0,4,4,4,4,0)+5\cdot(1,1,1,1,1,1)

(481)

\displaystyle=(5,17,22,22,17,5).

(482)

In general, it is easy to see that
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{p}]
will always be symmetric
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial
(a_{0},\cdots,a_{N})
, in the sense that
a_{k}=a_{N-k}
. In fact, we can easily write down the
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{p}]

\displaystyle=\left(a^{(p)}_{0},\cdots,a^{(p)}_{N}\right)

(483)

\displaystyle a^{(p)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=J-k}^{J}d_{j}(2j)^{p}=\sum_{j=J-k}^{J}(2j)^{p}\left[\binom{2J}{J-j}-\binom{2J}{J-j-1}\right]\quad\left(k\leq\lceil J\rceil\right)

(484)

\displaystyle a^{(p)}_{k}

\displaystyle=a^{(p)}_{N-k}\quad\left(\lceil J\rceil+1\leq k\leq N\right).

(485)

Notice that we write
a^{(p)}_{k}
as a convenient formula only for
0\leq k\leq\lceil J\rceil
, which accounts for just over half of the
N+1
terms in the
(c_{0},c_{1})
. (For the rest of the values, we use the symmetry that we previously observed.) This fact will become important later.

On the other side, we also want to write our formulas for these expectation values (ignoring the exponentially small error) as
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomials. However, there are two wrinkles. The first is that we will generally deal with expressions of degree less than
N
, meaning that we will need to multiply them by suitable powers of
(c_{1}+c_{0})
to raise their degree to
N
. The second is that we will have expressions with
\lambda
in the denominator, which can only be expressed with an extended
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial. For example, the quantity
c_{1}^{N+1}/\lambda
can be written as an infinite geometric series

\frac{c_{1}^{N+1}}{\lambda}=\frac{c_{1}^{N}}{1-(c_{0}/c_{1})}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}c_{1}^{N}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\right)^{k}=\left(1,1,1,\cdots,1\,|\,1,1,1,\cdots\right).

(486)

Once again, let us take
N=5
as an example. We have the polynomial ansatz

P^{(1)}_{\lambda}(N)=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda},

(487)

which is what is claimed in Lemma
18
to match
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
up to an exponentially small error. Then we can write

\displaystyle\lambda N=5(c_{1}-c_{0})

\displaystyle=5(c_{1}-c_{0})(c_{1}+c_{0})^{4}

(488)

\displaystyle=5(c_{1}-c_{0})(c_{1}^{4}+4c_{1}^{3}c_{0}+6c_{1}^{2}c_{0}^{2}+4c_{1}c_{0}^{3}+c_{0}^{4})

(489)

\displaystyle=5(c_{1}^{5}+3c_{1}^{4}c_{0}+2c_{1}^{3}c_{0}^{2}-2c_{1}^{2}c_{0}^{3}-3c_{1}c_{0}^{4}-c_{0}^{5})

(490)

\displaystyle=(5,15,10,-10,-15,-5)

(491)

\displaystyle\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}=\frac{2c_{0}}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{2c_{0}}{c_{1}-c_{0}}(c_{1}+c_{0})^{5}

(492)

\displaystyle=2(c_{1}^{5}+5c_{1}^{4}c_{0}+10c_{1}^{3}c_{0}^{2}+10c_{1}^{2}c_{0}^{3}+5c_{1}c_{0}^{4}+c_{0}^{5})\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\right)^{k}

(493)

\displaystyle=2(0,1,6,16,26,31\,|\,32,32,32,\cdots)

(494)

\displaystyle=(0,2,12,32,52,62\,|\,64,64,64,\cdots)

(495)

\displaystyle\therefore\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(5,15,10,-10,-15,-5)+(0,2,12,32,52,62\,|\,64,64,64,\cdots)

(496)

\displaystyle=(5,17,22,22,37,57\,|\,64,64,64,\cdots).

(497)

In this way, we have expressed
\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}
for
N=5
as a
(c_{1},c_{0})
extended polynomial.

Now let us compare our two
(c_{1},c_{0})
(extended) polynomials:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]

\displaystyle=(5,17,22,22,17,5)

(498)

\displaystyle\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(5,17,22,22,37,57\,|\,64,64,64,\cdots).

(499)

Notice that the first
4
terms (that is, from
k=0
to
k=3
) match. Furthermore, these are precisely the most dominant terms, since each successive term has an extra factor of
c_{0}/c_{1}
. Therefore, these two quantities are very nearly equal!

This is precisely the more general strategy we will adopt. We have already written
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{p}]
as a
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial (see the formula for
a^{(p)}_{k}
above). We will now write each polynomial ansatz
P^{(p)}_{\lambda}(N)
as a
(c_{1},c_{0})
extended polynomial. We will then show that the terms from
k=0
to
k=\lceil J\rceil
all match. (Recall that our above summation formula for
a^{(p)}_{k}
only applies to
0\leq k\leq\lceil J\rceil
, with the rest being defined by the symmetry between
k
and
N-k
. This is where that fact will come into play.) Finally, we will use the discrepancy from
k=\lceil J\rceil+1
onward to show that the error must be exponentially small.

Let us first tackle the first moment, namely
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
. Let
b^{(1)}_{k}
denote the coefficient of
c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}
in the polynomial ansatz

P^{(1)}_{\lambda}(N)=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}.

(500)

Generalizing the
N=5
example above, we have the following:
