\displaystyle\lambda N

\displaystyle=N(c_{1}-c_{0})(c_{1}+c_{0})^{N-1}

(501)

\displaystyle=N(c_{1}-c_{0})\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\binom{N-1}{k}c_{1}^{N-1-k}c_{0}^{k}

(502)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}N\left[\binom{N-1}{k}-\binom{N-1}{k-1}\right]c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(503)

\displaystyle\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=2(c_{1}+c_{0})^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\right)^{k}

(504)

\displaystyle=2\left[\sum_{k=0}^{N}\binom{N}{k}c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}\right]\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\right)^{k}\right]

(505)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k-1,N)}\binom{N}{j}\right]c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}.

(506)

Adding these two expressions together and replacing
N
with
2J
for convenience gives us our
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial:

\displaystyle P^{(1)}_{\lambda}(N)

\displaystyle=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}=\left(b^{(1)}_{0},\cdots,b^{(1)}_{N}\,|\,b^{(1)}_{N+1},b^{(1)}_{N+2},\cdots\right)

(507)

\displaystyle b^{(1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=2J\left[\binom{2J-1}{k}-\binom{2J-1}{k-1}\right]+2\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k-1,2J)}\binom{2J}{j}.

(508)

We want to show that
a^{(1)}_{k}=b^{(1)}_{k}
for all
0\leq k\leq\lceil N/2\rceil
. In this domain, our formulas simplify slightly as follows:

\displaystyle a^{(1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=J-k}^{J}2j\left[\binom{2J}{J-j}-\binom{2J}{J-j-1}\right]

(509)

\displaystyle b^{(1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=2J\left[\binom{2J-1}{k}-\binom{2J-1}{k-1}\right]+2\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}.

(510)

We now construct the following chain of equalities:

\displaystyle a^{(1)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=J-k}^{J}2j\left[\binom{2J}{J-j}-\binom{2J}{J-j-1}\right]

(511)

\displaystyle=2(J-k)\binom{2J}{J-(J-k)}+2\sum_{j=J-k+1}^{J}\binom{2J}{J-j}-2J\binom{2J}{J-J-1}

(512)

\displaystyle=2(J-k)\binom{2J}{k}+2\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(513)

\displaystyle=2J\left[\binom{2J-1}{k-1}+\binom{2J-1}{k}\right]-2k\binom{2J}{k}+2\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(514)

\displaystyle=2J\left[\binom{2J-1}{k-1}+\binom{2J-1}{k}\right]-4J\binom{2J-1}{k-1}+2\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(515)

\displaystyle=2J\left[\binom{2J-1}{k}-\binom{2J-1}{k-1}\right]+2\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(516)

\displaystyle=b^{(1)}_{k}.

(517)

Let us clarify each step. The first equality matches
a^{(1)}_{k}
with its formula. The second equality takes advantage of the fact that the previous expression is a telescoping sum. The third equality simplifies the previous expression and makes the substitution
j\mapsto J-j
for the dummy index. The fourth equality uses Pascal’s identity. The fifth equality uses the fact that
\binom{2J}{k}=\frac{2J}{k}\binom{2J-1}{k-1}
. The sixth equality merely simplifies the previous expression. The seventh equality matches
b^{(1)}_{k}
with its formula.

The last task is to bound the error, which we call
e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)
for convenience:

e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]-P^{(1)}_{\lambda}(N).

(518)

From the formulas for
a^{(1)}_{k}
and
b^{(1)}_{k}
, we can infer that

\displaystyle\lceil J\rceil+1\leq k\leq N

\displaystyle\implies-2^{N+1}<a^{(2)}_{k}-b^{(2)}_{k}<0

(519)

\displaystyle k\geq N+1

\displaystyle\implies a^{(2)}_{k}-b^{(2)}_{k}=-2^{N+1}.

(520)

Therefore, the error
e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)
is clearly negative, and we can upper bound its magnitude as follows:

\displaystyle\lvert e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=\lceil J\rceil+1}^{\infty}\left[b^{(2)}_{k}-a^{(2)}_{k}\right]c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(521)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{N+1}\sum_{k=\lceil J\rceil+1}^{\infty}c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(522)

\displaystyle=2^{N+1}c_{1}^{N-\lceil J\rceil-1}c_{0}^{\lceil J\rceil+1}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\right)^{k}

(523)

\displaystyle=2^{N+1}c_{1}^{N-\lceil J\rceil-1}c_{0}^{\lceil J\rceil+1}\frac{c_{1}}{c_{1}-c_{0}}

(524)

\displaystyle=2^{N+1}c_{1}^{N-\lceil J\rceil}c_{0}^{\lceil J\rceil}\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}-c_{0}}

(525)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}2^{N}c_{1}^{N-\lceil J\rceil}c_{0}^{\lceil J\rceil}

(526)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}(4c_{1}c_{0})^{N/2}

(527)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}.

(528)

We thus conclude that the polynomial ansatz
P^{(1)}_{\lambda}(N)
matches
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
up to
O\left(\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\right)
error. In fact, we can say even more precisely that

\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+e^{(1)}(N,\lambda),

(529)

where the error is bounded above and below as follows:

-\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\leq e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)\leq 0.

(530)

This concludes our proof of the first equation in Lemma
18
.

Let us now tackle the second moment, namely
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]
. The efficient thing to do at this point is actually to invoke the formula for
\mathbb{E}[4J^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}(N_{C}+2)]
as shown in Appendix
J
J.1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}(N_{C}+2)]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+\left(3-\lambda^{2}\right)N

(531)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]

\displaystyle=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)

(532)

\displaystyle\therefore\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}(N_{C}+2)]-2\cdot\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]

(533)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}-2e^{(1)}(N,\lambda).

(534)

Therefore, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}+e^{(2)}(N,\lambda),

(535)

where the error
e^{(2)}(N,\lambda)
satisfies

e^{(2)}(\lambda)=-2e^{(1)}(\lambda)\implies 0\leq e^{(2)}(N,\lambda)\leq\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}.

(536)

In this way, we quickly obtain
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]
up to exponentially small error and bounds on the error. More generally, if we want to compute
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{p}]
for
1\leq p\leq P
as efficiently as possible, we should start from
p=1
and work upward, and we should alternate between using the
(c_{1},c_{0})
extended polynomial method for odd
p
values and using
\mathbb{E}\left[(N_{C}(N_{C}+2))^{p/2}\right]
for even
p
values.

However, for the sake of thoroughness, let us prove the formula for
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]
using the
(c_{1},c_{0})
extended polynomial method anyway. Let
b^{(2)}_{k}
denote the coefficient of
c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}
in the polynomial ansatz

P^{(2)}_{\lambda}(N)=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}.

(537)

The first two terms can be written as
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomials. Furthermore, the last term can be written as a
(c_{1},c_{0})
extended polynomial (other than the factor of
-2
, we already wrote it in this manner previously when computing
b^{(1)}_{k}
). We proceed as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}N^{2}

\displaystyle=N^{2}(c_{1}-c_{0})^{2}(c_{1}+c_{0})^{N-2}

(538)

\displaystyle=N^{2}(c_{1}^{2}-2c_{1}c_{0}+c_{0}^{2})\left[\sum_{k=0}^{N-2}\binom{N-2}{k}c_{1}^{N-2-k}c_{0}^{k}\right]

(539)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}N^{2}\left[\binom{N-2}{k}-2\binom{N-2}{k-1}+\binom{N-2}{k-2}\right]c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(540)

\displaystyle(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N

\displaystyle=4Nc_{0}(2c_{1}+c_{0})(c_{1}+c_{0})^{N-2}

(541)

\displaystyle=4N(2c_{1}c_{0}+c_{0}^{2})\left[\sum_{k=0}^{N-2}\binom{N-2}{k}c_{1}^{N-2-k}c_{0}^{k}\right]

(542)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{N}4N\left[2\binom{N-2}{k-1}+\binom{N-2}{k-2}\right]c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(543)

\displaystyle-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}-4\left[\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k-1,N)}\binom{N}{j}\right]c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}.

(544)

Adding these three expressions together and replacing
N
with
2J
for convenience gives us our
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial:

\displaystyle P^{(2)}_{\lambda}(N)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}=\left(b^{(2)}_{0},\cdots,b^{(2)}_{N}\,|\,b^{(2)}_{N+1},b^{(2)}_{N+2},\cdots\right)

(545)

\displaystyle b^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle=4J^{2}\left[\binom{2J-2}{k}-2\binom{2J-2}{k-1}+\binom{2J-2}{k-2}\right]+8J\left[2\binom{2J-2}{k-1}+\binom{2J-2}{k-2}\right]-4\sum_{j=0}^{\min(k-1,2J)}\binom{2J}{j}.

(546)

We want to show that
a^{(2)}_{k}=b^{(2)}_{k}
for all
0\leq k\leq\lceil N/2\rceil
. In this domain, our formulas simplify slightly as follows:

\displaystyle a^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=J-k}^{J}(2j)^{2}\left[\binom{2J}{J-j}-\binom{2J}{J-j-1}\right]

(547)

\displaystyle b^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle=N^{2}\left[\binom{2J-2}{k}-2\binom{2J-2}{k-1}+\binom{2J-2}{k-2}\right]+N\left[8\binom{2J-2}{k-1}+4\binom{2J-2}{k-2}\right]-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}.

(548)

We now construct the following chain of equalities:

\displaystyle a^{(2)}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=J-k}^{J}(2j)^{2}\left[\binom{2J}{J-j}-\binom{2J}{J-j-1}\right]

(549)

\displaystyle=4(J-k)^{2}\binom{2J}{J-(J-k)}+4\sum_{j=J-k+1}^{J}(2j-1)\binom{2J}{J-j}-4J^{2}\binom{2J}{J-J-1}

(550)

\displaystyle=4(J-k)^{2}\binom{2J}{k}+4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(2(J-j)-1)\binom{2J}{j}

(551)

\displaystyle=4(J-k)^{2}\binom{2J}{k}+8J\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}-16J\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J-1}{j-1}-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(552)

\displaystyle=4(J-k)^{2}\binom{2J}{k}+8J\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\left[\binom{2J-1}{j}-\binom{2J-1}{j-1}\right]-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(553)

\displaystyle=4(J-k)^{2}\binom{2J}{k}+8J\binom{2J-1}{k-1}-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(554)

\displaystyle=4J^{2}\binom{2J}{k}-4k(2J-k)\binom{2J}{k}+8J\binom{2J-1}{k-1}-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(555)

\displaystyle=4J^{2}\binom{2J}{k}-8J(2J-k)\binom{2J-1}{k-1}+8J\binom{2J-1}{k-1}-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(556)

\displaystyle=4J^{2}\binom{2J}{k}-16J^{2}\binom{2J-1}{k-1}+8J(k-1)\binom{2J-1}{k-1}+16J\binom{2J-1}{k-1}-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(557)

\displaystyle=4J^{2}\binom{2J}{k}-16J(J-1)\binom{2J-1}{k-1}+8J(2J-1)\binom{2J-2}{k-2}-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(558)

\displaystyle=4J^{2}\binom{2J-2}{k}+(-8J^{2}+16J)\binom{2J-2}{k-1}+(4J^{2}+8J)\binom{2J-2}{k-2}-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(559)

\displaystyle=4J^{2}\left[\binom{2J-2}{k}-2\binom{2J-2}{k-1}+\binom{2J-2}{k-2}\right]+8J\left[2\binom{2J-2}{k-1}+\binom{2J-2}{k-2}\right]-4\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\binom{2J}{j}

(560)

\displaystyle=b^{(2)}_{k}.

(561)

We do not explain each step in detail, but the manipulations used are quite similar to those used to prove that
a^{(1)}_{k}=b^{(1)}_{k}
.

The last task is to bound the error, which we call
e^{(2)}(N,\lambda)
for convenience:

e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]-P^{(2)}_{\lambda}(N).

(562)

From the formulas for
a^{(2)}_{k}
and
b^{(2)}_{k}
, we can infer that

\displaystyle\lceil J\rceil+1\leq k\leq N

\displaystyle\implies 0<a^{(2)}_{k}-b^{(2)}_{k}<2^{N+2}

(563)

\displaystyle k\geq N+1

\displaystyle\implies a^{(2)}_{k}-b^{(2)}_{k}=2^{N+2}.

(564)

Notice that these bounds are just
-2
times the bounds used for
a^{(1)}_{k}-b^{(1)}_{k}
. Therefore, the error
e^{(2)}(N,\lambda)
is clearly positive, and we can upper bound its magnitude as just double of what we obtained for
e^{(1)}(N,\lambda)
:

\displaystyle\lvert e^{(2)}(N,\lambda)\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=\lceil J\rceil+1}^{\infty}\left[a^{(2)}_{k}-b^{(2)}_{k}\right]c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(565)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{N+2}\sum_{k=\lceil J\rceil+1}^{\infty}c_{1}^{N-k}c_{0}^{k}

(566)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}.

(567)

We thus conclude that the polynomial ansatz
P^{(2)}_{\lambda}(N)
matches
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]
up to
O\left(\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}\right)
error. In fact, we can say even more precisely that

\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]=\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}+e^{(2)}(N,\lambda),

(568)

where the error
e^{(2)}(N,\lambda)
is bounded above and below as follows:

0\leq e^{(2)}(N,\lambda)\leq\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}\left(1-\lambda^{2}\right)^{N/2}.

(569)

This concludes our proof of the second equation in Lemma
18
.

We do not present the details here, because they are very tedious, but the same general method works for
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{3}]
and
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{4}]
as well. Of course, it is best to only apply the
(c_{1},c_{0})
polynomial method to
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{3}]
, and then use
\mathbb{E}\left[((N_{C}(N_{C}+2))^{2}\right]
from Appendix
J
J.1
to compute
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{4}]
.

J.5
Full Computation of the Negative Moments of
N_{C}

To conclude this appendix, we combine the results from Appendix
J
J.3
and Appendix
J
J.4
to prove Lemma
17
. In particular, based on the infinite series we show in Appendix
J
J.3
, we need to compute
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-p}\right]
, we need to compute the leading factor
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-p}
, as well as the terms in the infinite series, which take the form

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\right)^{q}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{q}}.

(570)

We begin by restating the first moment (average) of
N_{C}
as shown in Lemma
18
:

\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]=\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+\text{e.s.e.}

(571)

We can take the reciprocal of this quantity as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+\text{e.s.e.}\right)^{-1}

(572)

\displaystyle=(\lambda N)^{-1}\left(1+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\text{e.s.e.}\right)^{-1}

(573)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda N}\left[1-\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]

(574)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{\lambda^{5}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(575)

We can now also compute the centered second moment (variance) of
N_{C}
as follows, using both the first and second equations in Lemma
18
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{2}

(576)

\displaystyle=\left[\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}+\text{e.s.e.}\right]

(577)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+\text{e.s.e.}\right)^{2}

(578)

\displaystyle=\left[\lambda^{2}N^{2}+(1-\lambda)(3+\lambda)N-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda}+\text{e.s.e.}\right]

(579)

\displaystyle\quad-\left[\lambda^{2}N^{2}+2(1-\lambda)N+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}+\text{e.s.e.}\right]

(580)

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda^{2})N-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(581)

We additionally compute the inverse square of the average of
N_{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-2}

\displaystyle=(\lambda N)^{-2}\left(1+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\text{e.s.e.}\right)^{-2}

(582)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}N^{2}}\left[1-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]

(583)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(584)

Combining the previous two results yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\right)^{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)\right]\left[(1-\lambda^{2})N-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}+\text{e.s.e.}\right]

(585)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{(1-\lambda^{2})(3-2\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right).

(586)

We now proceed to the third centered moment. This requires us to use the first three equations from Lemma
18
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\right)^{3}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{3}]-3\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]+3\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{2}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{3}

(587)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{3}]-3\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]-2\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{3}

(588)

\displaystyle=-2\lambda(1-\lambda^{2})N+\frac{2(1-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{3}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(589)

We additionally compute the inverse cube of the average of
N_{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-3}

\displaystyle=(\lambda N)^{-3}\left(1+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\text{e.s.e.}\right)^{-3}

(590)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}N^{3}}\left[1+O\left(N^{-1}\right)\right]

(591)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(592)

Combining the previous two results yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}])^{3}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{3}}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)\right]\left[-2\lambda(1-\lambda^{2})N+O(1)\right]

(593)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2(1-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right).

(594)

Finally, we proceed to the fourth centered moment. This requires us to use all four equations from Lemma
18
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\right)^{4}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{4}]-4\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{3}\right]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]+6\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{2}

(595)

\displaystyle\quad-4\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{3}+\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{4}

(596)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}^{4}]-4\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{3}\right]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]+6\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{2}-3\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{4}

(597)

\displaystyle=3(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}N^{2}+\frac{2(1-\lambda^{2})(-3+2\lambda^{2}+3\lambda^{4})}{\lambda^{2}}N-\frac{(1-\lambda^{2})(3+\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(598)

We additionally compute the inverse fourth power of the average of
N_{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-4}

\displaystyle=(\lambda N)^{-4}\left(1+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\text{e.s.e.}\right)^{-4}

(599)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{4}N^{4}}\left[1+O\left(N^{-1}\right)\right]

(600)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{4}}+O\left(N^{-5}\right).

(601)

Combining the previous two results yields

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}])^{4}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{4}}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{4}}+O\left(N^{-5}\right)\right]\left[3(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}N^{2}+O(N)\right]

(602)

\displaystyle=\frac{3(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right).

(603)

A useful sanity check for all the above results is the Gaussian approximation for the distribution of
N_{C}
, which has mean
\sim\lambda N
and variance
\sim(1-\lambda^{2})N

Keyl and Werner 2001
. As a result, the centered moments of
N_{C}
should always have leading-order behavior

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\right)^{p}\right]=\begin{cases}(1-\lambda^{2})^{p/2}N^{p/2}+O\left(N^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\right)&p\text{ even}\\
O\left(N^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\right)&p\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(604)

for any nonnegative integer
p
. This in turn implies that the terms in the infinite series always have leading-order behavior

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]\right)^{p}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{p}}=\begin{cases}\frac{(1-\lambda^{2})^{p/2}}{\lambda^{p}}N^{-p/2}+O\left(N^{-\frac{p}{2}-1}\right)&p\text{ even}\\
O\left(N^{-\frac{p+1}{2}}\right)&p\text{ odd}\end{cases}

(605)

for any positive integer
p\geq 2
.

We can now combine all of the above results to compute
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-1}\right]
to
\Theta\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)
precision. Since we need to compute the first three terms of this expansion, this specific calculation is the setting where all the fancy tricks (and especially the positive moments up to exponentially small error, as shown in Lemma
18
) are the most useful:

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-1}\right]

(606)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-1}\left[1+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}])^{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{2}}-\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}])^{3}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{3}}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}])^{4}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{4}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]

(607)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{\lambda^{5}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)\right]\Bigg\{1+\left[\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{(1-\lambda^{2})(3-2\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]

(608)

\displaystyle\quad-\left[-\frac{2(1-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]+\left[\frac{3(1-\lambda^{2})^{2}}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\Bigg\}

(609)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{\lambda^{5}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)\right]\Bigg\{1+\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{(1-\lambda^{2})(2-\lambda)}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\Bigg\}

(610)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+2\lambda-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(611)

We conclude that

\boxed{\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-1}\right]=\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+2\lambda-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)},

(612)

which precisely matches the first result in Lemma
17
. Next, we compute
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-2}\right]
to
\Theta\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)
precision, which is actually easier than the previous calculation, since here we only need to compute the first two terms of this expansion:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-2}\left[1+3\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[(N_{C}-\mathbb{E}[N_{C}])^{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{2}}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]

(613)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}-\frac{2(1-\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)\right]\left[1+\frac{3(1-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]

(614)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+3\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(615)

We conclude that

\boxed{\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-2}\right]=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+3\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)},

(616)

which precisely matches the second result in Lemma
17
. Finally, we compute
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-3}\right]
to
\Theta\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)
precision, which of course is the easiest of all:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-3}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]^{-3}\left[1+O\left(N^{-1}\right)\right]

(617)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)\right]\left[1+O\left(N^{-1}\right)\right]

(618)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(619)

We conclude that

\boxed{\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-3}\right]=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)},

(620)

which precisely matches the third and final result in Lemma
17
.

Appendix K
Understanding the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
Values

Throughout this paper, we have to invoke the matrix representation of the post-Schur-sampling state
\rho_{N_{C}/2}
when written in the basis of symmetrized Hamming weight states. In particular, we commonly refer to “the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values”, which are defined as follows:

P_{w,w^{\prime}}\coloneqq\bra{w^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}\ket{(w^{\prime})^{(s)}}.

(621)

This appendix is dedicated to proving the various useful facts about the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values that appear throughout this paper. This appendix is organized as follows:

•

In Appendix
K
K.1
, we will prove some miscellaneous useful formulas for these values.

–

This includes one formula that will be useful in Appendix
N
when studying the behavior of the optimal protocol in the equatorial case for
\lambda\approx 1
.

–

This also includes one formula that is helpful for the numerical analysis of these coherence distillation protocols.

•

In Appendix
K
K.2
, we explain why we organize the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values into those with a fixed “offset”
\alpha
(meaning values of the form
P_{w-\alpha,w}
), and we explain why we only focus on
\alpha=0
and
\alpha=1
as the ones that matter for the asymptotic analysis of optimal single-shot coherence distillation, as explained in Appendices
E
,
F
,
G
, and
I
.

•

In Appendix
K
K.3
, we show how to compute the relevant “moments” of the
P_{w,w}
values (corresponding to offset
\alpha=0
).

•

In Appendix
K
K.4
, we show how to compute the relevant “moments” of the
P_{w-1,w}
values (corresponding to offset
\alpha=1
) in the equatorial special case.

•

In Appendix
K
K.5
, we show how to compute the relevant “moments” of the
P_{w-1,w}
values (corresponding to offset
\alpha=1
) in the general case.

K.1
Miscellaneous Useful Facts

Since we can write the Schur-sampled state as

\rho_{N_{C}/2}=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N-k}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}},

(622)

we can write
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
as follows:

P_{w,w^{\prime}}=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N-k}Q_{wk}Q_{w^{\prime}k},

(623)

where for convenience, we have defined

\displaystyle Q_{wk}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bra{w^{(s)}}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}.

(624)

As a reminder, the computational basis states for a specific direction
\hat{n}
are defined so that
\ket{0_{\hat{n}}}
is a
-1
eigenstate of
\hat{n}\cdot\vec{\sigma}
, while
\ket{1_{\hat{n}}}
is a
+1
eigenstate of
\hat{n}\cdot\vec{\sigma}
. (This convention may seem backward, but it is done to keep in line with the convention used by Cirac et al.
Cirac et al. 1999
.) In particular, let
\hat{n}=\langle\sin\Theta,0,\cos\Theta\rangle
for some
0\leq\Theta\leq\pi
be some unit vector with azimuthal angle
\phi=0
. Then the computational basis states for direction
\hat{n}
are

\displaystyle\ket{0_{\hat{n}}}

\displaystyle=\sin\frac{\Theta}{2}\ket{0}-\cos\frac{\Theta}{2}\ket{1}

(625)

\displaystyle\ket{1_{\hat{n}}}

\displaystyle=\cos\frac{\Theta}{2}\ket{0}+\sin\frac{\Theta}{2}\ket{1}.

(626)

As a result, the inner product of a single-bit computational basis state with a single-bit state in some other direction given by polar angle
\Theta
(assuming that it has azimuthal angle
\phi=0
) is

\bra{b}\ket{b^{\prime}_{\hat{n}}}=\begin{cases}\sin\frac{\Theta}{2}&b=0,b^{\prime}=0\\
\cos\frac{\Theta}{2}&b=0,b^{\prime}=1\\
-\cos\frac{\Theta}{2}&b=1,b^{\prime}=0\\
\sin\frac{\Theta}{2}&b=1,b^{\prime}=1.\end{cases}

(627)

We can thus compute the
Q_{wk}
values as follows:

\displaystyle Q_{wk}

\displaystyle=\bra{w^{(s)}}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}

(628)

\displaystyle=\binom{N_{C}}{w}^{-1/2}\binom{N_{C}}{k}^{-1/2}\sum_{w(b)=w}\sum_{w(b^{\prime})=k}\bra{b}\ket{b^{\prime}_{\hat{n}}}

(629)

\displaystyle=\binom{N_{C}}{w}^{-1/2}\binom{N_{C}}{k}^{-1/2}\sum_{i=0}^{w}(-1)^{i}\left(\sin\frac{\Theta}{2}\right)^{N_{C}-k+w-2i}\left(\cos\frac{\Theta}{2}\right)^{k-w+2i}\binom{N_{C}}{k}\binom{N_{C}-k}{i}\binom{k}{w-i}

(630)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{k}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}}\left(\sin\frac{\Theta}{2}\right)^{N_{C}-k+w}\left(\cos\frac{\Theta}{2}\right)^{k-w}\sum_{i=0}^{w}(-1)^{i}\left(\frac{1+\cos\Theta}{1-\cos\Theta}\right)^{i}\binom{N_{C}-k}{i}\binom{k}{w-i}.

(631)

For clarity, what we have done here is let the dummy index
i
refer to the number of times a
\bra{1}
from
\bra{b}
collides with a
\ket{0_{\hat{n}}}
from
\ket{b^{\prime}_{\hat{n}}}
, which is what governs the number of
-1
factors. There are
\binom{N_{C}}{k}
ways to choose the string
b^{\prime}
, then
\binom{N_{C}-k}{i}
ways to choose the locations at which a
\bra{1}
from
\bra{b}
collides with a
\ket{0_{\hat{n}}}
from
\ket{b^{\prime}_{\hat{n}}}
, and then
\binom{k}{w-i}
ways to choose the locations at which a
\bra{1}
from
\bra{b}
collides with a
\ket{1_{\hat{n}}}
from
\ket{b^{\prime}_{\hat{n}}}
.

In the equatorial case
\hat{n}=\hat{x}
, this formula simplifies somewhat as follows:

\displaystyle Q_{wk}

\displaystyle=\bra{w^{(s)}}\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}

(632)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}}\sum_{w(b)=w}\bra{b}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{k}}}\sum_{w(b^{\prime})=k}\ket{b^{\prime}_{X}}\right)

(633)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{k}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}}\sum_{w(b)=w}\bra{b}\left(\ket{+}^{\otimes k}\ket{-}^{\otimes(N_{C}-k)}\right)

(634)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{k}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}2^{N_{C}}}}\sum_{i=0}^{w}(-1)^{i}\binom{N_{C}-k}{i}\binom{k}{w-i}.

(635)

Notice that plugging
\Theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
into the previous formula yields this result as well. This specific formula will actually find its greatest use in Appendix
N
, where we compute the behavior of the optimal coherence distillation protocol in the equatorial case for
\lambda
very close to
1
. In that setting, we treat the protocol as a power series in
c_{0}=\frac{1-\lambda}{2}
up to second order, meaning that we only need
k=N_{C},N_{C}-1,N_{C}-2
. In that setting, the somewhat complicated summation for
Q_{wk}
simplifies considerably, since it only has
1,2,3
nonzero terms (respectively).

At the moment, our formula for the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values may appear to be a triple summation, since it is a summation of the product
Q_{wk}Q_{w^{\prime}k}
, and since each of
Q_{wk}
and
Q_{w^{\prime}k}
is its own summation. However, it turns out that there is a much simpler formula for
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
, which only requires a single summation:

Lemma 19
.

Consider an input state of the form

\rho=\frac{\mathbb{I}+\lambda_{x}X+\lambda_{z}Z}{2}\quad\quad\quad\lambda_{x}=\lambda\sin\Theta_{\text{in}}\quad\quad\lambda_{z}=\lambda\cos\Theta_{\text{in}}.

(636)

Then the resulting
P_{w-\alpha,w}
values take the following form for all
0\leq\alpha\leq w\leq N
:

P_{w-\alpha,w}=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{x}}{1-\lambda_{z}}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1+\lambda_{z}}{2}\right)^{N}\left(\frac{1-\lambda_{z}}{1+\lambda_{z}}\right)^{w}\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N}{w}}{\binom{N}{w-\alpha}}}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\binom{w}{j+\alpha}\binom{N-w}{j}\left(\frac{\lambda_{x}^{2}}{1-\lambda_{z}^{2}}\right)^{j}.

(637)

Proof.

In this case, it actually helps to return to the perspective of the original tensor product state. In particular, we know that

\left(\Pi_{j=N_{C}/2}\right)\rho^{\otimes N_{C}}\left(\Pi_{j=N_{C}/2}\right)=p\left(j=\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right)\rho_{N_{C}/2},

(638)

where
p\left(j=N_{C}/2\right)
equals the probability of getting the highest possible total angular momentum when performing Schur sampling
Cirac et al. 1999
:

p\left(j=\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right)=\frac{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}{c_{1}-c_{0}}.

(639)

Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle p\left(j=\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right)P_{w-\alpha,w}

\displaystyle=p\left(j=\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right)\bra{(w-\alpha)^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(640)

\displaystyle=\bra{(w-\alpha)^{(s)}}\left(\Pi_{j=N_{C}/2}\right)\rho^{\otimes N_{C}}\left(\Pi_{j=N_{C}/2}\right)\ket{w^{(s)}}

(641)

\displaystyle=\bra{(w-\alpha)^{(s)}}\rho^{\otimes N_{C}}\ket{w^{(s)}}.

(642)

From now on, we can consider the tensor product state
\rho^{\otimes N_{C}}
. We start by writing

\rho^{\otimes N_{C}}=\frac{1}{2^{N_{C}}}\sum_{P\in\{I,X,Z\}^{\otimes N_{C}}}\lambda_{x}^{w_{X}(P)}\lambda_{z}^{w_{Z}(P)}P,

(643)

where the summation is taken over all
N
-qubit Pauli strings
P
that have only
I,X,Z
symbols, and
w_{X}(P)
denotes the
X
-weight of
P
, namely the number of
X
symbols in
P
(and
w_{Z}(P)
is defined analogously). It follows that

\displaystyle p\left(j=\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right)P_{w-\alpha,w}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N_{C}}}\sum_{P\in\{I,X,Z\}^{\otimes N_{C}}}\lambda_{x}^{w_{X}(P)}\lambda_{z}^{w_{Z}(P)}\bra{(w-\alpha)^{(s)}}P\ket{w^{(s)}}

(644)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N_{C}}}\sum_{P\in\{I,X,Z\}^{\otimes N_{C}}}\lambda_{x}^{w_{X}(P)}\lambda_{z}^{w_{Z}(P)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w-\alpha}\binom{N_{C}}{w}}}\sum_{w(b)=w-\alpha}\sum_{w(b^{\prime})=w}\bra{b}P\ket{b^{\prime}}.

(645)

As a result, for each bit string
b^{\prime}
with Hamming weight
w
, we need to count the number of Pauli strings
P
such that
P\ket{b^{\prime}}
is (up to sign) a computational basis state
b
with Hamming weight
w-\alpha
. Since the
X
symbols are the only ones (out of
I
,
X
,
Z
) that change the Hamming weight, the number of
X
symbols acting on a
1
bit has to be exactly
\alpha
more than the number of
X
symbols acting on a
0
bit. As a result, there are

\binom{w}{\frac{w_{X}(P)+\alpha}{2}}\binom{N_{C}-w}{\frac{w_{X}(P)-\alpha}{2}}

(646)

ways to choose the locations of the
X
symbols in the Pauli strings.

Let us consider what happens when we consider a Pauli string
P
that has just
I
and
X
symbols, and then choose some subset of the
I
symbols to replace with
Z
symbols. Due to the
\lambda_{z}^{w_{Z}(P)}
factor in the summation, and due to the fact that
Z\ket{0}=\ket{0}
but
Z\ket{1}=-\ket{1}
, each
I
replaced with a
Z
acting on a
0
bit in
b^{\prime}
changes the term associated with that Pauli string by a factor of
+\lambda_{z}
, while each
I
replaced with a
Z
acting on a
1
bit in
b^{\prime}
changes the term associated with that Pauli string by a factor of
-\lambda_{z}
. As a result, when we sum over all Pauli strings
P
with a fixed set of locations for the
X
symbols but with all
2^{N-w_{X}(P)}
possible choices of
I
and
Z
symbols, we accumulate a factor of

(1+\lambda_{z})^{N_{C}-w-j}(1-\lambda_{z})^{w-j-\alpha},

(647)

where we define
j\equiv\frac{w_{X}(P)-\alpha}{2}
as a convenient summation index. By summing over all possible values of
j
, we can simplify our summation to

p\left(j=\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right)P_{w-\alpha,w}=\frac{1}{2^{N_{C}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w-\alpha}\binom{N_{C}}{w}}}\sum_{w(b^{\prime})=w}\sum_{j=0}^{N_{C}}(1+\lambda_{z})^{N_{C}-w-j}(1-\lambda_{z})^{w-j-\alpha}\lambda_{x}^{2j+\alpha}.

(648)

Finally, we note that there are
\binom{N_{C}}{w}
input bit strings with Hamming weight
w
, and each of them contributes equally to the sum. We thus obtain

\displaystyle p\left(j=\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right)P_{w-\alpha,w}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N_{C}}}\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w}\binom{N_{C}}{w-\alpha}}}\sum_{j=0}^{N_{C}}\binom{w}{j+\alpha}\binom{N_{C}-w}{j}(1+\lambda_{z})^{N_{C}-w-j}(1-\lambda_{z})^{w-j-\alpha}\lambda_{x}^{2j+\alpha}

(649)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{x}^{\alpha}}{2^{N_{C}}}\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w-\alpha}}}\sum_{j=0}^{N_{C}}\binom{w}{j+\alpha}\binom{N_{C}-w}{j}(1+\lambda_{z})^{N_{C}-w-j}(1-\lambda_{z})^{w-j-\alpha}\lambda_{x}^{2j}

(650)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\lambda_{x}}{1-\lambda_{z}}\right)^{\alpha}\left(\frac{1+\lambda_{z}}{2}\right)^{N_{C}}\left(\frac{1-\lambda_{z}}{1+\lambda_{z}}\right)^{w}\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N}{w}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w-\alpha}}}\sum_{j=0}^{N_{C}}\binom{w}{j+\alpha}\binom{N_{C}-w}{j}\left(\frac{\lambda_{x}^{2}}{1-\lambda_{z}^{2}}\right)^{j},

(651)

exactly as desired.
∎

As a corollary, we can simplify the formula even a bit further in the equatorial case:

Corollary 20
.

The equatorial case
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
, which implies
\lambda_{x}=\lambda
and
\lambda_{z}=0
, yields the simplified formula

P_{w-\alpha,w}=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\frac{\lambda^{\alpha}}{2^{N_{C}}}\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{\binom{N}{w-\alpha}}}\sum_{j=0}^{w-\alpha}\binom{w}{j+\alpha}\binom{N_{C}-w}{j}\lambda^{2j}.

(652)

The primary advantage of Lemma
19
is actually
numerical
, rather than
analytical
. In particular, even though the single summation is quite complicated, it is much more efficient to evaluate numerically. Furthermore, the binomial coefficients in the summation have an especially nice structure. In particular, one can write the summation in the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
value in the form of a nested evaluation structure. For example, in the equatorial case (see Corollary
20
, the summation can be written as follows:

\displaystyle A_{0}\Big\{1+A_{1}\lambda^{2}\left[1+A_{2}\lambda^{2}\left(1+\cdots\{1+A_{w-1}\lambda^{2}\}\cdots\right)\right]\Big\}

(653)

\displaystyle A_{0}=w,\quad A_{j}=\frac{(N_{C}-w-j+1)(w-j)}{j(j+1)}\quad(1\leq j\leq w-1).

(654)

Using this nested evaluation structure, one can write a computer program to evaluate these
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values very efficiently and also without directly invoking any large binomial coefficients. This is very nice, because it substantially increases the values of
N
for which one can carry out effective numerics; otherwise, the act of computing large binomial coefficients explicitly would introduce numerical errors much sooner.

K.2
Defining the “Moments” of the
P_{w-\alpha,w}
Values

Now we discuss the properties of the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values that are the most important for our asymptotic analysis of single-shot qubit coherence distillation. In this appendix, we will always fix an integer
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, which we call the
offset
, and then we will study the values
P_{w-\alpha,w}
for
\alpha\leq w\leq N_{C}
. These values form a “distribution”, with the caveat that they are not normalized for
\alpha\neq 0
. (For
\alpha=0
, these values are the diagonal entries, and hence they must add up to
1
. For small values of
\alpha
, they add up to only slightly less than
1
.) Hence we can study the moments of these “distributions”, which turn out to play a crucial role in the asymptotic analysis of single-shot qubit coherence distillation, as shown in Appendices
E
,
F
,
G
, and
I
.

So why do we separate the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values by their offset, and why do we only focus on
\alpha=0
and
\alpha=1
? As shown in Appendix
D
, because our output state is a single qubit, the only
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values we care about are actually
P_{w-1,w}
and
P_{w,w}
. In general, when a state with
U(1)
asymmetry is written in the energy eigenbasis, each matrix entry will have an integer frequency at which it time evolves, and this is what we refer to as the “modes” of a coherent state. The state
\rho^{\otimes N}
has modes ranging from
-N
to
+N
(and similarly, the Schur-sampled state
\rho_{N_{C}/2}
has modes ranging from
-N_{C}
to
+N_{C}
), but our single-qubit output state only has modes
-1,0,+1
. (These ideas are shown more explicitly in the first proof of the Kraus representation of an optimal coherence distillation protocol, which is shown in Appendix
B
B.3
.) It turns out that
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
corresponds precisely to a matrix entry that time evolves with frequency
w^{\prime}-w
(in other words, the offset is the same as the frequency of time evolution). However, since the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values are known to be symmetric in
w
and
w^{\prime}
, the distribution with offset
-\alpha
is the same as the distribution with offset
+\alpha
. Hence the only values that we need to study are
P_{w-1,w}
(offset
\alpha=1
) and
P_{w,w}
(offset
\alpha=0
).

In general, we would like to study centered moments of these distributions. Furthermore, it is a little bit nicer to consider moments of
w/N_{C}
, rather than just
w
itself, since that helps make the decay of higher moments more apparent. As a result, it is natural to define the following quantity, which is the mean (first moment) of
w/N_{C}
under the distribution with offset
0
:

\mu\coloneqq\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\frac{w}{N_{C}}P_{w,w}.

(655)

Since the
P_{w,w}
values concentrate around
w\approx\mu N
, the performance of a coherence distillation protocol is controlled primarily by how it acts in the vicinity of
w\approx\mu N
. Therefore, it makes sense to define this quantity as an anchor point and optimize the behavior of the protocol in this range. We explain how to do this more systematically in Appendix
E
, and we carry out that optimization of the protocol in Appendices
F
,
G
, and
I
.

As we will see in Appendix
K
K.3
, the quantity
\mu
we defined above has the following form (where we will use “e.s.e.” from now on as an abbreviation for “exponentially small error”):

\mu=\frac{1-C_{\text{in}}}{2}+\frac{C_{\text{in}}(1-\lambda)}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(656)

Based on this, we can define the
p^{\text{th}}
centered moment of the distribution with offset
\alpha
as follows:

\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(\alpha)}\coloneqq\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\left(\frac{w-\frac{\alpha}{2}}{N_{C}}-\mu\right)^{p}P_{w-\alpha,w}.

(657)

Notice that we subtract
\frac{\alpha}{2}
from
w
because we want the quantity whose moments we are computing to be symmetric in the indices
w-\alpha
and
w
.

K.3
Moments of the
P_{w,w}
Values (Offset
\alpha=0
)

Now that we have defined the moments of the
P_{w-\alpha,w}
values, we are ready to state our first main result, which concerns the centered moments for offset
\alpha=0
:

Lemma 21

(Centered moments of the
P_{w,w}
values)
.

The centered moments for offset
\alpha=0
take the following values up to
\Theta(N_{C}^{-2})
precision:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=1

(658)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=0

(659)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)\left(\left(C_{\text{in}}^{2}-S_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)+C_{\text{in}}^{2}\lambda\right)}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(660)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}(3-\lambda^{2})}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)

(661)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3S_{\text{in}}^{4}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right).

(662)

To prove this, we will instead compute moments that are centered at
\frac{N_{C}}{2}
, instead of at the value
\mu
stated previously. In particular, define the following quantity:

\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(0)}\coloneqq\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\left(\frac{w}{N_{C}}-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{p}P_{w,w}

(663)

The reason this quantity is nice is that, if we consider the total
z
-spin operator

S_{z}\coloneqq 2J_{z}=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}Z_{j},

(664)

then this operator satisfies

S_{z}\ket{w^{(s)}}=(N_{C}-2w)\ket{w^{(s)}}\quad(0\leq w\leq N_{C}).

(665)

Therefore, we can immediately write

\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(0)}=\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\left(\frac{w}{N_{C}}-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{p}P_{w,w}=(-2N_{C})^{-p}\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}(N_{C}-2w)^{p}P_{w,w}=(-2N_{C})^{-p}\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{p}\right].

(666)

Now we need to find another way to compute the above trace. We can do it by having the powers of
S_{z}
act on
\rho_{N_{C}/2}
instead. Note that
\rho_{N_{C}/2}
is diagonal in the basis of
\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
states for
0\leq k\leq N_{C}
. As a result, we just need to know how
S_{z}
acts on such states. Fortunately, the action of
S_{z}
on such states is actually relatively simple. This is because, in the spin-
j
representation of
SU(2)
,
S_{z}
is a diagonal operator, while
S_{x}
and
S_{y}
have entries only on the superdiagonal and subdiagonal. In this way, they only produce interactions between adjacent Hamming weights.

In particular, since we are considering symmetrized Hamming weight states based on the Bloch vector
\hat{n}=S_{\text{in}}\hat{x}+C_{\text{in}}\hat{z}
, the operator
S_{z}
actually acts as
S_{\text{in}}S_{x}-C_{\text{in}}S_{z}
on the basis of
\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
states. Therefore, we have the following equation:

\displaystyle S_{z}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}

\displaystyle=S_{\text{in}}\sqrt{k(N_{C}-k+1)}\ket{(k-1)_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}

(667)

\displaystyle\quad-C_{\text{in}}(N_{C}-2k)\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}

(668)

\displaystyle\quad+S_{\text{in}}\sqrt{(k+1)N_{C}-k)}\ket{(k+1)_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}.

(669)

Due to the above fact,
S_{z}^{p}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
will contain terms from
\ket{(k-p)_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
to
\ket{(k+p)_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
. Each term can be produced by some path of length
p
, where at each step, you choose to increase the Hamming weight by
1
, decrease it by
1
, or keep the Hamming weight the same. We will call these options “up” (U), “down” (D), and “middle” (M), respectively. However, only the
\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
term will contribute to the trace, which corresponds to all possible paths that have net zero Hamming weight change (in other words, an equal number of U’s and D’s).

This yields a general strategy for computing
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(0)}
:

•

First, tabulate all paths of length
p
of the form above (each step is drawn from
\{U,D,M\}
, and there are an equal number of U’s and D’s).

•

Second, for each such path, write out the multiplicative factor that comes from each step. (For example, each “up” step produces a
\sqrt{(k+1)(N_{C}-k)}
factor when moving from Hamming weight
k
to Hamming weight
k+1
.)

•

Third, multiply all these factors to obtain the overall contribution for that path, and add up these contributions over all the paths. This yields the quantity

\mathcal{T}_{p}^{(0)}\coloneqq\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{p}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}.

(670)

•

Fourth and finally, multiply this quantity by the prefactor

\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k},

(671)

and sum over all
0\leq k\leq N_{C}
.

Interestingly, if the input states are equatorial (
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
), then the “middle” path closes, and only “up” and “down” steps are permitted. In this setting, the options reduce considerably. For example,
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(0)}
is automatically equal to
0
for odd
p
. If the output states are equatorial, then this simplification actually does not help us anyway, since bit-flip symmetry actually renders the
P_{w,w}
values irrelevant, such that only the
P_{w-1,w}
values contribute to the fidelity in a nontrivial way. However, if the input states are equatorial and the output states are non-equatorial, then this could provide some simplification.

Let us begin with the zeroth uncentered moment. Of course, this is actually trivial:

\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(0)}=\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}\right]=1.

(672)

And of course, converting this uncentered moment into a centered moment is also trivial, since
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}=\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(0)}
:

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(0)}=1}.

(673)

Now we proceed to the calculation of the first moment. By the definition of the centered moments, it is trivial to say that

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{1}^{(0)}=0}.

(674)

So here we actually only care about computing the uncentered moment
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}
, since that quantity will inform the mean
\mu
, which is then used for the centering for all other moments.

In this case, the only path of length
1
that stays at the same Hamming weight is a single M step. As a result, we get an overall factor of
-(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}
, leading to the following summation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=(-2N_{C})^{-1}\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}(-C_{\text{in}})(N_{C}-2k)

(675)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\frac{C_{\text{in}}}{2N_{C}}\left[-N_{C}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{0}^{k}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}+2\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}kc_{0}^{k}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}\right].

(676)

Note that we also invert the summation order with the mapping
k\mapsto N_{C}-k
, so that the exponent of
c_{0}
increases with
k
, while the exponent of
c_{1}
decreases with
k
.

Now we need to make a number of approximations. Fortunately, all of these approximations will have only exponentially small error. The first approximation is to approximate the denominator in the leading prefactor as follows (we will make this approximation repeatedly):

c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}=c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}(1+\text{e.s.e.}).

(677)

The second approximation is to extend all of the summations to
k=\infty
, instead of terminating them at
k=N_{C}
. All of these summations have a decaying geometric sequence with common ratio
(c_{0}/c_{1})
, times some polynomial of
k
, and these are all summations that can be computed using standard manipulations of the infinite geometric series. Furthermore, truncating any such summation after
N_{C}
terms yields only exponentially small error. In particular, for the evaluation of
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}
, we need the following summations:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-x}

(678)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}kx^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{x}{(1-x)^{2}}.

(679)

It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{0}^{k}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}}{\lambda}(1+\text{e.s.e.})

(680)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}kc_{0}^{k}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}c_{0}}{\lambda^{2}}(1+\text{e.s.e.}).

(681)

Plugging these results into the formula for
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}
and simplifying yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\frac{C_{\text{in}}}{2N_{C}}\left[-N_{C}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{0}^{k}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}+2\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}kc_{0}^{k}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}\right]

(682)

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}}(1+\text{e.s.e.})\frac{C_{\text{in}}}{2N_{C}}\left[-N_{C}\left(\frac{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}}{\lambda}\right)(1+\text{e.s.e.})+2\left(\frac{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}c_{0}}{\lambda^{2}}\right)(1+\text{e.s.e.})\right]

(683)

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\text{in}}}{2N_{C}}\left[-N_{C}(1)+2\left(\frac{c_{0}}{\lambda}\right)\right]+\text{e.s.e.}

(684)

\displaystyle=-\frac{C_{\text{in}}}{2}+\frac{(1-\lambda)C_{\text{in}}}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(685)

Finally, note that
\mu
is related to the uncentered moment
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}
as follows:

\mu\coloneqq\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\frac{w}{N_{C}}P_{w,w}=\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}+\frac{1}{2}.

(686)

We conclude that
\mu
, the mean value of
w/N_{C}
under the distribution given by the
P_{w,w}
values, takes the following form:

\boxed{\mu=\frac{1-C_{\text{in}}}{2}+\frac{(1-\lambda)C_{\text{in}}}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\text{e.s.e.}}

(687)

We now proceed to computing the second uncentered and centered moments. Now, there are
3
possible up-down-middle paths that have net zero Hamming weight change, and each of them contributes a term as follows:

\displaystyle(U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(688)

\displaystyle(M,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}^{2}

(689)

\displaystyle(D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(690)

Adding these three terms yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{2}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}

(692)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[N_{C}(N_{C}+2)-(N_{C}-2k)^{2}\right]S_{\text{in}}^{2}+(N_{C}-2k)^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}

(693)

\displaystyle=\left[N_{C}\left(-1+(N_{C}+1)C_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)\right]+\left[2N_{C}\left(1-3C_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)\right]k+\left[2\left(-1+3C_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)\right]k^{2}.

(694)

This implies that

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=(-2N_{C})^{-2}\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\quad\Bigg\{N_{C}\left(-1+(N_{C}+1)C_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}c_{0}^{k}

(695)

\displaystyle\quad+2N_{C}\left(1-3C_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}kc_{1}^{N_{C}-k}c_{0}^{k}+2\left(-1+3C_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}k^{2}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}c_{0}^{k}\Bigg\}.

(696)

Once again, we now need to make some approximations. We actually use exactly the same approximations that we used for
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}
, except now we add one extra. In particular, the infinite sum

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k^{2}x^{k}=\frac{x(1+x)}{(1-x)^{3}}

(697)

implies that

\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}k^{2}c_{0}^{k}c_{1}^{N_{C}-k}=\frac{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}c_{0}}{\lambda^{3}}(1+\text{e.s.e.}).

(698)

Plugging in all these approximations and simplifying yields

\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(0)}=\frac{C_{\text{in}}^{2}}{4}+\left[\frac{1+(-3+2\lambda)C_{\text{in}}^{2}}{2\lambda}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(-2+3C_{\text{in}}^{2})}{8\lambda^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(699)

Finally, we wish to relate the uncentered moment
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(0)}
to the centered moment
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(0)}
. We can do so using the formula
\text{Var}[X]=\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}
, which implies that

\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(0)}=\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(0)}-\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}\right)^{2}.

(700)

Simplifying this quantity reveals that the second centered moment of the
P_{w,w}
values looks as follows:

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(0)}=\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)\left(\left(C_{\text{in}}^{2}-S_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)+C_{\text{in}}^{2}\lambda\right)}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\text{e.s.e.}}

(701)

This completes the proof of the
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(0)}
formula that we stated in Lemma
21
. The first term in this formula can actually be understood as a straightforward consequence of the fact that the
P_{w,w}
values, as a distribution for the Hamming weight
w
, approximate a Gaussian with mean
\sim\frac{1-C_{\text{in}}}{2}N_{C}
and variance
\sim\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}{4\lambda}N_{C}
. However, the second term in this formula requires an understanding of the deviations away from this Gaussian behavior.

Now we proceed to the third moment. Here are all the up-down-middle paths of length
3
and net zero Hamming weight change, along with their respective contributions:

\displaystyle(M,M,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(N_{C}-2k)^{3}C_{\text{in}}^{3}

(702)

\displaystyle(U,M,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2(k+1))C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(703)

\displaystyle(D,M,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad-k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2(k-1))C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(704)

\displaystyle(U,D,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(705)

\displaystyle(M,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad-(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(706)

\displaystyle(D,U,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad-k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(707)

\displaystyle(M,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad-k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(708)

When we add these quantities together, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{3}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{3}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}

(709)

\displaystyle=-C_{\text{in}}\left(4k+12k^{3}-2N_{C}-6kN_{C}-18k^{2}N_{C}+3N_{C}^{2}+6kN_{C}^{2}\right)

(710)

\displaystyle\quad-C_{\text{in}}^{3}\left(-4k-20k^{3}+2N_{C}+6kN_{C}+30k^{2}N_{C}-3N_{C}^{2}-12kN_{C}^{2}+N_{C}^{3}\right).

(711)

We now plug this quantity into our infinite summation:

\mathcal{U}_{3}^{(0)}=(-2N_{C})^{3}\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}\mathcal{T}_{3}^{(0)}.

(712)

After making the usual approximations and invoking the formulas for
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k^{p}x^{k}
for
p=0,1,2,3
, we eventually obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{3}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\text{in}}^{3}}{8}+\frac{3C_{\text{in}}\left[1+C_{\text{in}}^{2}(-2+\lambda)\right]}{8\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}

(713)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C_{\text{in}}\left[(-9+6\lambda+\lambda^{2})+C_{\text{in}}^{2}(15-12\lambda-\lambda^{2})\right]}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(714)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C_{\text{in}}(1-\lambda)\left[(9-\lambda^{2})+3C_{\text{in}}^{2}(5-\lambda^{2})\right]}{8\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(715)

Finally, we wish to relate the uncentered moment
\mathcal{U}_{3}^{(0)}
to the centered moment
\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(0)}
. We can do so using the following formula:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mathbb{E}[X])^{3}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X^{3}]-3\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\mathbb{E}[X]+3\mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}-\mathbb{E}[X]^{3}

(716)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X^{3}]-3\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\mathbb{E}[X]+2\mathbb{E}[X]^{3},

(717)

which in turn implies that

\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(0)}=\mathcal{U}_{3}^{(0)}-3\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(0)}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}+2\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}\right)^{3}.

(718)

Plugging in the values that we have already computed yields

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(0)}=\frac{C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}(3-\lambda^{2})}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{C_{\text{in}}(1-\lambda)\left[(-6-3\lambda+\lambda^{2})+C_{\text{in}}^{2}(8+5\lambda-\lambda^{2})\right]}{8\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+\text{e.s.e.}}

(719)

We do not actually use the
N_{C}^{-3}
term for anything in this paper, so in Appendix
G
, we use the simplified formula

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(0)}=\frac{C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}(3-\lambda^{2})}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)}.

(720)

This completes the proof of the
\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(0)}
formula that we stated in Lemma
21
. The curious reader may be interested to know that the computation of this third moment was the breaking point that finally compelled the first author of this paper to swallow his pride and use Mathematica.

Finally, we proceed to the fourth moment. We begin by tabulating all the up-down-middle paths of length
4
and net zero Hamming weight change, along with their respective contributions. Now the number of paths is considerably larger. They can be organized as follows:
1
path with four M’s; 12 paths with one U, one D, and two M’s; and
6
paths with two U’s and two D’s. Here is the full list:

\displaystyle(M,M,M,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(N_{C}-2k)^{4}C_{\text{in}}^{4}

(721)

\displaystyle(M,M,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2k)^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(722)

\displaystyle(M,M,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2k)^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(723)

\displaystyle(U,D,M,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2k)^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(724)

\displaystyle(D,U,M,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2k)^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(725)

\displaystyle(M,U,D,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2k)^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(726)

\displaystyle(M,D,U,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2k)^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(727)

\displaystyle(M,U,M,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2k)(N_{C}-2(k+1))C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(728)

\displaystyle(M,D,M,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2(k-1))(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(729)

\displaystyle(U,M,M,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2(k+1))^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(730)

\displaystyle(D,M,M,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2(k-1))^{2}C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(731)

\displaystyle(U,M,D,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(N_{C}-2k)(N_{C}-2(k+1))C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(732)

\displaystyle(D,M,U,M):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(N_{C}-2(k-1))(N_{C}-2k)C_{\text{in}}^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{2}

(733)

\displaystyle(U,U,D,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)(k+2)(N_{C}-k-1)S_{\text{in}}^{4}

(734)

\displaystyle(U,D,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{4}

(735)

\displaystyle(U,D,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(k+1)(N_{C}-k)S_{\text{in}}^{4}

(736)

\displaystyle(D,U,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(k+1)(N_{C}-k)S_{\text{in}}^{4}

(737)

\displaystyle(D,U,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{2}S_{\text{in}}^{4}

(738)

\displaystyle(D,D,U,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)S_{\text{in}}^{4}

(739)

When we add these quantities together, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{4}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{4}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}

(740)

\displaystyle=\Big\{10k^{2}+6k^{4}-2N_{C}-10kN_{C}+6k^{2}N_{C}-12k^{3}N_{C}

(741)

\displaystyle\quad+3N_{C}^{2}+6kN_{C}^{2}+6k^{2}N_{C}^{2}\Big\}

(742)

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\text{in}}^{2}\Big\{-60k^{2}-60k^{4}+8N_{C}+60kN_{C}+36k^{2}N_{C}+120k^{3}N_{C}

(743)

\displaystyle\quad-14N_{C}^{2}-36kN_{C}^{2}-72k^{2}N_{C}^{2}+6N_{C}^{3}+12kN_{C}^{3}\Big\}

(744)

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\text{in}}^{4}\Big\{50k^{2}+70k^{4}-6N_{C}-50kN_{C}-30k^{2}N_{C}-140k^{3}N_{C}

(745)

\displaystyle\quad+11N_{C}^{2}+30kN_{C}^{2}+90k^{2}N_{C}^{2}-6N_{C}^{3}-20kN_{C}^{3}+N_{C}^{4}\Big\}.

(746)

We now plug this quantity into our infinite summation:

\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(0)}=(-2N_{C})^{4}\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}\mathcal{T}_{4}^{(0)}.

(747)

After making the usual approximations and invoking the formulas for
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k^{p}x^{k}
for
p=0,1,2,3,4
, we eventually obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\text{in}}^{4}}{16}+\frac{C_{\text{in}}^{2}\left[3+C_{\text{in}}^{2}(-5+2\lambda)\right]}{8\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}

(748)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{3+2C_{\text{in}}^{2}(-18+9\lambda+2\lambda^{2})+C_{\text{in}}^{4}(45-30\lambda-4\lambda^{2})}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}

(749)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(-9+6\lambda+\lambda^{2})+2C_{\text{in}}^{2}(45-36\lambda-9\lambda^{2}+4\lambda^{3})+C_{\text{in}}^{4}(-105+90\lambda+25\lambda^{2}-16\lambda^{3})}{16\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}

(750)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(1-\lambda)\left[(9-\lambda^{2})+30C_{\text{in}}^{2}(-3+\lambda^{2})+15C_{\text{in}}^{4}(7-3\lambda^{2})\right]}{16\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{4}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(751)

Finally, we wish to relate the uncentered moment
\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(0)}
to the centered moment
\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(0)}
. We can do so using the following formula:

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mathbb{E}[X])^{4}\right]

(752)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X^{4}]-4\mathbb{E}[X^{3}]\mathbb{E}[X]+6\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}-4\mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[X]^{3}+\mathbb{E}[X]^{4}

(753)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[X^{4}]-4\mathbb{E}[X^{3}]\mathbb{E}[X]+6\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}-3\mathbb{E}[X]^{4},

(754)

which in turn implies that

\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(0)}=\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(0)}-4\mathcal{U}_{3}^{(0)}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}+6\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(0)}\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}\right)^{2}-3\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{(0)}\right)^{4}.

(755)

Plugging in the values that we have already computed yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3S_{\text{in}}^{4}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{2}\left[(-9+6\lambda+\lambda^{2})+3C_{\text{in}}^{2}(9-2\lambda-5\lambda^{2})\right]}{16\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}

(756a)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(1-\lambda)\left[(9-\lambda^{2})+4C_{\text{in}}^{2}(-15-6\lambda+5\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{3})+3C_{\text{in}}^{4}(20+11\lambda-8\lambda^{2}-3\lambda^{3})\right]}{16\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{4}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(756b)

We do not actually use the
N_{C}^{-3}
and
N_{C}^{-4}
terms for anything in this paper, so in Appendix
G
, we use the simplified formula

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(0)}=\frac{3S_{\text{in}}^{4}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)}.

(757)

This completes the proof of the
\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(0)}
formula that we stated in Lemma
21
. At this level of precision, the formula can actually be understood directly from the Gaussian approximation for the
P_{w,w}
values.

K.4
Moments of the
P_{w-1,w}
Values (Offset
\alpha=1
) in the Equatorial Case

Our second main result shows the centered moments for offset
\alpha=1
in the equatorial case, up to
\Theta(N_{C}^{-3})
precision:

Lemma 22

(Centered moments of the
P_{w-1,w}
values in the equatorial case)
.

In the equatorial special case, the even centered moments of the
P_{w-1,w}
values take the following values up to
\Theta\left(N^{-3}\right)
precision:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=1+\left(-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{-3+4\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-9+12\lambda-10\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(758)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{-3+2\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(-\frac{3+4\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(759)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-21+12\lambda+2\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(760)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{15}{64\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(761)

It is worth highlighting some consistent patterns in these formulas:

•

The leading-order asymptotic of
\mathcal{M}_{2p}^{(1)}
is given by
\mathcal{M}_{2p}^{(1)}\sim\frac{(2p-1)!!}{(4\lambda)^{p}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{p}}
. This is a direct consequence of the fact that the “distribution” of the
P_{w-1,w}
values tends to a Gaussian with normalization
\sim 1
and variance
\sim\frac{1}{4\lambda N_{C}}
.

•

In general, each coefficient of
N_{C}^{-p}
is a rational function of
\lambda
, where the denominator is a power of
2
times
\lambda^{p}
, and the numerator is an integer-coefficient polynomial in
\lambda
of degree at most
p-1
.

•

The coefficient of
N_{C}^{-p}
always has
\lambda^{p}
in the denominator. This is a result of the fact that the quantity
\lambda N_{C}\sim\lambda^{2}N
is an indicator of whether you should consider yourself to be in the “large
N
” asymptotic regime for this problem.

Let us now explain how we derive these formulas. The strategy we show here for the equatorial case will also apply to the general case, but it will become noticeably more complicated, which is why we first show it in this simpler setting. In Appendix
K
K.5
, we will explain how this strategy has to be adjusted for the general case.

First, define the total spin operator in the
\hat{n}
direction:

S_{\hat{n}}\coloneqq 2J_{\hat{n}}=\sum_{j=1}^{N_{C}}\left(\hat{n}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\right)_{j}.

(762)

The central idea is that we will use two different methods to evaluate the quantity

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\right].

(763)

For the first evaluation method, let us see how
S_{\hat{n}}
acts on a symmetrized Hamming weight state in the computational basis:

\displaystyle S_{\hat{n}}\ket{w^{(s)}}

\displaystyle=S_{\text{in}}\sqrt{(w+1)(N_{C}-w)}\ket{(w+1)^{(s)}}

(764)

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\text{in}}(N_{C}-2w)\ket{w^{(s)}}

(765)

\displaystyle\quad+S_{\text{in}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\ket{(w-1)^{(s)}}.

(766)

In the equatorial case
\hat{n}=\hat{x}
, this simplifies to

S_{x}\ket{w^{(s)}}=\sqrt{(w+1)(N_{C}-w)}\ket{(w+1)^{(s)}}+\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\ket{(w-1)^{(s)}}.

(767)

Furthermore, the operator
S_{z}^{p}
acts in a predictable way on such states:

S_{z}^{p}\ket{w^{(s)}}=(N_{C}-2w)^{p}\ket{w^{(s)}}.

(768)

Therefore, if we evaluate the trace of interest by expanding it in the symmetrized computational basis, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\quad\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{p}S_{x}\right]

(769)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\bra{w^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{p}S_{x}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(770)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{p}+(N_{C}-2w)^{p}\right]\bra{(w-1)^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(771)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{p}+(N_{C}-2w)^{p}\right]P_{w-1,w}.

(772)

We now define for convenience

z\coloneqq\frac{1}{N_{C}+1}\left[\left(w-\frac{1}{2}\right)-\frac{N_{C}}{2}\right]=\frac{w}{N_{C}+1}-\frac{1}{2}.

(773)

The result is that the quantity

(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{p}+(N_{C}-2w)^{p}=[-2(N_{C}+1)z+1]^{p}+[-2(N_{C}+1)z-1]^{p}

(774)

is some degree-
p
polynomial in
z
that only has terms of degree matching the parity of
p
. Furthermore, using a Taylor series, the quantity
\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}
can be written as a power series in
z
. Therefore, the second term in the above quantity can be evaluated as some infinite series with higher and higher centered moments
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}
. We can then put everything together to get a result for this trace.

Now we must evaluate this same trace in a different way. Note that
S_{\hat{n}}
acts as follows on the symmetrized Hamming weight states with direction
\hat{n}
:

S_{\hat{n}}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}=(2k-N_{C})\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}.

(775)

Therefore, we can compute the trace of interest as follows:

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\right]=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}(2k-N_{C})\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{p}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}.

(776)

We already know how to compute
\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{p}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
using “up-down-middle” paths of length
p
, as shown in Appendix
K
K.3
. (In fact, as we explained there, in the equatorial case where
\hat{n}=\hat{x}
, the “middle” option closes, so we actually just need to consider “up-down” paths of length
p
. This also implies that the result is trivially zero for all odd
p
.) The result will be some polynomial in
N_{C}
. We can then plug that polynomial into the summation, and use the usual approximations where we invert the summation index as
k\mapsto N_{C}-k
and then let the new summation index
k
go to infinity instead of stopping at
N_{C}
. Using standard manipulations of the infinite geometric series, these quantities can all be evaluated up to exponentially small error.

We can now compare our two different ways of evaluating
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{p}S_{x}\right]
. On one hand, we wrote it as an infinite series of decaying moments. On the other hand, we evaluated it up to exponentially small error using “up-down” paths. In general, to get the moments up to
\Theta(N_{C}^{-p})
precision, you need to evaluate this trace for all
0\leq q\leq p
and put together the results, because in any one of these results, the
\Theta(N_{C}^{-q})
contributions from different moments
\mathcal{M}_{r}^{(1)}
will collide, so you need to solve for them by collecting multiple equations.

This is all fairly involved, but it will become clearer once we actually go through the calculations. To this end, let us begin with
p=0
. Using the first method of evaluation, the trace we are interested in is

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\bra{w^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(777)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\bra{(w-1)^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(778)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}P_{w-1,w}.

(779)

We can expand the square root as

\displaystyle\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{C}+1}{2}\sqrt{1-4z^{2}}

(780)

\displaystyle=\frac{N_{C}+1}{2}\left[1-2z^{2}-2z^{4}-4z^{6}+\cdots\right],

(781)

where we used the Taylor series

\sqrt{1+\varepsilon}=1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{8}+\frac{\varepsilon^{3}}{16}+\cdots

(782)

We now define the adjusted moments as follows. They are actually the same as the centered moments in the equatorial case, but we use powers of
(N_{C}+1)
in the denominator instead of powers of
N_{C}
:

\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(1)}\coloneqq(N_{C}+1)^{-p}\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\left(w-\frac{N_{C}+1}{2}\right)^{p}P_{w-1,w}=\left(\frac{N_{C}}{N_{C}+1}\right)^{p}\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}.

(783)

In fact, it is nicer to derive most quantities in this subsection as power series in
(N_{C}+1)
or
(N_{C}+1)^{-1}
, which is why we will do so from this point forward. Only at the end will we convert our final results into power series in
N_{C}^{-1}
, for the sake of consistency with the presentation of all other results throughout this paper.

Using this definition, and using the Taylor series above, we can express the trace as an infinite series of moments:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\bra{w^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(784)

\displaystyle=(N_{C}+1)\left[\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}+\cdots\right].

(785)

Due to this result, we define a new quantity that will encode this infinite series of adjusted moments:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2p}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-4)^{k}\binom{1/2}{k}\mathcal{U}_{2(p+k)}^{(1)}

(786)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}_{2p}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2(p+1)}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2(p+2)}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{2(p+3)}^{(1)}+\cdots.

(787)

In particular, the coefficient of
\mathcal{U}_{2(p+k)}^{(1)}
is always the same as the coefficient of
z^{k}
in the Taylor series for
\sqrt{1-4z^{2}}
centered at
z=0
.

The evaluation of
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\right]
only involves
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
, but as we will see later, the evaluation of
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2p}S_{x}\right]
will involve
\mathcal{V}_{2q}^{(1)}
for all
0\leq q\leq p
. This is why we define
\mathcal{V}_{2p}^{(1)}
for all integers
p\geq 0
.

Now we turn to the second method of evaluation, which tells us that the trace we are interested in is

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\right]=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}(2k-N_{C})c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}.

(788)

In particular, we need to use the summation

\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}(2k-N_{C})c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}=\frac{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}}{\lambda^{2}}\left[\lambda(N_{C}+1)-1+\text{e.s.e.}\right]

(789)

to eventually obtain the result

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\right]=(N_{C}+1)-\frac{1}{\lambda}+\text{e.s.e.}

(790)

Now that we have evaluated
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{x}\right]
in two different ways, we can equate these two results. This gives us a formula for the quantity
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
, which encodes information about the moments of the
P_{w-1,w}
values:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}+\cdots

(791a)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}+1}+\text{e.s.e.}

(791b)

It may not be immediately clear what we can get from this. However, the concentration phenomenon means that
\mathcal{U}_{2p}^{(\alpha)}=\Theta((N_{C}+1)^{-p})
. Therefore, when we match the
\Theta(1)
terms on both sides of the above equation, we obtain

\boxed{\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}=1+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-1}\right)}.

(792)

In this way, we have found the
\Theta(1)
term in the adjusted moment
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
. In fact, if we also match the
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-1}\right)
terms on both sides, we get

\boxed{\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}=1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}+1}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-2}\right)}.

(793)

In this sense, we also have some information about the
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-1}\right)
terms in both
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}
. However, we will need a second equation to fully solve for them.

This is the part where continuing this process further gives us more information. When we proceed to
p=2
, meaning that we evaluate
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]
, we will obtain another equation. That equation will also have the
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-1}\right)
terms of
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}
mixed together. But now that we have two different equations relating the
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-1}\right)
terms of
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}
, we can solve for both of them. Similarly, when we proceed to
p=4
, we can solve for the
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-2}\right)
terms of
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(2)}
. Using this process, we can eventually compute all the moments to whatever precision we desire, and Lemma
22
is the result.

Let us proceed to
p=2
now. Using the first method of evaluation, the trace of interest is

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{2}+(N_{C}-2w)^{2}\right]P_{w-1,w}.

(794)

The polynomial in the summation is

(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{2}+(N_{C}-2w)^{2}=2\left[4(N_{C}+1)^{2}z^{2}+1\right].

(795)

Using this and the previously derived expression for
\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}
as a Taylor series in
z
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{2}+(N_{C}-2w)^{2}\right]

(796)

\displaystyle=\left[4(N_{C}+1)^{3}z^{2}+(N_{C}+1)\right]\left[1-2z^{2}-2z^{4}-4z^{6}+\cdots\right]

(797)

\displaystyle=4(N_{C}+1)^{3}\left[z^{2}-2z^{4}-2z^{6}-4z^{8}+\cdots\right]

(798)

\displaystyle\quad+(N_{C}+1)\left[1-2z^{2}-2z^{4}-4z^{6}+\cdots\right].

(799)

Therefore, the trace of interest is

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]=4(N_{C}+1)^{3}

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{8}^{(1)}+\cdots\right],

(800)

which can be rewritten succinctly as

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]=4(N_{C}+1)^{3}\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}+(N_{C}+1)\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}.

(801)

As you can see, whereas
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]
only involved
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
,
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]
involves both
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}
. When we continue the pattern, we will find that
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2p}S_{x}\right]
involves
\mathcal{V}_{2q}^{(1)}
for all
0\leq q\leq p
. This is why it made sense earlier to define
\mathcal{V}_{2p}^{(1)}
for all
p\geq 0
.

Now we evaluate the trace of interest using the second method. We begin as follows:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\left[S_{x}\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}\right]

(802)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N-k}(2k-N_{C})\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{2}\right]

(803)

We can evaluate
\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{2}\right]
using “up-down” paths of length
2
. In this case, there are just
2
possible paths, which have the following contributions:

\displaystyle(U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad(k+1)(N_{C}-k)

(804)

\displaystyle(D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad k(N_{C}-k+1)

(805)

Adding these quantities together yields

\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{2}\right]=-2k^{2}+N_{C}+2kN_{C}.

(806)

Also, as always, we will make frequent use of the approximation

\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}=\frac{\lambda}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}}\left(1+\text{e.s.e.}\right).

(807)

Putting this all together yields

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]=\frac{\lambda}{c_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{c_{0}}{c_{1}}\right)^{k}(N_{C}-2k)(-2k^{2}+N_{C}+2kN_{C})+\text{e.s.e.}

(808)

We now employ the same set of tricks that served us back in Appendix
K
K.3
. In particular, we extend the summation to
k=\infty
(which only imposes an exponentially small error) and use the formulas for
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k^{q}x^{k}
for
q=0,1,2
, which can be obtained using standard manipulations of the infinite geometric series. The end result is

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]=\frac{1}{\lambda}(N_{C}+1)^{2}+\frac{-3+\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}(N_{C}+1)+\frac{3-2\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{3}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(809)

Therefore, equating our two different expressions for
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{2}S_{x}\right]
, and using the fact that we have already solved for
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
, we can solve for
\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}+\cdots

(810a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}+1}-\frac{3}{4\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{2}}+\frac{3-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(810b)

At this point, we now have enough information to solve for the
\Theta\left(N_{C}+1)^{-1}\right)
terms in both
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}
. However, we will actually save this task for later, because as we will show at the very end, once we compute
\mathcal{V}_{2q}^{(1)}
for all
0\leq q\leq p
up to exponentially small error, there is a more clever way to solve for
\mathcal{U}_{2q}^{(1)}
for all
0\leq q\leq p
up to
