\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-p}\right)
precision.

Let us proceed to
p=4
now. Using the first method of evaluation, the trace of interest is

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{4}+(N_{C}-2w)^{4}\right]P_{w-1,w}.

(811)

The polynomial in the summation is

(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{4}+(N_{C}-2w)^{4}=2\left[16(N_{C}+1)^{4}z^{4}+24(N_{C}+1)^{2}z^{2}+1\right].

(812)

Using this and the previously derived expression for
\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}
as a Taylor series in
z
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{4}+(N_{C}-2w)^{4}\right]

(813)

\displaystyle=\left[16(N_{C}+1)^{5}z^{4}+24(N_{C}+1)^{3}z^{2}+(N_{C}+1)\right]\left[1-2z^{2}-2z^{4}-4z^{6}+\cdots\right]

(814)

\displaystyle=16(N_{C}+1)^{5}\left[z^{4}-2z^{6}-2z^{8}-4z^{10}+\cdots\right]

(815)

\displaystyle\quad+24(N_{C}+1)^{3}\left[z^{2}-2z^{4}-2z^{6}-4z^{8}+\cdots\right]

(816)

\displaystyle\quad+(N_{C}+1)\left[1-2z^{2}-2z^{4}-4z^{6}+\cdots\right].

(817)

Therefore, the trace of interest is

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}S_{x}\right]=16(N_{C}+1)^{5}

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{10}^{(1)}+\cdots\right]

(818)

\displaystyle+24(N_{C}+1)^{3}

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{8}^{(1)}+\cdots\right]

(819)

\displaystyle+(N_{C}+1)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}+\cdots\right],

(820)

which can be rewritten succinctly as

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}S_{x}\right]=16(N_{C}+1)^{5}\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(0)}+24(N_{C}+1)^{3}\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}+(N_{C}+1)\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}.

(821)

Now we evaluate the trace of interest using the second method. Just as before, we begin as follows:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\left[S_{x}\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}\right]

(822)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N-k}(2k-N_{C})\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{4}\right].

(823)

We can evaluate
\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{4}\right]
using “up-down” paths of length
4
. In this case, there are
\binom{4}{2}=6
possible paths, which have the following contributions:

\displaystyle(U,U,D,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)][(k+2)(N_{C}-k-1)]

(824)

\displaystyle(U,D,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{2}

(825)

\displaystyle(U,D,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]

(826)

\displaystyle(D,U,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]

(827)

\displaystyle(D,U,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{2}

(828)

\displaystyle(D,D,U,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)][k(N_{C}-k+1)]

(829)

Adding these quantities together yields

\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{4}\right]=10k^{2}+6k^{4}-2N_{C}-10kN_{C}-6k^{2}N_{C}-12k^{3}N_{C}+3N_{C}^{2}+6kN_{C}^{2}+6k^{2}N_{C}^{2}.

(830)

Combining this with the usual approximation for the prefactor yields

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{c_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(N_{C}-2k)(10k^{2}+6k^{4}-2N_{C}-10kN_{C}

(831)

\displaystyle\quad-6k^{2}N_{C}-12k^{3}N_{C}+3N_{C}^{2}+6kN_{C}^{2}+6k^{2}N_{C}^{2})+\text{e.s.e.}

(832)

By extending the summation to
k=\infty
and invoking the formulas for
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k^{q}x^{k}
for integers
0\leq q\leq 4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{\lambda^{2}}(N_{C}+1)^{3}+\frac{-18+7\lambda^{2}}{\lambda^{3}}(N_{C}+1)^{2}

(833)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{45-33\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{4}}{\lambda^{4}}(N_{C}+1)+\frac{-45+48\lambda^{2}-8\lambda^{4}}{\lambda^{5}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(834)

Therefore, equating our two different expressions for
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{4}S_{x}\right]
, and using the fact that we have already solved for
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}
, we can solve for
\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{10}^{(1)}+\cdots

(835a)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{2}}+\frac{-18+\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}

(835b)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{15(3-\lambda^{2})}{16\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{4}}+\frac{-45+30\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{4}}{16\lambda^{5}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{5}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(835c)

Finally, we proceed to
p=6
. Using the first method of evaluation, the trace of interest is

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{6}+(N_{C}-2w)^{6}\right]P_{w-1,w}.

(836)

The polynomial in the summation is

(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{6}+(N_{C}-2w)^{6}=2\left[64(N_{C}+1)^{6}z^{6}+240(N_{C}+1)^{4}z^{4}+60(N_{C}+1)^{2}z^{2}+1\right].

(837)

Using this and the previously derived expression for
\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}
as a Taylor series in
z
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1))^{6}+(N_{C}-2w)^{6}\right]

(838)

\displaystyle=\left[64(N_{C}+1)^{6}z^{6}+240(N_{C}+1)^{4}z^{4}+60(N_{C}+1)^{2}z^{2}+1\right]\left[1-2z^{2}-2z^{4}-4z^{6}+\cdots\right]

(839)

\displaystyle=64(N_{C}+1)^{7}\left[z^{6}-2z^{8}-2z^{10}-4z^{12}+\cdots\right]

(840)

\displaystyle\quad+240(N_{C}+1)^{5}\left[z^{4}-2z^{6}-2z^{8}-4z^{10}+\cdots\right]

(841)

\displaystyle\quad+60(N_{C}+1)^{3}\left[z^{2}-2z^{4}-2z^{6}-4z^{8}+\cdots\right]

(842)

\displaystyle\quad+(N_{C}+1)\left[1-2z^{2}-2z^{4}-4z^{6}+\cdots\right].

(843)

Therefore, the trace of interest is

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}S_{x}\right]=64(N_{C}+1)^{7}

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{10}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{12}^{(1)}+\cdots\right]

(844)

\displaystyle+240(N_{C}+1)^{5}

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{10}^{(1)}+\cdots\right]

(845)

\displaystyle+60(N_{C}+1)^{3}

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{8}^{(1)}+\cdots\right]

(846)

\displaystyle+(N_{C}+1)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}+\cdots\right],

(847)

which can be rewritten succinctly as

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}S_{x}\right]=64(N_{C}+1)^{7}\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+240(N_{C}+1)^{5}\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(0)}+60(N_{C}+1)^{3}\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}+(N_{C}+1)\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}.

(848)

Now we evaluate the trace of interest using the second method. As always, we begin as follows:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\left[S_{x}\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}\right]

(849)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N-k}(2k-N_{C})\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{6}\right].

(850)

We can evaluate
\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{6}\right]
using “up-down” paths of length
4
. In this case, there are
\binom{6}{3}=20
possible paths, which have the following contributions:

\displaystyle(U,U,U,D,D,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)][(k+2)(N_{C}-k-1)][(k+3)(N_{C}-k-2)]

(851)

\displaystyle(U,U,D,U,D,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)][(k+2)(N_{C}-k-2)]^{2}

(852)

\displaystyle(U,D,U,U,D,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{2}[(k+2)(N_{C}-k-2)]

(853)

\displaystyle(D,U,U,U,D,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)][(k+2)(N_{C}-k-2)]

(854)

\displaystyle(U,U,D,D,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{2}[(k+2)(N_{C}-k-2)]

(855)

\displaystyle(U,D,U,D,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{3}

(856)

\displaystyle(D,U,U,D,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{2}

(857)

\displaystyle(U,D,D,U,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{2}

(858)

\displaystyle(D,U,D,U,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{2}[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]

(859)

\displaystyle(D,D,U,U,U,D):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)][k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]

(860)

\displaystyle(U,U,D,D,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)][(k+2)(N_{C}-k-1)]

(861)

\displaystyle(U,D,U,D,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]^{2}

(862)

\displaystyle(D,U,U,D,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{2}[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]

(863)

\displaystyle(U,D,D,U,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{2}[(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]

(864)

\displaystyle(D,U,D,U,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{3}

(865)

\displaystyle(D,D,U,U,D,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)][k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{2}

(866)

\displaystyle(U,D,D,D,U,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)][k(N_{C}-k+1)][(k+1)(N_{C}-k)]

(867)

\displaystyle(D,U,D,D,U,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)][k(N_{C}-k+1)]^{2}

(868)

\displaystyle(D,D,U,D,U,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)]^{2}[k(N_{C}-k+1)]

(869)

\displaystyle(D,D,D,U,U,U):

\displaystyle\quad\quad[(k-2)(N_{C}-k+3)][(k-1)(N_{C}-k+2)][k(N_{C}-k+1)]

(870)

Adding these quantities together yields

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\ket{k_{X}^{(s)}}\bra{k_{X}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{6}\right]

\displaystyle=-112k^{2}-140k^{4}-20k^{6}

(871)

\displaystyle\quad+16N_{C}+112kN_{C}+90k^{2}N_{C}+280k^{3}N_{C}+30k^{4}N_{C}+60k^{5}N_{C}

(872)

\displaystyle\quad-30N_{C}^{2}-90kN_{C}^{2}-180k^{2}N_{C}^{2}-60k^{3}N_{C}^{2}-60k^{4}N_{C}^{2}

(873)

\displaystyle\quad+15N_{C}^{3}+40kN_{C}^{3}+30k^{2}N_{C}^{3}+20k^{3}N_{C}^{3}.

(874)

Combining this with the usual approximation for the prefactor yields

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda}{c_{1}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(N_{C}-2k)(-112k^{2}-140k^{4}-20k^{6}

(875)

\displaystyle\quad+16N_{C}+112kN_{C}+90k^{2}N_{C}+280k^{3}N_{C}+30k^{4}N_{C}+60k^{5}N_{C}

(876)

\displaystyle\quad-30N_{C}^{2}-90kN_{C}^{2}-180k^{2}N_{C}^{2}-60k^{3}N_{C}^{2}-60k^{4}N_{C}^{2}

(877)

\displaystyle\quad+15N_{C}^{3}+40kN_{C}^{3}+30k^{2}N_{C}^{3}+20k^{3}N_{C}^{3})+\text{e.s.e.}

(878)

By extending the summation to
k=\infty
and invoking the formulas for
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}k^{q}x^{k}
for integers
0\leq q\leq 6
, we obtain

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}S_{x}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{15}{\lambda^{3}}(N_{C}+1)^{4}+\frac{30(-5+2\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}(N_{C}+1)^{3}+\frac{675-510\lambda^{2}+31\lambda^{4}}{\lambda^{5}}(N_{C}+1)^{2}

(879)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{-1575+1725\lambda^{2}-333\lambda^{4}+\lambda^{6}}{\lambda^{6}}(N_{C}+1)+\frac{1575-2250\lambda^{2}+768\lambda^{4}-32\lambda^{6}}{\lambda^{7}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(880)

Therefore, equating our two different expressions for
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{6}S_{x}\right]
and invoking the previously derived results for
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}
, and
\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}
, we can solve for
\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{10}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{12}^{(1)}+\cdots

(881a)

\displaystyle=\frac{15}{64\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}+\frac{15(-10+\lambda^{2})}{64\lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{4}}

(881b)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{675-240\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{4}}{64\lambda^{5}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{5}}+\frac{21(-75+50\lambda^{2}-3\lambda^{4})}{64\lambda^{6}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{6}}

(881c)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1575-1575\lambda^{2}+273\lambda^{4}-\lambda^{6}}{64\lambda^{7}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{7}}+\text{e.s.e.}

(881d)

Now that we have computed all the
\mathcal{V}_{2p}^{(1)}
quantities that are relevant at
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-3}\right)
precision, we will explain how to convert them into the adjusted moments
\mathcal{U}_{2p}^{(1)}
up to
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-3}\right)
precision. Recall that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}+\cdots

(882)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}+\cdots

(883)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{10}^{(1)}+\cdots

(884)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{8}^{(1)}-2\mathcal{U}_{10}^{(1)}-4\mathcal{U}_{12}^{(1)}+\cdots

(885)

These equations can be inverted to solve for the adjusted moments
\mathcal{U}_{2p}^{(1)}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}+2\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}+6\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}+20\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+\cdots

(886)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}+2\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}+6\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+20\mathcal{V}_{8}^{(1)}+\cdots

(887)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}+2\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+6\mathcal{V}_{8}^{(1)}+20\mathcal{V}_{10}^{(1)}+\cdots

(888)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+2\mathcal{V}_{8}^{(1)}+6\mathcal{V}_{10}^{(1)}+20\mathcal{V}_{12}^{(1)}+\cdots

(889)

For example, to solve for
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
, start with
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
, which has the desired
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
as its first term. Then add a suitably chosen multiple of
\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}
to eliminate the presence of
\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}
. Since
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
contains
-2\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}
, we need to add
2\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}
. Then add a suitably chosen multiple of
\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}
to eliminate the presence of
\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}
. Since
\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}
contains
-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}
contains
-2\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}
, we need to add
6\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}
. We can repeat this process as many times as needed to write
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}
as an infinite series in terms of the
\mathcal{V}_{2p}^{(1)}
quantities.

In our case, because
\mathcal{V}_{2p}^{(1)}=O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-p}\right)
, the relevant expressions up to
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-3}\right)
precision are as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{0}^{(1)}+2\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}+6\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}+20\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)

(890)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{2}^{(1)}+2\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}+6\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)

(891)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{4}^{(1)}+2\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)

(892)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{6}^{(1)}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right).

(893)

After plugging our solutions for
\mathcal{V}_{2p}^{(1)}
for
q=0,1,2,3
into the above equations, we obtain the adjusted moments
\mathcal{U}_{2p}^{(1)}
for
p=0,1,2,3
up to
\Theta\left((N_{C}+1)^{-3}\right)
precision, exactly as desired:

\boxed{\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}=\frac{15}{64\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)}

(894)

\boxed{\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}=\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{2}}+\frac{-21+2\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)}

(895)

\boxed{\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}+1}-\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{2}}-\frac{3+4\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)}

(896)

\boxed{\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}=1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}+1}-\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{2}}-\frac{9+2\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)}.

(897)

Our final task is to convert the adjusted moments
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(1)}
into the desired centered moments
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}
. (As a reminder, in the equatorial case, we have
\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(1)}=\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}=0
for odd
p
, so we do not need to worry about those.) The formula relating the two is very simple:

\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}=\left(\frac{N_{C}+1}{N_{C}}\right)^{p}\mathcal{U}_{p}^{(1)}

(898)

So all we need to do is to apply this formula and also rewrite the powers of
(N_{C}+1)
in terms of powers of
N_{C}
as needed. We begin with
p=6
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{N_{C}+1}{N_{C}}\right)^{6}\mathcal{U}_{6}^{(1)}

(899)

\displaystyle=\frac{15}{64\lambda^{3}}\frac{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}{N_{C}^{6}}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{2}N_{C}^{-6}\right)

(900)

\displaystyle=\frac{15}{64\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(901)

We now handle
p=4
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{N_{C}+1}{N_{C}}\right)^{4}\mathcal{U}_{4}^{(1)}

(902)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{(N_{C}+1)^{2}}{N_{C}^{4}}+\frac{-21+2\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{(N_{C}+1)}{N_{C}^{4}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(903)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)\right]+\left[\frac{-21+2\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)\right]+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(904)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{-21+12\lambda+2\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(905)

Next, we move on to
p=2
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{N_{C}+1}{N_{C}}\right)^{2}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{(1)}

(906)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{(N_{C}+1)}{N_{C}^{2}}-\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}-\frac{3+4\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}+1)}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-2}N_{C}^{-2}\right)

(907)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}\right]+\left[-\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}\right]+\left[-\frac{3+4\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)\right]+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(908)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\frac{-3+2\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}-\frac{3+4\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(909)

Finally, we tackle
p=0
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}_{0}^{(1)}

(910)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}+1}+\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{2}}-\frac{9+2\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{(N_{C}+1)^{3}}+O\left((N_{C}+1)^{-4}\right)

(911)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)\right]-\frac{3}{8\lambda^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}-\frac{2}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)\right]

(912)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{9+2\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)\right]+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)

(913)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\frac{-3+4\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\frac{-9+12\lambda-10\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(914)

Let us write out our final results for these centered moments, just for cleanliness:

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{6}^{(1)}=\left(\frac{15}{64\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)}

(915)

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(1)}=\left(\frac{3}{16\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-21+12\lambda+2\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)}

(916)

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}=\left(\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{-3+2\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(-\frac{3+4\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)}

(917)

\boxed{\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}=1+\left(-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{-3+4\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-9+12\lambda-10\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right)}.

(918)

These four equations agree precisely with the formulas stated in Lemma
22
, so the proof is complete.

K.5
Moments of the
P_{w-1,w}
Values (Offset
\alpha=1
) in the General Case

Our third and final main result shows the centered moments for offset
\alpha=1
in the general case, up to
\Theta(N_{C}^{-2})
precision:

Lemma 23

(Centered moments of the
P_{w-1,w}
values in the general case)
.

The centered moments for offset
\alpha=1
take the following values up to
\Theta(N_{C}^{-2})
precision:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2S_{\text{in}}^{2}\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(-\frac{C_{\text{in}}^{2}}{2S_{\text{in}}^{4}}+\frac{1}{2S_{\text{in}}^{2}\lambda}-\frac{3}{8S_{\text{in}}^{4}\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)

(919)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{1}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\text{in}}(1+\lambda^{2})}{4S_{\text{in}}^{2}\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)

(920)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}{4\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left[-\frac{1}{8}+\frac{(1-\lambda)\left(-1+\left(C_{\text{in}}^{2}-S_{\text{in}}^{2}\right)(2+\lambda)\right)}{8\lambda^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)

(921)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{3}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\text{in}}S_{\text{in}}^{2}(3-\lambda^{2})}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)

(922)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3S_{\text{in}}^{4}}{16\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right).

(923)

Once again, it is worth highlighting some consistent patterns in these formulas:

•

The leading-order asymptotic of each even moment
\mathcal{M}_{2p}^{(1)}
is given by
\mathcal{M}_{2p}^{(1)}\sim\frac{(2p-1)!!S_{\text{in}}^{2p}}{(4\lambda)^{p}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{p}}
. This is a direct consequence of the fact that the “distribution” of the
P_{w-1,w}
values tends to a Gaussian with normalization
\sim 1
and variance
\sim\frac{S_{\text{in}}^{2}}{4\lambda N_{C}}
.

•

Plugging in
C_{\text{in}}=0
and
S_{\text{in}}=1
immediately recovers the formulas in the equatorial special case as stated in Lemma
22
. This includes the fact that all the odd moments
\mathcal{M}_{2p+1}^{(1)}
vanish in this case.

•

In general, each coefficient of
N_{C}^{-p}
is a rational function of
\lambda
, where the denominator is a power of
2
times
\lambda^{p}
, and the numerator is a polynomial in
\lambda
of degree at most
p
(where the coefficients can depend on
\Theta_{\text{in}}
). Furthermore, in the equatorial case
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
, the degree of the numerator becomes at most
p-1
. (For example, the coefficient of
N_{C}^{-2}
in
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{(1)}
can be written as a rational function of
\lambda
with denominator
8\lambda^{2}
and numerator
(4C_{\text{in}}^{2}-3)+2S_{\text{in}}^{2}\lambda-2C_{\text{in}}^{2}\lambda^{2}
, which is quadratic in
\lambda
. Plugging in
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
makes the numerator reduce to
-3+2\lambda
, which is linear in
\lambda
.)

•

The coefficient of
N_{C}^{-p}
always has
\lambda^{p}
in the denominator. This is a result of the fact that the quantity
\lambda N_{C}\sim\lambda^{2}N
is an indicator of whether you should consider yourself to be in the “large
N
” asymptotic regime for this problem.

Let us now explain how we derive these formulas. Deriving these formulas was truly the most painful part of this work by far, which is why we forgo the full details. However, we at least explain the general strategy, which is very similar to what we showed in Appendix
K
K.4
, but with some slight complications.

In the equatorial case, we computed the trace

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}S_{z}^{p}S_{x}\right]

(924)

in two different ways. Here, we adjust this quantity to

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\right],

(925)

where
a
is some quantity that we will define later.

For the first evaluation method, we begin by observing how
S_{\hat{n}}
acts on a symmetrized Hamming weight state in the computational basis:

\displaystyle S_{\hat{n}}\ket{w^{(s)}}

\displaystyle=S_{\text{in}}\sqrt{(w+1)(N_{C}-w)}\ket{(w+1)^{(s)}}

(926)

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\text{in}}(N_{C}-2w)\ket{w^{(s)}}

(927)

\displaystyle\quad+S_{\text{in}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\ket{(w-1)^{(s)}}.

(928)

In the equatorial case, we did not have to worry about the middle term. Furthermore, the operator
(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})
acts in a predictable way on such states:

(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}\ket{w^{(s)}}=(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}\ket{w^{(s)}}.

(929)

Therefore, if we evaluate this trace by expanding it in the symmetrized computational basis, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle\quad\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\right]

(930)

\displaystyle=\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\bra{w^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(931)

\displaystyle=C_{\text{in}}\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}(N_{C}-2w)(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}\bra{w^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(932)

\displaystyle\quad+S_{\text{in}}\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1)-a)^{p}+(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}\right]\bra{(w-1)^{(s)}}\rho_{N_{C}/2}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(933)

\displaystyle=C_{\text{in}}\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\left(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p+1}+a(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}\right]P_{w,w}

(934)

\displaystyle\quad+S_{\text{in}}\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1)-a)^{p}+(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}\right]P_{w-1,w}

(935)

\displaystyle=C_{\text{in}}(-2N_{C})^{p}\left[(-2N_{C})\mathcal{M}_{p+1}^{(0)}+a\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(0)}\right]

(936)

\displaystyle\quad+S_{\text{in}}\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}\left[(N_{C}-2(w-1)-a)^{p}+(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}\right]P_{w-1,w}.

(937)

The first line in this final expression was not present in the equatorial case. Fortunately, it is written in terms of
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(0)}
values, which we already know how to evaluate from Appendix
K
K.3
. Evaluating the second line in this final expression will be similar to the equatorial case. The factor
(N_{C}-2(w-1)-a)^{p}+(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}
is some degree-
p
polynomial in
z
, and the factor
\sqrt{w(N_{C}-w+1)}
can be written as a Taylor series in
z
. Therefore, the second term in the above quantity can be evaluated as some infinite series with higher and higher centered moments
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}
. We can then put everything together to get a result for this trace as an infinite series of higher and higher centered moments
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}
. In the equatorial case, both of these quantities were even functions of
z
, but now this will not be the case, so the infinite series will be considerably more complicated.

For the second evaluation method, we proceed as follows:

\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\right]=\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{k=0}^{N_{C}}c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}(2k-N_{C})\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}.

(938)

Notice that

\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}=\sum_{l=0}^{p}\binom{p}{l}(-a)^{p-l}\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{l}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}},

(939)

where we know how to compute
\bra{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}S_{z}^{l}\ket{k_{\hat{n}}^{(s)}}
using “up-down-middle” paths of length
l
, as shown in Appendix
K
K.3
. In this case, we need to consider “up-down-middle” paths of every length
0\leq l\leq p
. The result will be some polynomial in
N_{C}
. We can then plug that polynomial into the summation, and use the usual approximations where we invert the summation index as
k\mapsto N_{C}-k
and then let the new summation index
k
go to infinity instead of stopping at
N_{C}
. These quantities can all be evaluated using standard manipulations of the infinite geometric series. The conclusion is that we know how to evaluate
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\right]
up to exponentially small error.

Since we need to solve for a greater number of quantities, we actually need to perform both evaluation methods above for two different values of
a
for each nonnegative integer
p
. It is now finally time to make smart choices for the value of
a
. The first convenient choice is
a=0
, because it makes the calculation using “up-down-middle” paths the easiest in the second evaluation method. In particular, we only need to consider “up-down-middle” paths of length
p
, as opposed to having to consider them for all lengths
0\leq l\leq p
. The second convenient choice of is
a=N_{C}(1-2\mu)
. The reason is that the degree-
p
polynomial in
z
that appears in the first evaluation method now simplifies as follows:

(N_{C}-2(w-1)-a)^{p}+(N_{C}-2w-a)^{p}=[-2(N_{C}+1)z+1]^{p}+[-2(N_{C}+1)z-1]^{p}.

(940)

In particular, it will now only have terms of degree matching the parity of
p
, thus simplifying the calculation somewhat.

We can now compare our two different ways of evaluating
\text{Tr}\left[\rho_{N_{C}/2}(S_{z}-a\mathbb{I})^{p}S_{\hat{n}}\right]
. On one hand, we wrote it as an infinite series of decaying moments. On the other hand, we evaluated it up to exponentially small error using “up-down-middle” paths. In general, to get the moments up to
\Theta(N_{C}^{-p})
precision, you need to evaluate this trace for all
0\leq q\leq p
and put together the results, because in any one of these results, the
\Theta(N_{C}^{-q})
contributions from different moments
\mathcal{M}_{r}^{(1)}
will collide, so you need to solve for them by collecting multiple equations. However, once all these calculations are done, we can (with great difficulty) extract the moments
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{(1)}
from them, and Lemma
23
is the result.

Appendix L
Dissipation of Purity of Coherence

We know that purity of coherence is a resource that cannot be created under TI channels, only destroyed. As such, when distilling coherence, we should closely watch how much of this valuable resource we waste. This raises a natural question: how much purity of coherence is “dissipated” by our optimal distillation protocol?

Given that the bound on fidelity imposed by monotonicity of purity of coherence is tight at the leading order, one might intuitively expect that purity of coherence is essentially conserved. This intuition turns out to be correct, but we will compute the loss of purity of coherence to slightly higher precision to allow us to observe at least some wastage. For simplicity, we will restrict this discussion to the equatorial special case
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
.

There is a very important subtlety that we must observe. Because purity of coherence and fidelity are both monotonically increasing functions of the purity parameter, maximizing the output qubit fidelity indeed maximizes the purity of coherence of the output state. However, while fidelity is a linear function of the purity parameter, purity of coherence is a nonlinear function of the purity parameter. As a result, the expected value of the purity of coherence is not just the purity of coherence of the average output state. Therefore, the average purity of coherence of the output state is a function of not only the quantum channel but also the specific realization of that quantum channel. In particular, post-selection plays a key role. If our output state is a weighted average of output states resulting from different measurement outcomes, then we retain more purity of coherence if we remember the measurement outcome, since purity of coherence is convex.

For the realization of our equatorial distillation protocol, we will consider two possibilities: one in which we keep track of the value
j
we obtained in the total angular momentum measurement as part of the initial Schur sampling step
Cirac et al. 1999
, and one in which we forget this value. We begin with the naive implementation, in which we forget this value:

Theorem 24

(Dissipation of purity of coherence, assuming naive implementation)
.

If one forgets the outcome of the angular momentum measurement, then the optimal
N\rightarrow 1
equatorial distillation protocol produces an average dissipation of purity of coherence of
6+O\left(N^{-1}\right)
.

Proof.

For convenience, we will define the quantity

\eta\equiv P_{H}(\rho)=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{1-\lambda^{2}}.

(941)

The purity of coherence of the input state is

P_{H_{\text{in}}}\left(\rho^{\otimes N}\right)=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{1-\lambda^{2}}N=\eta N.

(942)

We now compute the purity of coherence of the output state. We can naively evaluate it by taking the optimal purity parameter
\tilde{\lambda}
that we expressed as a power series in
1/N
and then passing that value through the purity-of-coherence function
f(x)=\frac{4x^{2}}{1-x^{2}}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{2}{\eta}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{6}{\eta^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)

(943)

\displaystyle\implies\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}^{2}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{4}{\eta}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{8}{\eta^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)

(944)

\displaystyle\therefore P_{H}\left(\rho\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}^{2}}{1-\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}^{2}}

(945)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\left[1-\frac{4}{\eta}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{8}{\eta^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]}{\frac{4}{\eta}\frac{1}{N}+\frac{8}{\eta^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)}

(946)

\displaystyle=\eta N\left[1-\frac{4}{\eta}\frac{1}{N}-\frac{8}{\eta^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}+O\left(N^{-3}\right)\right]\left[1-\frac{2}{\eta}\frac{1}{N}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]

(947)

\displaystyle=\eta N\left[1-\frac{6}{\eta}\frac{1}{N}+O\left(N^{-2}\right)\right]

(948)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{1-\lambda^{2}}N-6+O\left(N^{-1}\right).

(949)

The first term is in fact just the purity of coherence of the input state, which tells us that the optimal distillation protocol conserves purity of coherence at the leading order, as expected. Subtracting the above amount from the original purity of coherence, we see that the dissipated purity of coherence is
6+O\left(N^{-1}\right)
, as desired.

∎

The fact that there is nonzero purity of coherence wastage at the
\Theta(1)
order (which is the order immediately following the leading order), is directly tied to a fact that we previously observed, namely that the minimum infidelity fails to saturate the bound set by purity of coherence at the
\Theta(N^{-2})
order (which is again the order immediately following the leading order), as we showed in Appendix
G
G.3
.

However, one can object to this naive calculation. The optimal purity parameter
\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}
, when written in terms of
N
, is in fact an average over the different angular momentum outcomes. In other words, we average
\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})
over different outcomes
N_{C}=2j
to obtain
\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}
, and then pass this value through the purity-of-coherence function
f(x)=\frac{4x^{2}}{1-x^{2}}
. However, we always know what the angular momentum measurement outcome was, so when computing the average purity of coherence, we should not blindly average together the purity parameters resulting from the protocol following the angular momentum measurement. In particular,
f
is a convex function in
x
(this is a special case of the broader fact that purity of coherence is a convex resource). So by Jensen’s inequality,
\mathbb{E}[f(X)]\geq f\left(\mathbb{E}[X]\right)
. This means that we should actually take our values
\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})
that are written as a power series in
1/N_{C}
, pass these values through the purity-of-coherence function
f(x)=\frac{4x^{2}}{1-x^{2}}
, and then average those values together to get the average purity of coherence of the distilled output qubit. Assuming that we remember our angular momentum outcome (which of course we would), this is a more accurate reflection of the average purity of coherence that our distilled qubit contains.

Theorem 25

(Dissipation of purity of coherence, assuming angular momentum post-selection)
.

If one keeps track of the outcome of the angular momentum measurement but implements the post-Schur-sampling distillation protocol naively, then the optimal
N\rightarrow 1
equatorial distillation protocol produces an average dissipation of purity of coherence of
2+O\left(N^{-1}\right)
.

Proof.

We start with the power series for
\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})
, the optimal purity parameter for a specific angular momentum outcome, which we derived in a previous section:

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}(1+\lambda)(3-\lambda)}{8\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)

(950)

\displaystyle\implies\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})^{2}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1+\lambda)}{2\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right).

(951)

We now compute
P(N_{C})
, the purity of coherence for a specific total angular momentum outcome by passing this value through the purity-of-coherence function:

\displaystyle P(N_{C})

\displaystyle=f\left(\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})\right)

(952)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})^{2}}{1-\tilde{\lambda}_{\text{opt}}(N_{C})^{2}}

(953)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\left[1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1+\lambda)}{2\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)\right]}{\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\frac{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1+\lambda)}{2\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)}

(954)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda}{1-\lambda^{2}}N_{C}\left[1-\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{3}(1+\lambda)}{2\lambda^{2}}\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-3}\right)\right]\left[1-\frac{(1-\lambda)^{2}}{2\lambda}\frac{1}{N_{C}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-2}\right)\right]

(955)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda}{1-\lambda^{2}}N_{C}-\frac{2(3+\lambda)}{1+\lambda}+O\left(N_{C}^{-1}\right).

(956)

And only now do we take the average over different angular momentum outcomes. This requires us to use the formula for
\mathbb{E}[N_{C}]
, which we derive up to exponentially small error (abbreviated “e.s.e.” for convenience) in Appendix
J
. The resulting average purity of coherence is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[P(N_{C})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda}{1-\lambda^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}\right]-\frac{2(3+\lambda)}{1+\lambda}+\mathbb{E}\left[O\left(N_{C}^{-1}\right)\right]

(957)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda}{1-\lambda^{2}}\left[\lambda N+\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}+\text{e.s.e.}\right]-\frac{2(3+\lambda)}{1+\lambda}+O\left(N^{-1}\right)

(958)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\lambda^{2}}{1-\lambda^{2}}N-2+O\left(N^{-1}\right).

(959)

Just as before, the first term is the purity of coherence of the input state. Subtracting the above quantity from the initial purity of coherence yields a dissipation of
2+O\left(N^{-1}\right)
, as desired.

∎

As an aside, we find the fact that the leading-order dissipation of purity of coherence has no dependence on
\lambda
or
N
and is just a plain number in both cases (
6
for naive implementation, and
2
for angular momentum post-selection) oddly amusing.

The dissipated purity of coherence dropping from
6+O\left(N^{-1}\right)
to
2+O\left(N^{-1}\right)
shows that there is some advantage, however slight, of keeping track of the angular momentum measurement outcome along with the single purified coherent qubit, rather than naively forgetting that result. In general, for any convex resource such as purity of coherence, the power of post-selection is that it preserves more of the resource. Naively averaging together states from different measurement outcomes wastes the resource. Another useful intuition is that it is better to optimize distillation procedures for each individual measurement outcome, rather than optimizing just one distillation procedure for the average.

But this naturally raises the question: why stop at only one “layer” of post-selection? Right now, we have the protocol (following Schur sampling) written abstractly in terms of Kraus operators. However, if we could find a more physical interpretation of the post-Schur-sampling protocol, where the last step would involve averaging over different outcomes of some additional measurement, then we could take advantage of post-selection on those measurement outcomes too and thereby dissipate even less purity of coherence. It would be especially interesting if there were a way to use this additional post-selection to completely eliminate the dominant term (that is, the
\Theta(1)
term) in the purity of coherence wastage, and thereby produce only
O\left(N^{-1}\right)
wastage.

Appendix M
Entanglement-Breaking Distillation Protocols

One interesting question we might ask is the following: how well can we do if we restrict ourselves to only entanglement-breaking (aka measure-and-prepare) protocols? At the leading order (that is, minimizing the
\Theta\left(N^{-1}\right)
term of the infidelity), this question was already answered by
Marvian 2020
, which describes a TI measure-and-prepare channel
\mathcal{E}_{\text{TI-MP}}
whose infidelity factor is
\delta_{1}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\text{TI-MP}}\right)=\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}
. This is known to be the smallest possible infidelity factor for a single-shot entanglement-breaking distillation protocol, due to a bound that states that the RLD Fisher information of the output for an entanglement-breaking protocol will be upper-bounded by the SLD Fisher information of the input
Marvian 2020
. However, we can analyze this question at higher orders as well. For simplicity, we restrict this discussion to the equatorial case
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
.

M.1
Characterizing Entanglement-Breaking Permutation-Invariant Distillation Protocols

Let us first characterize which single-shot protocols are entanglement-breaking. We will still restrict our attention to permutation-invariant protocols, so that we can focus on the
N_{C}
-qubit symmetric subspace and think about the protocol as something that we do after performing Schur sampling and obtaining an
N_{C}
-qubit fully symmetric state. We start by writing the Choi matrix on the
N_{C}
-qubit symmetric subspace for a TI distillation protocol, which we derived in Appendix
B
B.3
:

J(\mathcal{E})=\begin{array}[]{|cc|cc|cc|cc|cc|cc|}\hline\cr\cos^{2}\theta_{0}&&&A_{1}&&&&&&&&\\
&\sin^{2}\theta_{0}&&&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&\cos^{2}\theta_{1}&&&A_{2}&&&&&&\\
\overline{A}_{1}&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{1}&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&\cos^{2}\theta_{2}&&&\ddots&&&&\\
&&\overline{A}_{2}&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{2}&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&\ddots&&&A_{N_{C}-1}&&\\
&&&&\ddots&&&\ddots&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&\cos^{2}\theta_{N_{C}-1}&&&A_{N_{C}}\\
&&&&&&\overline{A}_{N_{C}-1}&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{N_{C}-1}&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&&&\cos^{2}\theta_{N_{C}}&\\
&&&&&&&&\overline{A}_{N_{C}}&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{N_{C}}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}.

(960)

As a brief aside, it is worth mentioning that this channel does NOT necessarily have the Kraus representation stated in Theorem
15
. The reason is that, because we now want to ensure that the channel is entanglement-breaking, we must sacrifice some of the “obvious” optimizations we made from the above form. In general, as we showed in Appendix
B
B.4
, a TI channel
\mathcal{E}
mapping the
N_{C}
qubits in the symmetric subspace to
1
qubit will necessarily have Kraus operators

K_{(w,\alpha)}=c(w-1,\alpha)\ket{0}\bra{(w-1)^{(s)}}+d(w,\alpha)\ket{1}\bra{w^{(s)}}\quad(0\leq w\leq N_{C}+1)

(961)

for some complex numbers
c(w,\alpha)
and
d(w,\alpha)
, and where we set
\ket{(-1)^{(s)}}=\ket{(N_{C}+1)^{(s)}}=0
by convention. However, the multiplicity index
\alpha
means that this channel does not match the form stated in Theorem
15
.

Let us return to studying the Choi matrix above. This Choi matrix is block-diagonal with
2
blocks of size
1\times 1
and
N_{C}
blocks of size
2\times 2
. From the requirement that the Choi matrix be positive semidefinite (PSD) to represent a completely positive (CP) map, we derive the conditions

\lvert A_{w}\rvert\leq\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w}\quad 1\leq w\leq N_{C}.

(962)

It is known that a Choi matrix represents an entanglement-breaking channel, also known as a super-positive (SP) map, if and only if it corresponds to a separable state (up to normalization). Furthermore, a necessary condition for this to hold is the positive partial transpose (PPT) condition, namely that the partial transpose of the Choi matrix is also PSD. The partial transpose of the above Choi matrix can be computed easily by taking the transpose of each block (this corresponds to the partial transpose on the output qubit):

J(\mathcal{E})^{T_{\text{out}}}=\begin{array}[]{|cc|cc|cc|cc|cc|cc|}\hline\cr\cos^{2}\theta_{0}&&&&&&&&&&&\\
&\sin^{2}\theta_{0}&A_{1}&&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&\overline{A}_{1}&\cos^{2}\theta_{1}&&&&&&&&&\\
&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{1}&A_{2}&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&\overline{A}_{2}&\cos^{2}\theta_{2}&&&\ddots&&&&\\
&&&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{2}&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&\ddots&&&&&\\
&&&&\ddots&&&\ddots&A_{N_{C}-1}&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&\overline{A}_{N_{C}-1}&\cos^{2}\theta_{N_{C}-1}&&&\\
&&&&&&&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{N_{C}-1}&A_{N_{C}}&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&&&\overline{A}_{N_{C}}&\cos^{2}\theta_{N_{C}}&\\
&&&&&&&&&&&\sin^{2}\theta_{N_{C}}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}.

(963)

Amazingly, this matrix is also block-diagonal with
2
blocks of size
1\times 1
and
N_{C}
blocks of size
2\times 2
, but the blocks are a bit different. This matrix is PSD if and only if

\lvert A_{w}\rvert\leq\sin\theta_{w-1}\cos\theta_{w}\quad(1\leq w\leq N_{C}).

(964)

Therefore, for us to have a valid protocol that is also entanglement-breaking, we must have

\lvert A_{w}\rvert\leq\text{min}\{\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w},\sin\theta_{w-1}\cos\theta_{w}\}\quad(1\leq w\leq N_{C}).

(965)

Of course, if we want the best possible performance, we should have
A_{w}
be nonnegative real and saturate this bound:

A_{w}=\text{min}\{\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w},\sin\theta_{w-1}\cos\theta_{w}\}\quad(1\leq w\leq N_{C}).

(966)

So now we can ask: what is the best possible entanglement-breaking channel? Recall that the output purity parameter
\tilde{\lambda}=1-2\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E})
is given by

\tilde{\lambda}=2\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}P_{w-1,w}A_{w}.

(967)

However, we can easily construct the upper bound

\displaystyle A_{w}

\displaystyle=\text{min}\{\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w},\sin\theta_{w-1}\cos\theta_{w}\}

(968)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\left(\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w}\right)\left(\sin\theta_{w-1}\cos\theta_{w}\right)}

(969)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\sin\left(2\theta_{w-1}\right)\sin\left(2\theta_{w}\right)}

(970)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}.

(971)

Clearly this bound is saturated if and only if
\theta_{w}=\frac{\pi}{4}
, such that
\cos^{2}\theta_{w}=\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=\frac{1}{2}
, for all
0\leq w\leq N_{C}
. Hence we immediately obtain the optimal time-translation invariant, permutation-invariant, measure-and-prepare channel:

\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=\frac{1}{2}\quad(0\leq w\leq N_{C}).

(972)

In general, the PPT condition is merely a necessary condition for a channel to be entanglement-breaking, not a sufficient one. However, in Appendix
M
M.2
, we close this gap in this specific case by showing a manifestly separable expression for the above Choi matrix. As a result, the protocol given by
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=\frac{1}{2}
for all
0\leq w\leq N_{C}
is truly the optimal entanglement-breaking protocol in the equatorial case.

Interestingly, if we consider the optimal first-order protocol in the asymptotic regime, which we derived in Appendix
F
, and we take the
\lambda\rightarrow 0
limit of that protocol, we actually get
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=\frac{1-\cos\Theta_{\text{out}}}{2}
for all
0\leq w\leq N_{C}
. In the equatorial case, this corresponds precisely to the protocol we derived above. Intuitively, as
\lambda\rightarrow 0
, the protocol becomes closer to entanglement-breaking, which agrees with the observation made by Marvian that the minimum infidelity factor of an entanglement-breaking protocol
\delta_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{\text{opt},EB})=\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}
gets closer and closer to saturating the bound for a more general protocol
\delta_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{\text{opt}})=\frac{1-\lambda^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}}
in the high-noise limit
\lambda\rightarrow 0

Marvian 2020
.

From the above protocol, we obtain the following output purity parameter:

\tilde{\lambda}=\sum_{w=1}^{N_{C}}P_{w-1,w}.

(973)

This is precisely the “zeroth moment” of the
P_{w-1,w}
values, as computed in Appendix
K
K.4
. Hence this zeroth moment is more than just a mathematically useful quantity that we use to compute the optimal distillation protocol. It has an operational meaning as the optimal purity parameter of a measure-and-prepare distillation protocol. In particular, in Appendix
K
, we state that

\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}=1+\left(-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{-3+4\lambda}{8\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{-9+12\lambda-10\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(974)

Therefore, the minimal infidelity of an entanglement-breaking protocol in the equatorial case is

\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E}_{\text{opt,EB}})=\frac{1-\mathcal{M}_{0}^{(1)}}{2}=\left(\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}}+\left(\frac{3-4\lambda}{16\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}+\left(\frac{9-12\lambda+10\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}+O\left(N_{C}^{-4}\right).

(975)

We now use the expressions for
\mathbb{E}\left[N_{C}^{-p}\right]
for
p=1,2,3
, which we take from Lemma
17
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N}+\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+2\lambda-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(976)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+3\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(977)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{N_{C}^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O\left(N^{-4}\right).

(978)

Using the above formulas, we can convert the aforementioned power series in
N_{C}^{-1}
into a power series in
N^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{E}_{\text{opt,EB}})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)\Bigg\{\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\frac{1}{N}+\left(\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+2\lambda-\lambda^{2})}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}\Bigg\}

(979)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\left(\frac{3-4\lambda}{16\lambda^{2}}\right)\Bigg\{\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left[\frac{(1-\lambda)(1+3\lambda)}{\lambda^{4}}\right]\frac{1}{N^{3}}\Bigg\}

(980)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\left(\frac{9-12\lambda+10\lambda^{2}}{32\lambda^{3}}\right)\Bigg\{\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{3}}\Bigg\}+O\left(N^{-4}\right)

(981)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{N}+\left(\frac{3-4\lambda^{2}}{16\lambda^{4}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{2}}+\left(\frac{15-16\lambda^{2}+8\lambda^{4}}{32\lambda^{6}}\right)\frac{1}{N^{3}}+O(N^{-4}).

(982)

Another interesting question we can ask is the following: suppose we choose the true optimal
A_{w}
, disregarding the entanglement-breaking condition:

A_{w}=\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w}\quad(1\leq w\leq N).

(983)

In that case, what conditions must the
\theta_{w}
values satisfy for the protocol to be entanglement-breaking?

It turns out that the PPT condition provides an amazingly simple necessary condition. In particular, if we set
A_{w}=\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w}
, then we must have

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{w-1}\sin\theta_{w}

\displaystyle\leq\sin\theta_{w-1}\cos\theta_{w}\quad 1\leq w\leq N

(984)

\displaystyle\iff\tan\theta_{w}

\displaystyle\leq\tan\theta_{w-1}\quad 1\leq w\leq N

(985)

\displaystyle\iff\theta_{w-1}

\displaystyle\leq\theta_{w}\quad 1\leq w\leq N.

(986)

This is equivalent to saying that the
\theta_{w}
values are decreasing. Hence we reach the following necessary condition:
if a permutation-invariant TI channel that satisfies all “obvious” optimizations (namely that the
A_{w}
values saturate the bounds needed for the channel to be completely positive) is entanglement-breaking, then the
\theta_{w}
values must be decreasing.
(We suspect that this condition is also sufficient, although we do not yet have a proof.)

For now, we have assumed that the
\theta_{w}
values can be anything in
\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, and we have just maximized the off-diagonal elements
A_{w}
conditioned on the
\theta_{w}
values. However, the last “obvious” optimization (before we are forced to do the complicated boundary value problem stuff) is
\theta_{0}=0
and
\theta_{N}=\frac{\pi}{2}
. But this immediately implies that the
\theta_{w}
values cannot be decreasing for the optimal distillation protocol! We thus achieve the following corollary:
an absolutely optimal single-shot qubit coherence distillation protocol is NEVER entanglement-breaking.

M.2
Explicit Demonstration of Separability of Choi Matrix

In general, it is very hard to discern whether a quantum channel is entanglement-breaking just from the Choi matrix. However, for the protocol we derived in the previous subsection, which is optimal among all those satisfying the PPT condition, we can write out a manifestly separable formula for the Choi matrix. In particular, we will write
J(\mathcal{E})
as a convex combination of pure states that are tensor products of pure states on the
(N_{C}+1)
-dimensional input space and pure states on the
2
-dimensional output space.

For convenience, define the following families of states on the input and output Hilbert spaces, respectively:

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi(\phi)}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{C}+1}}\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}e^{i\phi w}\ket{w^{(s)}}

(987)

\displaystyle\ket{\eta(\phi)}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\ket{0}+e^{i\phi}\ket{1}}{\sqrt{2}}.

(988)

Notice that
\ket{\Psi(\phi)}
is the
forward
time evolution of
\ket{\Psi(0)}
under the Hamiltonian
H=Z_{1}+\cdots+Z_{N_{C}}
, and
\ket{\eta(\phi)}
is similarly the
forward
time evolution of
\ket{\eta(0)}
under the Hamiltonian
H=Z
. However, let us now consider
\ket{\eta(-\phi)}
, which is the
backward
time evolution of
\ket{\eta(0)}
under the Hamiltonian
H=Z
. The tensor product of
\ket{\Psi(\phi)}
and
\ket{\eta(-\phi)}
looks as follows:

\displaystyle\quad\ket{\Psi(\phi)}\bra{\Psi(\phi)}\otimes\ket{\eta(-\phi)}\bra{\eta(-\phi)}

(989)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2(N_{C}+1)}\sum_{w=0}^{N_{C}}\sum_{w^{\prime}=0}^{N_{C}}\sum_{b=0}^{1}\sum_{b^{\prime}=0}^{1}e^{i\phi\left[(w-w^{\prime})-(b-b^{\prime})\right]}\ket{w^{(s)}}\bra{(w^{\prime})^{(s)}}\otimes\ket{b}\bra{b^{\prime}}.

(990)

Now we integrate the above state over the unit circle. Using the fact that
\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\phi n}d\phi=2\pi\delta_{n,0}
, we conclude that

\int_{0}^{2\pi}\ket{\Psi(\phi)}\bra{\Psi(\phi)}\otimes\ket{\eta(-\phi)}\bra{\eta(-\phi)}\,d\phi=\frac{\pi}{N_{C}+1}\sum_{w-w^{\prime}=b-b^{\prime}}\ket{w^{(s)}}\bra{(w^{\prime})^{(s)}}\otimes\ket{b}\bra{b^{\prime}}.

(991)

But notice that this is just a rescaling of our desired Choi matrix! This matrix has
\frac{\pi}{N_{C}+1}
for all entries such that
w-w^{\prime}=b-b^{\prime}
, while
J(\mathcal{E})
has
\frac{1}{2}
for all such entries. Therefore,

J(\mathcal{E})=\frac{N_{C}+1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\ket{\Psi(\phi)}\bra{\Psi(\phi)}\otimes\ket{\eta(-\phi)}\bra{\eta(-\phi)}\,d\phi,

(992)

which is a manifestly separable expression for the Choi matrix, since it is
(N_{C}+1)
times a convex combination of the pure product states
\ket{\Psi(\phi)}\bra{\Psi(\phi)}\otimes\ket{\eta(-\phi)}\bra{\eta(-\phi)}
. We conclude that the distillation channel we derived in the previous subsection is indeed entanglement-breaking.

This manifestly separable expression for the Choi matrix also immediately implies a measure-and-prepare implementation scheme for the optimal entanglement-breaking channel. More generally, a separable Choi matrix (representing an entanglement-breaking channel) can be written in the form

\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{E})=\sum_{j}c_{j}\ket{\Psi_{j}}\bra{\Psi_{j}}\otimes\ket{\eta_{j}}\bra{\eta_{j}},

(993)

where
\ket{\Psi_{j}}
are pure states on the input Hilbert space,
\ket{\eta_{j}}
are pure states on the output Hilbert space, and
c_{j}
are nonnegative real numbers satisfying
\sum_{j}c_{j}=d_{\text{in}}
, where
d_{\text{in}}
is the dimension of the Hilbert space. Furthermore, suppose that

\sum_{j}\frac{c_{j}}{d_{\text{in}}}\ket{\Psi_{j}}\bra{\Psi_{j}}=\mathbb{I}_{d_{\text{in}}}.

(994)

Then such a quantum channel has a Kraus representation of the form

K_{j}=\sqrt{\frac{c_{j}}{d_{\text{in}}}}\ket{\eta_{j}^{*}}\bra{\Psi_{j}},

(995)

where
\ket{\eta_{j}^{*}}
denotes the complex conjugate of
\ket{\eta_{j}}
. Furthermore, this channel can be implemented by applying the POVM

M_{j}=\frac{c_{j}}{d_{\text{in}}}\ket{\Psi_{j}}\bra{\Psi_{j}}

(996)

and then preparing the state
\ket{\eta_{j}^{*}}\bra{\eta_{j}^{*}}
based on the POVM outcome
j
. The optimal entanglement-breaking single-shot qubit coherence distillation protocol shown above has exactly this form (except we have an integral instead of a sum). Therefore, we can implement this protocol by applying the continuous POVM

dM=\frac{N_{C}+1}{2\pi}\ket{\Psi(\phi)}\bra{\Psi(\phi)}\,d\phi

(997)

and then preparing the state
\ket{\eta(\phi)}\bra{\eta(\phi)}
based on the POVM outcome
\phi
.

Appendix N
Optimal Equatorial Protocols in the Low-Noise Limit

One interesting aside we can consider is what the optimal protocol looks like in the regime of
\lambda
values extremely close to
1
(low-noise limit) or
0
(high-noise limit). In particular, instead of treating
N
perturbatively (consider the limit
N\rightarrow\infty
, and expand in powers of
N^{-1}
), we will treat
\lambda
perturbatively. As far as we know, there is no obvious information-theoretic consequence for the behavior of the optimal protocol in these regimes. However, we believe it offers some nice insight into the single-shot qubit coherence distillation problem in its own right. For convenience, we will restrict to the equatorial special case
\Theta_{\text{in}}=\Theta_{\text{out}}=\frac{\pi}{2}
.

In this appendix, we will focus on the low-noise limit
\lambda\rightarrow 1
, since that limit is easier to analyze. Numerically solving the boundary value problem in Appendix
D
reveals that, for
\lambda\approx 1
, the
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
values follow a relatively smooth curve. This naturally motivates the idea of expanding the protocol perturbatively in powers of
c_{0}=\frac{1-\lambda}{2}
. In particular, we will prove the following result:

Theorem 26

(Optimal equatorial distillation protocol in the low-noise limit)
.

In the low-noise limit, the optimal
\lambda\rightarrow 1
equatorial distillation protocol can be expanded as a power series in
c_{0}=\frac{1-\lambda}{2}
, where the zeroth, first, and second orders look as follows:

\displaystyle S_{w}=\sin^{2}\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=f_{0}(w)+f_{1}(w)c_{0}+f_{2}(w)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)

(998)

\displaystyle f_{0}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{w}{N_{C}}

(999)

\displaystyle f_{1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}-2)}w(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-2w)

(1000)

\displaystyle f_{2}(w)

\displaystyle=Aw(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-2w)(w^{2}-N_{C}w+B)

(1001)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\frac{16(3N_{C}-4)}{N_{C}^{3}(N_{C}-1)(N_{C}-2)(N_{C}-4)}

(1002)

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{3N_{C}^{3}-7N_{C}^{2}+16}{4(3N_{C}-4)}.

(1003)

N.1
First-Order Perturbative Protocol for
\lambda\rightarrow 1

To study the first-order perturbative protocol, we should write everything in terms of powers of
c_{0}
and ignore all quantities that are quadratic or higher in
c_{0}
. In particular, we want to write the protocol as

\sin^{2}\theta_{w}=f_{0}(w)+f_{1}(w)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right).

(1004)

From Appendix
D
, we already know that
f_{0}(w)=\frac{w}{N_{C}}
, so we now need to find
f_{1}(w)
.

To accomplish this, we must first write the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values to the first order in
c_{0}
. We begin by approximating the prefactor corresponding to the probability of the
j=N_{C}/2
outcome in the angular momentum measurement when performing Schur sampling:

\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}=\frac{1-2c_{0}}{(1-c_{0})^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}=1+(N_{C}-1)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right).

(1005)

Next, we need to write the
Q_{wk}
values (which are defined in Appendix
K
K.1
) perturbatively as well. Because the formula for
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
has a factor of
c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}
in front of the term involving
Q_{wk}
and
Q_{w^{\prime}k}
, we only need to consider
k=N_{C}
and
k=N_{C}-1
, because all other
Q_{wk}
values will be quadratic or higher in
c_{0}
and can thus be ignored. We thus obtain

\displaystyle Q_{w,N_{C}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}2^{N_{C}}}}\left[\binom{N_{C}}{w}\right]=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{2^{N_{C}}}}

(1006)

\displaystyle Q_{w,N_{C}-1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{N_{C}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}2^{N_{C}}}}\left[\binom{N_{C}}{w}-\binom{N_{C}-1}{w-1}\right]=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{2^{N_{C}}}}\frac{N_{C}-2w}{\sqrt{N_{C}}}.

(1007)

Putting this all together, we get the desired first-order perturbative formula for the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values:

\displaystyle P_{w,w^{\prime}}=\,\,

\displaystyle\left[1+(N_{C}-1)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right]

(1008)

\displaystyle\left[\left(1-N_{C}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right)\frac{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w}\binom{N_{C}}{w^{\prime}}}}{2^{N_{C}}}+\left(c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right)\frac{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w}\binom{N_{C}}{w^{\prime}}}}{2^{N_{C}}}(N_{C}-2w)(N_{C}-2w^{\prime})\right]

(1009)

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w}\binom{N_{C}}{w^{\prime}}}}{2^{N_{C}}}\left[1+c_{0}\left(\frac{(N_{C}-2w)(N_{C}-2w^{\prime})}{N_{C}}-1\right)+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right].

(1010)

Finally, we take the ratios of suitably chosen
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values to find the
R_{w}
values to first order in
c_{0}
:

\displaystyle R_{w}=\frac{P_{w-1,w}}{P_{w,w+1}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N}{w-1}}{\binom{N}{w+1}}}\times\frac{1+c_{0}\left(\frac{(N-2(w-1))(N-2w)}{N}-1\right)+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}{1+c_{0}\left(\frac{(N-2w)(N-2(w+1))}{N}-1\right)+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}

(1011)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w(w+1)}{(N-w)(N-w+1)}}\left[1+\frac{4(N-2w)}{N}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right].

(1012)

As we show in Appendix
D
D.1
, the optimal protocol in the equatorial case is fully determined by the
R_{w}
values, so these are really the only values we need.

We must now expand various trigonometric functions of the
\theta_{w}
values to first order in
c_{0}
. We need to do this because we need to see how the first-order changes in the
R_{w}
values, when plugged into the three-angle relation, shifts the
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
values. Since the three-angle relation involves
\tan\theta_{w}
,
\cos\theta_{w-1}
, and
\sin\theta_{w+1}
, we want to write these quantities in terms of
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
. We do so as follows:

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{w}{N_{C}}+f_{1}(w)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)

(1013)

\displaystyle\therefore\tan\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w+N_{C}f_{1}(w)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}{N_{C}-w-N_{C}f_{1}(w)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}}

(1014)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w}{N_{C}-w}}\left[1+\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w)}{2w}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right]\left[1+\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w)}{2(N_{C}-w)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right]

(1015)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w}{N_{C}-w}}\left[1+\frac{N_{C}^{2}f_{1}(w)}{2w(N_{C}-w)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right]

(1016)

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{w-1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{N_{C}-w+1}{N_{C}}-f_{1}(w-1)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}

(1017)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{N_{C}-w+1}{N_{C}}}\left[1-\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w-1)}{2(N_{C}-w+1)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right]

(1018)

\displaystyle\sin\theta_{w+1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w+1}{N_{C}}+f(w+1)c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}

(1019)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w+1}{N_{C}}}\left[1+\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w+1)}{2(w+1)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right].

(1020)

Plugging these formulas into the three-angle relation yields

\displaystyle\tan\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos\theta_{w-1}}{\sin\theta_{w+1}}R_{w}

(1021)

\displaystyle\iff 1+\frac{N_{C}^{2}f_{1}(w)}{2w(N_{C}-w)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w-1)}{2(N_{C}-w+1)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}{1+\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w+1)}{2(w+1)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)}\left[1+\frac{4(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right)\right]

(1022)

\displaystyle\iff\frac{N_{C}^{2}f_{1}(w)}{2w(N_{C}-w)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w-1)}{2(N_{C}-w+1)}-\frac{N_{C}f_{1}(w+1)}{2(w+1)}+\frac{4(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}.

(1023)

In other words, we have turned our three-angle relation for
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
into a three-value relation for
f_{1}(w)
. Furthermore, the boundary conditions and the bit-flip symmetry condition for the equatorial special case also immediately translate as follows:

•

boundary conditions:
\theta_{0}=0,\,\theta_{N_{C}}=\frac{\pi}{2}\implies f_{1}(0)=f_{1}(N)=0

•

bit-flip symmetry:
\theta_{w}+\theta_{N_{C}-w}=\frac{\pi}{2}\implies f_{1}(N_{C}-w)=-f_{1}(w)
.

Turning one boundary value problem into another may not feel like progress. But the boundary value problem for
f_{1}(w)
is a lot easier than the one for
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
, because its solution is much more guessable. One way to guess the solution, inspired by the numerics, is to guess that
f_{1}(w)
is scale-invariant, in the sense that it only depends on
w
as a fraction of
N_{C}
. Doing this dramatically simplifies the three-value relation above:

\displaystyle f_{1}(w)

\displaystyle\approx h\left(\frac{w}{N}\right)

(1024)

\displaystyle\implies\frac{h(x)}{2x(1-x)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{h(x)}{2(1-x)}-\frac{h(x)}{2x}+4(1-2x)

(1025)

\displaystyle\implies h(x)

\displaystyle=4x(1-x)(1-2x).

(1026)

If you try to plug in this result for
f_{1}(w)
, it will not work. However, it is also natural to try this functional form, but with an adjustment to the leading factor that can depend on
N_{C}
. Such an ansatz looks as follows:

\displaystyle f(w)=c(N_{C})w(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-2w)

(1027)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle\frac{c(N_{C})N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}-2w)}{2}=-\frac{c(N_{C})N_{C}(w-1)(N_{C}-2w+2)}{2}

(1028)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\frac{c(N_{C})N_{C}(N_{C}-w-1)(N_{C}-2w-2)}{2}+\frac{4(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}

(1029)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle c(N_{C})N_{C}\left[N_{C}(N_{C}-2w)+(w-1)(N_{C}-2w+2)+(N_{C}-w-1)(N_{C}-2w-2)\right]=\frac{8(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}

(1030)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle 2N_{C}(N_{C}-2)(N_{C}-2w)c(N_{C})=\frac{8(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}

(1031)

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle c(N_{C})=\frac{4}{N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}-2)}.

(1032)

Sure enough, adjusting the leading factor allowed us to find a solution to the boundary value problem! We conclude that the first-order perturbation to the optimal protocol in the low-noise limit looks as follows:

\displaystyle f_{1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{4w(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}-2)}

(1033)

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{w}{N_{C}}+\frac{4w(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}-2)}c_{0}+O\left(c_{0}^{2}\right).

(1034)

It is worthwhile to make a few nice observations regarding
f_{1}(w)
:

•

First, it is indeed a cubic function in
w
, which one may have suspected from the numerics.

•

Second, this cubic has roots
w=0,\frac{N_{C}}{2},N_{C}
, which are natural consequences of the boundary conditions and bit-flip symmetry.

•

Third, although it is not exactly scale-invariant (in the sense of depending only on
\frac{w}{N_{C}}
), it approaches scale invariance in the
N_{C}\rightarrow\infty
limit, which one may also have suspected from the numerics.

•

Fourth, it is well-defined for all
N_{C}
except for
N_{C}=0
and
N_{C}=2
. This is fine because the cases with
N_{C}\leq 2
do not allow for perturbations anyway, in the sense that the optimal protocol is the same regardless of
\lambda
. For all interesting cases (
N_{C}\geq 3
), this perturbation is perfectly well-behaved.

N.2
Second-Order Perturbative Protocol for
\lambda\rightarrow 1

It turns out that computing the second-order perturbative protocol in the
\lambda\rightarrow 1
limit, while more tedious than computing the first-order perturbative protocol, is indeed doable. Furthermore, it has a fairly satisfying form. The zeroth-order protocol (expressed using
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
) is linear in
w
, while the first-order perturbative protocol is cubic in
w
. This loosely suggests that the second-order protocol is quintic (i.e., a degree-
5
polynomial) in
w
. This idea turns out to be precisely correct, as we will now show.

We will follow much the same workflow that we used to compute the first-order perturbative protocol in the
\lambda\rightarrow 1
limit. We begin by approximating the prefactor corresponding to the probability of the
j=N_{C}/2
outcome in the angular momentum measurement when performing Schur sampling:

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}-c_{0}}{c_{1}^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-2c_{0}}{(1-c_{0})^{N_{C}+1}-c_{0}^{N_{C}+1}}

(1035)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-2c_{0}}{1-(N_{C}+1)c_{0}+\binom{N_{C}+1}{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)}

(1036)

\displaystyle=\left(1-2c_{0}\right)\left(1+(N_{C}+1)c_{0}+\binom{N_{C}+2}{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right)

(1037)

\displaystyle=1+(N_{C}-1)c_{0}+\frac{(N_{C}+1)(N_{C}-2)}{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right).

(1038)

Next, we need to compute how
c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}
is written up to second order in
c_{0}
. Since we discard all terms that are cubic or higher in
c_{0}
, we only need to consider
k=N_{C}
,
k=N_{C}-1
, and
k=N_{C}-2
:

\displaystyle k=N_{C}\rightarrow c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}

\displaystyle=\left(1-c_{0}\right)^{N}_{C}=1-N_{C}c_{0}+\frac{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)

(1039)

\displaystyle k=N_{C}-1\rightarrow c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}

\displaystyle=\left(1-c_{0}\right)^{N_{C}-1}c_{0}=c_{0}-(N_{C}-1)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)

(1040)

\displaystyle k=N_{C}-2\rightarrow c_{1}^{k}c_{0}^{N_{C}-k}

\displaystyle=\left(1-c_{0}\right)^{N_{C}-2}c_{0}^{2}=c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right).

(1041)

In Appendix
N
N.1
, we already computed
Q_{wk}
for
k=N_{C}
and
k=N_{C}-1
:

\displaystyle Q_{w,N_{C}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{2^{N_{C}}}}

(1042)

\displaystyle Q_{w,N_{C}-1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{N_{C}\cdot 2^{N_{C}}}}(N_{C}-2w).

(1043)

Now that we are studying the second-order perturbation, we need to introduce
k=N_{C}-2
as well:

\displaystyle Q_{w,N_{C}-2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{2}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}2^{N_{C}}}}\left[\binom{2}{0}\binom{N_{C}-2}{w}-\binom{2}{1}\binom{N_{C}-2}{w-1}+\binom{2}{2}\binom{N_{C}-2}{w-2}\right]

(1044)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{2}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}2^{N_{C}}}}\binom{N_{C}-2}{w}\left[1-2\cdot\frac{w}{N_{C}-w-1}+\frac{w(w-1)}{(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-w-1)}\right]

(1045)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{2}}{\binom{N_{C}}{w}2^{N_{C}}}}\binom{N_{C}}{w}\frac{(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-w-1)}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}\left[1-\frac{2w}{N_{C}-w-1}+\frac{w(w-1)}{(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-w-1)}\right]

(1046)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{2N_{C}(N_{C}-1)2^{N_{C}}}}\left[(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-w-1)-2w(N_{C}-w)+w(w-1)\right]

(1047)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\binom{N_{C}}{w}}{2N_{C}(N_{C}-1)2^{N_{C}}}}\left[(N_{C}-2w)^{2}-N_{C}\right].

(1048)

We can now put everything together and compute
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
to second order in
c_{0}
:

\displaystyle P_{w,w^{\prime}}=\,\,

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w}\binom{N_{C}}{w^{\prime}}}}{2^{N_{C}}}\left[1+(N_{C}-1)c_{0}+\frac{(N_{C}+1)(N_{C}-2)}{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1049)

\displaystyle\Bigg\{\left[1-N_{C}c_{0}+\frac{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1050)

\displaystyle+\left[c_{0}-(N_{C}-1)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]\frac{1}{N_{C}}(N_{C}-2w)(N_{C}-2w^{\prime})

(1051)

\displaystyle+\left[c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]\frac{1}{2N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}[(N_{C}-2w)^{2}-N_{C}][(N_{C}-2w^{\prime})^{2}-N_{C}]\Bigg\}.

(1052)

The above formula simplifies as follows:

\displaystyle P_{w,w^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\binom{N_{C}}{w}\binom{N_{C}}{w^{\prime}}}}{2^{N_{C}}}\Bigg\{1+\left[\frac{(N_{C}-2w)(N_{C}-2w^{\prime})}{N_{C}}-1\right]c_{0}+(\cdots)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\Bigg\}

(1053)

\displaystyle\left[c_{0}^{2}\text{ coeff}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{[(N_{C}-2w)^{2}-N_{C}][(N_{C}-2w^{\prime})^{2}-N_{C}]}{2N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}-1,

(1054)

where we write the
c_{0}^{2}
coefficient on a separate line to make it easier to read.

Finally, we need to take the ratios of suitable
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values to compute the
R_{w}
values. For two general quantities written as second-order perturbation series in
c_{0}
, their quotient looks as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{1+\alpha_{1}c_{0}+\alpha_{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)}{1+\beta_{1}c_{0}+\beta_{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)}

\displaystyle=\left[1+\alpha_{1}c_{0}+\alpha_{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]\left[1-\beta_{1}c_{0}+\left(\beta_{1}^{2}-\beta_{2}\right)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1055)

\displaystyle=1+\left(\alpha_{1}-\beta_{1}\right)c_{0}+\left[\left(\alpha_{2}-\beta_{2}\right)-\beta_{1}\left(\alpha_{1}-\beta_{1}\right)\right]c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right).

(1056)

After some tedious but ultimately straightforward calculation, the quotient formula and the formula for the
P_{w,w^{\prime}}
values yield

\displaystyle R_{w}=\frac{P_{w-1,w}}{P_{w,w+1}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{w(w+1)}{(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-w+1)}}\left[1+\frac{4(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}c_{0}+(\cdots)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1057)

\displaystyle\left[c_{0}^{2}\text{ coeff}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{4(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}\left[\frac{(N_{C}-2w)^{2}}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}+\frac{2(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}-\frac{1}{N_{C}-1}\right],

(1058)

where we once again separate out the
c_{0}^{2}
coefficient to make it easier to read. Just as in the first-order perturbation analysis, the
R_{w}
values are the only ones we need to focus on, since they fully determine the optimal protocol.

We now need to examine the perturbation in the optimal protocol itself. First, we write
\sin^{2}\theta_{w}
as a second-order perturbation series in
c_{0}
, where we derived the zeroth-order contribution in Appendix
D
, and we derived the first-order contribution in Appendix
N
N.1
:

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=f_{0}(w)+f_{1}(w)c_{0}+f_{2}(w)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)

(1059)

\displaystyle f_{0}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{w}{N_{C}}

(1060)

\displaystyle f_{1}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}-2)}w(N_{C}-w)(N_{C}-2w).

(1061)

Our objective is to solve for
f_{2}(w)
. To do so, we must expand various trigonometric functions of the
\theta_{w}
values to second order in
c_{0}
. We use the following Taylor series:

\displaystyle\left(1+\epsilon\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\epsilon}{2}-\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{8}+O\left(\epsilon^{3}\right)

(1062)

\displaystyle\left(1-\epsilon\right)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\frac{3}{8}\epsilon^{2}+O\left(\epsilon^{3}\right).

(1063)

Here, we have to expand these Taylor series out to the
\epsilon^{2}
term, whereas in Appendix
N
N.1
, we only expanded them out to the
\epsilon
term. These Taylor series allow us to eventually compute all of the trigonometric functions we need:

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=f_{0}(w)\left[1+\frac{f_{1}(w)}{f_{0}(w)}c_{0}+\frac{f_{2}(w)}{f_{0}(w)}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1064)

\displaystyle\sin\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{f_{0}(w)}\left[1+\frac{f_{1}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)}c_{0}+\left(\frac{f_{2}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)}-\frac{f_{1}(w)^{2}}{8f_{0}(w)^{2}}\right)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1065)

\displaystyle\csc\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{f_{0}(w)}}\left[1-\frac{f_{1}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)}c_{0}+\left(\frac{3f_{1}(w)^{2}}{8f_{0}(w)^{2}}-\frac{f_{2}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)}\right)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1066)

\displaystyle\cos^{2}\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)\left[1-\frac{f_{1}(w)}{1-f_{0}(w)}c_{0}-\frac{f_{2}(w)}{1-f_{0}(w)}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1067)

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-f_{0}(w)}\left[1-\frac{f_{1}(w)}{2\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)}c_{0}-\left(\frac{f_{2}(w)}{2\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)}+\frac{f_{1}(w)^{2}}{8\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)^{2}}\right)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1068)

\displaystyle\tan\theta_{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin\theta_{w}}{\cos\theta_{w}}

(1069)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{f_{0}(w)}{1-f_{0}(w)}}\left[1+\frac{f_{1}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)}c_{0}+(\text{stuff})c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1070)

\displaystyle(\text{stuff})

\displaystyle=-\beta_{1}\left(\alpha_{1}-\beta_{1}\right)+\left(\alpha_{2}-\beta_{2}\right)

(1071)

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{1}(w)}{2\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)}\cdot\frac{f_{1}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)}+\frac{f_{2}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)}+\frac{f_{1}(w)^{2}\left(2f_{0}(w)-1\right)}{8f_{0}(w)^{2}\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)^{2}}

(1072)

\displaystyle=\frac{f_{2}(w)}{2f_{0}(w)\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)}+\frac{f_{1}(w)^{2}\left(4f_{0}(w)-1\right)}{8f_{0}(w)^{2}\left(1-f_{0}(w)\right)^{2}},

(1073)

where in the above calculation of
\tan\theta_{w}
, we once again used the formula for the quotient of two second-order perturbation series.

Now we need to plug all of these results into our three-angle recurrence relation

\tan\theta_{w}=\frac{\cos\theta_{w-1}}{\sin\theta_{w+1}}R_{w}=\cos\theta_{w-1}\csc\theta_{w+1}R_{w}.

(1074)

To compute the right side, we need to multiply three different second-order perturbation series: one for
\cos\theta_{w-1}
, one for
\csc\theta_{w+1}
, and one for
R_{w}
. In the most general case, this is done as follows:

\displaystyle\left[1+\alpha_{1}c_{0}+\alpha_{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]\left[1+\beta_{1}c_{0}+\beta_{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]\left[1+\gamma_{1}c_{0}+\gamma_{2}c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right)\right]

(1075)

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle 1+\left(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}+\gamma_{1}\right)c_{0}+\left(\alpha_{2}+\beta_{2}+\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1}\gamma_{1}+\beta_{1}\gamma_{1}\right)c_{0}^{2}+O\left(c_{0}^{3}\right).

(1076)

This will also be the time to actually plug in the known values for
f_{0}(w)
and
f_{1}(w)
. When we do so, the zeroth-order and first-order terms on both sides of the three-angle recurrence relation will match, since we solved for the
f_{0}(w)
and
f_{1}(w)
that would make them match. When we compare the second-order terms on both sides, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{N_{C}^{2}}{2w(N_{C}-w)}f_{2}(w)+\frac{N_{C}}{2(N_{C}-w+1)}f_{2}(w-1)+\frac{N_{C}}{2(w+1)}f_{2}(w+1)

(1077)

\displaystyle=\,\,

\displaystyle\frac{2(N_{C}-2w)^{2}(N_{C}-4w)}{N_{C}(N_{C}-2)^{2}}+\frac{4(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}}\left[\frac{(N_{C}-2w)^{2}-N_{C}}{N_{C}(N_{C}-1)}+\frac{4(N_{C}-2w)}{N_{C}(N_{C}-2)}\right]

(1078)

\displaystyle+\frac{2(N_{C}-4)(N_{C}-2w)[3(N_{C}-w-1)(N_{C}-2w-2)-(w-1)(N_{C}-2w+2)]}{N_{C}^{2}(N_{C}-2)^{2}}.

(1079)
